@blurb Design types that manage their own resources and behave like built-ins: RAII, copy and move, smart pointers, templates, operators, interfaces, variants, compile-time code and error handling without exceptions.
@plainvoice true

=== cpp3-01 | RAII: let destructors clean up
--- teach
Last lesson, "Design: give repeated work a name", ended the intermediate course by giving repeated work one home. This course goes one level deeper. In the basics and intermediate courses you *used* types that look after themselves: `std::vector`, `std::string`, `std::map`, `std::unique_ptr`. Now you will *write* them: types that own things, copy and move correctly, work for any element type and behave like built-ins. It starts with the idea underneath all of them.

### Borrow it, give it back

Picture a library book. You borrow it, and you must give it back exactly once: not zero times, not twice. And you must give it back however your week goes: whether you finish it, get bored halfway, or leave on a trip early.

A program borrows things too. Memory, an open file, a lock, a network connection: each one is a **[[resource|resource-word]]**, something the program takes and must give back exactly once. It has to give it back on **every** way out of the code: the normal end, an early `return`, a `break` out of a loop.

Doing that by hand is where leaks come from:

```cpp
bool process(const std::string& name) {
    open_file(name);
    if (name.empty()) return false;   // oops: never closed
    // ...
    close_file(name);
    return true;
}
```

The early `return` leaves the function before it reaches `close_file`. One forgotten path, and a file stays open.

### The destructor: code that runs at the closing brace

You know constructors: the member function that runs when an object is born. A class can also have a **destructor**: a member function that runs automatically when the object dies. Its name is the class name with a `~` in front, read "tilde", and it has no parameters and no return type:

```cpp
struct Say {
    std::string word;
    explicit Say(const std::string& w) : word(w) { std::cout << "hello " << word << "\n"; }
    ~Say() { std::cout << "bye " << word << "\n"; }
};
```

A local object dies when it goes out of scope: when the program reaches the closing `}` of the block where the object was made. You never call the destructor yourself. C++ calls it for you, whichever way the program leaves the block: by reaching the end, by `return`, or by `break`.

### RAII: tie the resource to an object

Put those two ideas together and you get C++'s answer to leaks, **[[RAII|raii-name]]**, short for *Resource Acquisition Is Initialization*. It means: tie each resource to an object. The constructor takes the resource (it **acquires** it), and the destructor gives it back (it **releases** it). Now the cleanup cannot be forgotten, because it is no longer a line you have to remember on every path.

Here is a lock. A **[[mutex|mutex-word]]** is a lock that lets only one part of a program use something at a time. Imagine a type `Mutex` with two member functions, `lock()` and `unlock()`:

```cpp
class Lock {
public:
    explicit Lock(Mutex& m) : m_(m) { m_.lock(); }
    ~Lock() { m_.unlock(); }
private:
    Mutex& m_;
};
```

Go through it one piece at a time:

- The member `m_` is a reference, `Mutex&`. The `Lock` does not hold a mutex of its own; it refers to the caller's. A reference member must get its value in the member initializer list, `: m_(m)`, because a reference can never be changed later to refer to something else.
- The constructor body locks. The destructor, `~Lock()`, unlocks.

Now use it:

```cpp
void work(Mutex& m) {
    Lock guard(m);        // locked here
    if (bad()) return;    // unlocked here
}                         // ... and here
```

There is no `unlock` anywhere in `work`, yet every path unlocks. The object `guard` dies at the early `return` and at the closing `}`, and its destructor runs either way.

### Last made, first destroyed

When several objects live in one block, their destructors run in **reverse order** of construction: the last one made is the first one destroyed. So nested guards [[unwind like a stack|reverse-order]] of plates. With the `Say` type from above:

```cpp fragment
int main() {
    Say a("a");
    Say b("b");
}
```

This prints:

```
hello a
hello b
bye b
bye a
```

### Anything that must be undone

A resource does not have to be a file or a lock. Anything that must be undone is a job for RAII. Say a program switches a setting to a temporary value while it does one careful job, and the old value must come back afterwards. An object can remember the old value in its constructor, switch the setting, and put the old value back in its destructor. However the job ends, the setting is restored.

### An owner must not be copied by accident

If a `Lock` could be copied, there would be two objects that both think they own the same lock, and both destructors would unlock it: one unlock too many. A class that owns something usually must not be copied by accident.

Copying goes through two special member functions, which C++ writes for you unless you say otherwise:

- the **copy constructor**, `Lock(const Lock&)`: builds a new `Lock` from an existing one, as in `Lock b = a;`.
- the **copy assignment operator**, `Lock& operator=(const Lock&)`: runs when you write `b = a;` and `b` already exists. `operator=`, read "operator equals", is the name C++ gives the function behind the `=` sign.

Say "no copying" by [[deleting both of them|deleted-functions]]:

```cpp
Lock(const Lock&) = delete;
Lock& operator=(const Lock&) = delete;
```

`= delete`, read "is deleted", tells the compiler this function must not exist. Any code that tries to copy a `Lock` now stops with a compile error that mentions a deleted function. The next lesson writes these two functions properly, for a class that should be copyable.

**Watch out:** the real mistake is doing half the job. If the destructor releases but copying is still allowed, a copy made by accident releases too, and a resource taken once is given back twice. And if you acquire in the constructor but still release by hand somewhere else, you are back to remembering every path. Acquire in the constructor, release in the destructor, delete copying, and nothing else.

You have used RAII types all along: `std::vector` frees its memory, `std::unique_ptr` deletes its object, and a file stream closes its file, each in its destructor.

::: context resource-word What counts as a resource
A resource is anything a program gets from the computer and must hand back, because there is only a limited supply. Memory is the famous one, but files are limited too: on many Linux systems a program may have only 1024 files open at once, and in the Terminal course's bash you can see your limit with `ulimit -n`. A program that forgets to close one file per request works fine in a quick test, then fails after running for an hour. Locks, network connections and hardware devices are resources in the same way.
:::

::: context raii-name A clumsy name for a great idea
Bjarne Stroustrup, who created C++, came up with the name RAII. Even people who love the idea admit the name is awkward: it talks about acquiring, but the important half is the destructor that releases. Some people call it "scope-bound resource management" instead, which describes what it does: the resource lives exactly as long as the scope of the object that owns it. Whatever the name, it is the reason well-written C++ rarely has cleanup code scattered around.
:::

::: context mutex-word Locks and a lander on Mars
"Mutex" is short for *mutual exclusion*: while one part of a program holds the lock, every other part that wants it has to wait. The real C++ types are `std::mutex` and `std::lock_guard`, which is exactly the `Lock` class in this lesson, written for you. Locks matter in flight software. In 1997 the Mars Pathfinder lander kept resetting itself on Mars, because a low-priority task held a mutex that a high-priority task needed. Engineers found the cause on a copy of the system on the ground and sent up a fix that changed how the mutex behaved.
:::

::: context reverse-order Why the last one goes first
Objects made later may depend on objects made earlier: a guard made second might use something the first one set up. Destroying in reverse order means every object is torn down while everything it relies on still exists. It works like a stack of plates: the last plate put on is the first one taken off.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">built: a, b, c</text>
  <text x="270" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">destroyed: c, b, a</text>
  <rect x="50" y="100" width="80" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="118" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">a (1st)</text>
  <rect x="50" y="72" width="80" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="90" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">b (2nd)</text>
  <rect x="50" y="44" width="80" height="26" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="62" font-size="12" fill="#ffffff" text-anchor="middle">c (3rd)</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="85" x2="200" y2="85" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <path d="M 208 85 L 198 80 L 198 90 Z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="270" y="50" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">1. ~c()</text>
  <text x="270" y="78" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">2. ~b()</text>
  <text x="270" y="106" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">3. ~a()</text>
  <text x="180" y="144" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">at the closing } the top of the stack goes first</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context deleted-functions Saying no out loud
`= delete` arrived in C++11. Before that, programmers stopped copying by declaring the copy functions `private` and never writing them, which gave confusing errors. Now the compiler says it plainly. With g++ the message reads "use of deleted function"; with clang, which this app uses, it reads "call to deleted constructor". Either way the mistake is caught before the program ever runs, which is always the cheapest place to catch it. `std::unique_ptr` deletes its copy functions in exactly this way.
:::
--- task
The starter has a **global** variable `open_handles`, made outside every function so that every function can see it. It counts the files open right now. The function `open_file` adds 1 to it, and `close_file` takes 1 away.

1. Make `FileHandle` an RAII type: its constructor calls `open_file(name)`, its destructor `~FileHandle()` calls `close_file(name)`, `name()` still returns the name, and it cannot be copied (delete its copy constructor and its copy assignment operator).
2. Write `class ValueGuard` with a constructor `ValueGuard(int& target, int temporary)` that saves `target`'s current value and then sets `target` to `temporary`, and a destructor that puts the saved value back. It cannot be copied either.

No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>

int open_handles = 0;   // how many files are open right now
void open_file(const std::string&) { ++open_handles; }
void close_file(const std::string&) { --open_handles; }

class FileHandle {
public:
    explicit FileHandle(const std::string& name) : name_(name) {}
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>

int open_handles = 0;   // how many files are open right now
void open_file(const std::string&) { ++open_handles; }
void close_file(const std::string&) { --open_handles; }

class FileHandle {
public:
    explicit FileHandle(const std::string& name) : name_(name) { open_file(name_); }
    ~FileHandle() { close_file(name_); }
    FileHandle(const FileHandle&) = delete;
    FileHandle& operator=(const FileHandle&) = delete;
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class ValueGuard {
public:
    ValueGuard(int& target, int temporary) : target_(target), saved_(target) { target_ = temporary; }
    ~ValueGuard() { target_ = saved_; }
    ValueGuard(const ValueGuard&) = delete;
    ValueGuard& operator=(const ValueGuard&) = delete;
private:
    int& target_;
    int saved_;
};
--- hint
Start with `FileHandle`. The constructor body is the place to acquire: `open_file(name_);`. The destructor, `~FileHandle()`, is the place to release: `close_file(name_);`.
--- hint
`ValueGuard` needs two private members: a reference `int& target_` to the caller's variable, and an `int saved_` holding its old value. Fill both in the initializer list, `: target_(target), saved_(target)`, then set `target_ = temporary;` in the constructor body.
--- hint
The destructor is `~ValueGuard() { target_ = saved_; }`. Delete copying in both classes with `ClassName(const ClassName&) = delete;` and `ClassName& operator=(const ClassName&) = delete;`, writing the real class name each time.
--- check test | A handle is open while it lives, closed after
[] { bool during; { FileHandle f("log.txt"); during = open_handles == 1 && f.name() == "log.txt"; } return during && open_handles == 0; }()
--- check test | An early return still closes every handle
[] { auto work = [] { FileHandle a("a"); FileHandle b("b"); if (open_handles == 2) return true; return false; }; bool ok = work(); return ok && open_handles == 0; }()
--- check test | FileHandle cannot be copied
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<FileHandle> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<FileHandle>
--- check test | ValueGuard sets and restores
[] { int level = 1; bool inside; { ValueGuard g(level, 5); inside = level == 5; } return inside && level == 1; }()
--- check test | Nested guards unwind in reverse order
[] { int x = 0; bool ok = true; { ValueGuard a(x, 1); { ValueGuard b(x, 2); ok = ok && x == 2; } ok = ok && x == 1; } return ok && x == 0; }()

+++ practice | A trace that logs every way out
--- task
The starter has a **global** `std::vector<std::string> trace_log`. Finish `class Trace` so that it writes its own entries into that log:

- `explicit Trace(const std::string& name)` adds `"enter <name>"` to the end of `trace_log`.
- The destructor `~Trace()` adds `"exit <name>"`.
- A `Trace` cannot be copied: delete its copy constructor and its copy assignment operator.

So a block that makes `Trace a("a");` and then `Trace b("b");` leaves the log as `"enter a"`, `"enter b"`, `"exit b"`, `"exit a"`. An early `return` must still write the `exit` line. No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> trace_log;

class Trace {
public:
    explicit Trace(const std::string& name) : name_(name) {}
private:
    std::string name_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> trace_log;

class Trace {
public:
    explicit Trace(const std::string& name) : name_(name) { trace_log.push_back("enter " + name_); }
    ~Trace() { trace_log.push_back("exit " + name_); }
    Trace(const Trace&) = delete;
    Trace& operator=(const Trace&) = delete;
private:
    std::string name_;
};
--- hint
The constructor body is where the "enter" line goes, and the destructor body is where the "exit" line goes. Build each line with `+`, as in `"enter " + name_`.
--- hint
A destructor is written `~Trace() { … }`: no parameters and no return type. Delete copying with `Trace(const Trace&) = delete;` and `Trace& operator=(const Trace&) = delete;`.
--- check test | One trace: enter, then exit at the closing brace
[] { trace_log.clear(); { Trace t("boot"); } return trace_log == std::vector<std::string>{"enter boot", "exit boot"}; }()
--- check test | Two traces unwind in reverse order
[] { trace_log.clear(); { Trace a("a"); Trace b("b"); } return trace_log == std::vector<std::string>{"enter a", "enter b", "exit b", "exit a"}; }()
--- check test | An early return still writes exit
[] { trace_log.clear(); auto f = [](bool stop) { Trace t("f"); if (stop) return 1; Trace u("g"); return 2; }; int r = f(true); return r == 1 && trace_log == std::vector<std::string>{"enter f", "exit f"}; }()
--- check test | Trace cannot be copied
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<Trace> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<Trace>

+++ practice | A guard that pushes and pops
--- task
Write `class PushGuard`. It puts a number on the end of a caller's vector for as long as the guard lives:

- `PushGuard(std::vector<int>& stack, int value)` adds `value` to the end of `stack`.
- The destructor removes the last element of `stack` again.
- A `PushGuard` cannot be copied.

If `path` is `{1}`, then inside a block with `PushGuard g(path, 7);` it is `{1, 7}`, and after the block it is `{1}` again. The starter pushes, but never pops. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

class PushGuard {
public:
    PushGuard(std::vector<int>& stack, int value) : stack_(stack) { stack_.push_back(value); }
private:
    std::vector<int>& stack_;
};
--- solution
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

class PushGuard {
public:
    PushGuard(std::vector<int>& stack, int value) : stack_(stack) { stack_.push_back(value); }
    ~PushGuard() { stack_.pop_back(); }
    PushGuard(const PushGuard&) = delete;
    PushGuard& operator=(const PushGuard&) = delete;
private:
    std::vector<int>& stack_;
};
--- hint
The starter does half the job: it acquires in the constructor. Releasing belongs in a destructor.
--- hint
The destructor is `~PushGuard() { stack_.pop_back(); }`. Then delete the copy constructor and the copy assignment operator, as in the lesson.
--- check test | The value is there inside, gone after
[] { std::vector<int> path{1}; bool inside; { PushGuard g(path, 7); inside = path == std::vector<int>{1, 7}; } return inside && path == std::vector<int>{1}; }()
--- check test | Nested guards on an empty vector
[] { std::vector<int> s; bool ok = true; { PushGuard a(s, 1); { PushGuard b(s, 2); ok = ok && s == std::vector<int>{1, 2}; } ok = ok && s == std::vector<int>{1}; } return ok && s.empty(); }()
--- check test | A guard in a loop pops every time round
[] { std::vector<int> s{9}; int seen = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 3; ++i) { PushGuard g(s, i); if (s.size() == 2 && s.back() == i) ++seen; } return seen == 3 && s == std::vector<int>{9}; }()
--- check test | PushGuard cannot be copied
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<PushGuard> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<PushGuard>

+++ practice | Marking a name as busy in a map
--- task
A program keeps a `std::map<std::string, int>` that says how many parts of the program are using each device right now. Write `class InUse`, an RAII guard for one use of one device:

- `InUse(std::map<std::string, int>& table, const std::string& name)` adds 1 to `table[name]`.
- The destructor takes 1 away again, and when the count for `name` reaches 0 it removes `name` from the map, so only devices in use are listed. `table.erase(name)` removes the key `name` from a map.
- An `InUse` cannot be copied.

Two guards for `"radio"` make `table["radio"]` equal to 2. When both have died, `"radio"` is no longer a key at all. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>

class InUse {
public:
    InUse(std::map<std::string, int>& table, const std::string& name) : table_(table), name_(name) {}
private:
    std::map<std::string, int>& table_;
    std::string name_;
};
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>

class InUse {
public:
    InUse(std::map<std::string, int>& table, const std::string& name) : table_(table), name_(name) { ++table_[name_]; }
    ~InUse() {
        if (--table_[name_] == 0) table_.erase(name_);
    }
    InUse(const InUse&) = delete;
    InUse& operator=(const InUse&) = delete;
private:
    std::map<std::string, int>& table_;
    std::string name_;
};
--- hint
Acquire in the constructor body: `++table_[name_];`. Release in the destructor.
--- hint
In the destructor, take 1 away first, then check whether the count is 0. If it is, `table_.erase(name_);` removes the key.
--- check test | Two uses of one device count 2
[] { std::map<std::string, int> t; InUse a(t, "radio"); InUse b(t, "radio"); return t.size() == 1 && t["radio"] == 2; }()
--- check test | The key is gone when the last use ends
[] { std::map<std::string, int> t; bool mid; { InUse a(t, "radio"); { InUse b(t, "radio"); } mid = t.count("radio") == 1 && t.at("radio") == 1; } return mid && t.count("radio") == 0 && t.empty(); }()
--- check test | Different devices are counted apart, early return included
[] { std::map<std::string, int> t; auto use = [&t](bool stop) { InUse a(t, "cam"); InUse b(t, "heater"); if (stop) return t.size(); return std::size_t(0); }; std::size_t during = use(true); return during == 2 && t.empty(); }()
--- check test | InUse cannot be copied
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<InUse> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<InUse>

+++ practice | Roll back unless committed
--- task
Write `class Transaction`, which undoes changes to an `int` unless you say they are final. Then use it. No `main`.

- `explicit Transaction(int& value)` remembers the current value of `value`.
- `void commit()` marks the changes as final.
- The destructor puts the remembered value back, **unless** `commit()` was called. A `Transaction` cannot be copied.
- `bool transfer(int& from, int& to, int amount)` moves `amount` from `from` to `to`, using one `Transaction` for each of the two numbers. It returns `true` when it worked. If `amount` is 0 or less, or `from` would go below 0, it returns `false` and both numbers must end up exactly as they were.

With `from` 50 and `to` 5, `transfer(from, to, 20)` returns `true` and leaves 30 and 25. `transfer(from, to, 80)` returns `false` and leaves 50 and 5.
--- starter
#include <type_traits>

class Transaction {
public:
    explicit Transaction(int& value) : value_(value), saved_(value) {}
    void commit() {}
private:
    int& value_;
    int saved_;
};

bool transfer(int& from, int& to, int amount) {
    from -= amount;
    to += amount;
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <type_traits>

class Transaction {
public:
    explicit Transaction(int& value) : value_(value), saved_(value) {}
    ~Transaction() {
        if (!committed_) value_ = saved_;
    }
    Transaction(const Transaction&) = delete;
    Transaction& operator=(const Transaction&) = delete;
    void commit() { committed_ = true; }
private:
    int& value_;
    int saved_;
    bool committed_ = false;
};

bool transfer(int& from, int& to, int amount) {
    Transaction take(from);
    Transaction give(to);
    from -= amount;
    to += amount;
    if (amount <= 0 || from < 0) return false;
    take.commit();
    give.commit();
    return true;
}
--- hint
`Transaction` needs one more member, a `bool committed_ = false;`. `commit()` sets it to `true`, and the destructor only restores `value_` when it is still `false`.
--- hint
In `transfer`, make the two transactions first, then change both numbers. If the result is not allowed, just `return false;`: the destructors put both numbers back. Only on success call `commit()` on both.
--- check test | A transaction that is not committed rolls back
[] { int x = 4; { Transaction t(x); x = 99; x = 7; } return x == 4; }()
--- check test | A committed transaction keeps the change
[] { int x = 4; { Transaction t(x); x = 12; t.commit(); } return x == 12; }()
--- check test | A good transfer moves the amount
[] { int a = 50, b = 5; bool ok = transfer(a, b, 20); return ok && a == 30 && b == 25; }()
--- check test | Too much, zero or negative changes nothing
[] { int a = 50, b = 5; bool big = transfer(a, b, 80); bool zero = transfer(a, b, 0); bool neg = transfer(a, b, -10); return !big && !zero && !neg && a == 50 && b == 5; }()
--- check test | Moving everything is allowed
[] { int a = 50, b = 0; return transfer(a, b, 50) && a == 0 && b == 50; }()
--- check test | Transaction cannot be copied
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<Transaction> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<Transaction>

+++ practice | Debug: the dimmer that never dims
--- task
**Bug report:** "`Dimmer d(brightness, 10);` should turn the caller's `brightness` down to 10 while `d` lives, and put the old value back when `d` dies. Instead, `brightness` never changes at all."

The destructor, the deleted copy functions and the constructor's steps are all right. Find the one member that is wrong and fix it, keeping the class's name and its constructor's parameters. No `main`.
--- starter
class Dimmer {
public:
    Dimmer(int& level, int dimmed) : level_(level), saved_(level) { level_ = dimmed; }
    ~Dimmer() { level_ = saved_; }
    Dimmer(const Dimmer&) = delete;
    Dimmer& operator=(const Dimmer&) = delete;
private:
    int level_;
    int saved_;
};
--- solution
class Dimmer {
public:
    Dimmer(int& level, int dimmed) : level_(level), saved_(level) { level_ = dimmed; }
    ~Dimmer() { level_ = saved_; }
    Dimmer(const Dimmer&) = delete;
    Dimmer& operator=(const Dimmer&) = delete;
private:
    int& level_;
    int saved_;
};
--- hint
The constructor sets `level_`, but is `level_` the caller's variable, or a copy of it?
--- hint
A plain `int` member holds its own copy. To change the caller's variable, the member must refer to it: make it a reference.
--- check test | Dimmed while the dimmer lives
[] { int brightness = 80; bool inside; { Dimmer d(brightness, 10); inside = brightness == 10; } return inside; }()
--- check test | Restored when it dies
[] { int brightness = 80; { Dimmer d(brightness, 10); } return brightness == 80; }()
--- check test | Two dimmers unwind in reverse order
[] { int b = 80; bool ok = true; { Dimmer a(b, 50); { Dimmer c(b, 5); ok = ok && b == 5; } ok = ok && b == 50; } return ok && b == 80; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: a mission log in phases
--- task
Write `class Phase`, which writes a mission log in phases, and a function that uses it. No `main`.

- `Phase(std::vector<std::string>& log, const std::string& name)` adds `"begin <name>"` to `log`.
- `void event(const std::string& what)` adds `"<name>: <what>"` to `log` and counts the event.
- The destructor adds `"end <name> (<n> events)"`, where `<n>` is how many events this phase had. Write `event` for exactly one: `"end burn (1 event)"`, and `"end burn (0 events)"` for none.
- A `Phase` cannot be copied.
- `int poll(std::vector<std::string>& log, const std::vector<std::string>& answers)` opens a phase named `"poll"`, then goes through `answers` in order, logging each one as an event. It stops at the first answer that is `"no go"` (after logging it) and returns how many answers were `"go"` before it. If there is no `"no go"`, it returns the number of `"go"` answers in all.

`poll(log, {"go", "go", "no go", "go"})` returns 2, and the log ends `"begin poll"`, `"poll: go"`, `"poll: go"`, `"poll: no go"`, `"end poll (3 events)"`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Phase {
public:
    Phase(std::vector<std::string>& log, const std::string& name) : log_(log), name_(name) {}
    void event(const std::string& what) {}
private:
    std::vector<std::string>& log_;
    std::string name_;
};

int poll(std::vector<std::string>& log, const std::vector<std::string>& answers) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Phase {
public:
    Phase(std::vector<std::string>& log, const std::string& name) : log_(log), name_(name) {
        log_.push_back("begin " + name_);
    }
    ~Phase() {
        std::string word = events_ == 1 ? " event)" : " events)";
        log_.push_back("end " + name_ + " (" + std::to_string(events_) + word);
    }
    Phase(const Phase&) = delete;
    Phase& operator=(const Phase&) = delete;

    void event(const std::string& what) {
        log_.push_back(name_ + ": " + what);
        ++events_;
    }
private:
    std::vector<std::string>& log_;
    std::string name_;
    int events_ = 0;
};

int poll(std::vector<std::string>& log, const std::vector<std::string>& answers) {
    Phase phase(log, "poll");
    int go = 0;
    for (const std::string& a : answers) {
        phase.event(a);
        if (a == "no go") return go;
        if (a == "go") ++go;
    }
    return go;
}
--- hint
Give `Phase` an `int events_ = 0;` member. `event` pushes its line and adds 1. The destructor builds the "end" line with `std::to_string(events_)`, choosing `" event)"` or `" events)"` by whether `events_ == 1`.
--- hint
In `poll`, make the `Phase` first thing. Then you can `return` from the middle of the loop: the phase's destructor still writes the "end" line, after everything else.
--- check test | A phase with two events
[] { std::vector<std::string> log; { Phase p(log, "burn"); p.event("ignite"); p.event("cutoff"); } return log == std::vector<std::string>{"begin burn", "burn: ignite", "burn: cutoff", "end burn (2 events)"}; }()
--- check test | One event and no events
[] { std::vector<std::string> log; { Phase a(log, "a"); a.event("x"); } { Phase b(log, "b"); } return log == std::vector<std::string>{"begin a", "a: x", "end a (1 event)", "begin b", "end b (0 events)"}; }()
--- check test | Nested phases end inner first
[] { std::vector<std::string> log; { Phase outer(log, "flight"); { Phase inner(log, "coast"); inner.event("quiet"); } outer.event("landed"); } return log == std::vector<std::string>{"begin flight", "begin coast", "coast: quiet", "end coast (1 event)", "flight: landed", "end flight (1 event)"}; }()
--- check test | poll stops at the first no go, and still ends the phase
[] { std::vector<std::string> log; int n = poll(log, {"go", "go", "no go", "go"}); return n == 2 && log == std::vector<std::string>{"begin poll", "poll: go", "poll: go", "poll: no go", "end poll (3 events)"}; }()
--- check test | poll with every station go, and with nobody asked
[] { std::vector<std::string> a, b; int n = poll(a, {"go", "go", "go"}); int m = poll(b, {}); return n == 3 && a.back() == "end poll (3 events)" && m == 0 && b == std::vector<std::string>{"begin poll", "end poll (0 events)"}; }()

=== cpp3-02 | Copying: the rule of three
--- teach
Last lesson you wrote owners that must not be copied, and deleted their copy functions. Some owners *should* be copyable, though. A copy of a buffer of numbers ought to be a second buffer with the same numbers in it. This lesson writes the two copy functions by hand.

### What a copy does on its own

When you copy an object and have not written any copy functions, C++ copies each member, one at a time. For `int`s, `std::string`s and `std::vector`s that is exactly right: a copied vector gets its own elements.

A **raw pointer** is different. Copying a pointer copies the *address*, not the thing it points at. Think of a slip of paper with a house address on it: photocopying the slip does not build a second house. You end up with two slips and one house.

### Raw arrays on the heap

To see this go wrong, this lesson and the next break a rule from the basics course on purpose. The basics told you never to write raw `new` and `delete`. Here you will, once, to see what `std::vector` and `std::unique_ptr` do for you.

```cpp
int* data = new int[3]();   // three ints on the heap, all 0
data[1] = 7;                // use it like an array: 0 7 0
delete[] data;              // give all three back
```

- `new int[3]` asks the heap for three `int`s side by side and gives back a pointer to the first one. The `()` after it sets them all to zero; without it they would hold leftover junk, like the uninitialised values in the intermediate course.
- `data[1]` reaches an element through the pointer with square brackets, the same way you index a vector.
- `delete[]`, read "delete array", gives the whole block back. Memory from `new[]` must go back with [[delete with brackets|delete-brackets]], never plain `delete`.

### Two owners, one block

Now put that array inside a class:

```cpp
class Bad {
public:
    Bad() : data_(new int[3]()) {}
    ~Bad() { delete[] data_; }
private:
    int* data_;
};

Bad a;
Bad b = a;   // b.data_ == a.data_
             // writing through b changes a; at the end both destructors
             // delete the same block — a double free
```

`Bad b = a;` copies the member `data_`, so both objects hold the same address. This is a **shallow copy**: the pointer was copied, the block it points at was not. Change a number through `b` and `a` sees it change too. Worse, at the closing `}` both destructors run `delete[]` on the same block. Giving memory back twice is a **[[double free|double-free]]**, and it is undefined behavior.

What you want is a **deep copy**: the copy gets its own new block, with the same numbers copied into it. The picture in the [[deep copy|shallow-deep]] note shows the difference.

### Copying a range: std::copy

`std::copy(from_begin, from_end, to)`, from `<algorithm>`, copies the elements from `from_begin` up to (not including) `from_end`, and writes them starting at `to`. Plain pointers work as its iterators:

```cpp
int* a = new int[3]{4, 5, 6};   // the braces give the starting values
int* b = new int[3];
std::copy(a, a + 3, b);         // b now holds 4 5 6
delete[] a;
delete[] b;
```

`a` points at the first element, and `a + 3` means [[three ints further along|pointer-arithmetic]]: one past the last element, the end of a half-open range, like `end()` on a vector.

### The rule of three

Here is the rule this lesson is named after. The **[[rule of three|rule-of-three-name]]**: if a class needs its own destructor, it almost certainly needs its own **copy constructor** and **copy assignment operator** too, because all three deal with the same owned resource.

The copy constructor builds a brand-new object, so it only has to make its own block and fill it:

```cpp
Bad(const Bad& other)                  // copy constructor: build a new object
    : data_(new int[3]) {
    std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + 3, data_);
}
```

`other` is the object being copied. The initializer list gives the new object its own three `int`s, and the body copies `other`'s numbers into them.

### Copy assignment: overwrite an object that already exists

Assignment is the harder one. The object on the left already owns a block, which must be given back, and assigning an object to itself must be safe:

```cpp
Bad& operator=(const Bad& other) {     // copy assignment: overwrite an existing one
    if (this != &other) {              // a = a must not destroy a's own data
        int* fresh = new int[3];
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + 3, fresh);
        delete[] data_;
        data_ = fresh;
    }
    return *this;
}
```

Take it line by line:

- `this` is the hidden pointer to the object whose member function is running, from the const correctness lesson. `&other` is the address of `other`. So `this != &other`, read "this is not equal to the address of other", asks: is `other` a different object from me? For `a = a;` the answer is no, and there is nothing to do.
- Make the new block `fresh` and copy into it **first**. Only then `delete[]` the old block, and point `data_` at the new one. That order keeps the object valid at every step: at no moment does `data_` point at memory that has been given back.
- `return *this;` hands back the object itself, as a reference (the `Bad&` return type). That is what lets you [[chain assignments|chained-assignment]] like `a = b = c;`, the same as with `int`s.

### One counter for the whole class

The starter also has this line inside the class:

```cpp
static inline int copies = 0;
```

A **static data member** belongs to the class rather than to each object, like the static members in the lesson "Classes that keep their own rules". There is one counter, shared by every `Buffer`, and you reach it as `Buffer::copies`: "copies from Buffer". The word `inline` is what lets you give it its first value right there in the class.

**Watch out:** `Buffer b = a;` has an `=` sign in it, but it does **not** call `operator=`. `b` is being born on that line, so the copy constructor runs. Copy assignment only runs when the object on the left already exists, as in `c = a;` two lines after `Buffer c(4);`. Both paths make a copy, so both must do the deep copy, and both must count it.

In real code you would store a `std::vector<int>` and write none of this; the next lesson shows why. Writing it once by hand is how you understand what those types do for you.

::: context delete-brackets Two kinds of delete
`new int` makes one `int`; `new int[3]` makes an array of them. Each has its own way back: `delete` for one object, `delete[]` for an array. Mixing them up, such as `delete` on memory from `new[]`, is undefined behavior. The program may seem fine, or it may crash, or quietly damage the heap. The compiler cannot always spot the mix-up, because an `int*` looks the same either way. That is one more reason modern C++ keeps raw arrays inside types like `std::vector`, which always use the right one.
:::

::: context double-free Why giving back twice is so bad
When memory is given back, the heap may hand that same block to the next `new`, for some other part of the program. A second `delete[]` then frees memory that now belongs to someone else, and the heap's own bookkeeping can be corrupted. The damage often shows up far away from the mistake, much later. Double frees and "use after free" bugs are among the most common ways attackers break into programs, which is why security teams hunt for them.
:::

::: context shallow-deep Two slips, one house
A shallow copy duplicates only the pointer, so both objects share one block. A deep copy duplicates the block too, so each object owns its own.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="85" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">shallow copy</text>
  <text x="270" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">deep copy</text>

  <text x="230" y="128" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">4 5 6</text>
  <rect x="275" y="110" width="70" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="310" y="128" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">4 5 6</text>
  <line x1="230" y1="56" x2="230" y2="106" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="310" y1="56" x2="310" y2="106" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="270" y="156" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">two blocks, one owner each</text>
```
