:::

::: context pointer-arithmetic Adding to a pointer
Adding a number to a pointer moves it by that many *elements*, not bytes. An `int` usually takes 4 bytes, so `a + 3` is an address 12 bytes past `a`, which is exactly where a fourth `int` would start. That is also how `data[1]` works: it means `*(data + 1)`, "follow the pointer one element along". Pointing one past the end is allowed, as long as you never follow it with `*`; following it is undefined behavior, the same as `*` on a vector's `end()`.
:::

::: context rule-of-three-name A rule of thumb with a long history
The rule of three has been taught to C++ programmers since the early 1990s. It is a rule of thumb, not a rule the compiler enforces: the compiler will happily write a shallow copy for a class with a hand-written destructor, and warn about nothing. The rule exists because the three functions share one job, looking after the owned block. Change how one of them treats the block and the other two almost always have to change with it.
:::

::: context chained-assignment Why assignment returns the object
For `int`s, `a = b = c;` means `a = (b = c);`: first `b` gets `c`'s value, then that result is given to `a`. For your own type to work the same way, `operator=` must return the object it has changed. Returning `*this` by reference, `Bad&`, hands back the object itself rather than a copy of it, so no extra copy is made along the way.
:::
--- task
`Buffer` owns an `int` array and frees it in its destructor, but a copy shares the array with the original. Give `Buffer` a **deep** copy constructor, `Buffer(const Buffer& other)`, and a deep copy assignment operator, `Buffer& operator=(const Buffer& other)`.

- Each copy gets its own array with the same size and the same numbers, so changing one buffer never changes the other. After `b = a;`, `b` has `a`'s size, even if it started with a different one.
- Both functions add 1 to the static counter `Buffer::copies` each time they copy.
- Assigning a buffer to itself must leave it unchanged.

No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Buffer {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;

    explicit Buffer(std::size_t size) : size_(size), data_(new int[size]()) {}
    ~Buffer() { delete[] data_; }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Buffer {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;

    explicit Buffer(std::size_t size) : size_(size), data_(new int[size]()) {}
    ~Buffer() { delete[] data_; }

    Buffer(const Buffer& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(new int[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
        ++copies;
    }

    Buffer& operator=(const Buffer& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = new int[other.size_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- hint
Start with the copy constructor. It is building a new buffer, so in its initializer list it takes `other.size_` and makes its own array with `new int[other.size_]`. Then it copies the numbers across with `std::copy` and adds 1 to `copies`.
--- hint
The copy constructor's body is `std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_); ++copies;`. For `operator=`, check `this != &other` first. Inside that `if`: make `int* fresh = new int[other.size_];`, copy into it, `delete[] data_;`, then set `data_ = fresh;` and `size_ = other.size_;`, and add 1 to `copies`.
--- hint
After the `if`, `operator=` ends with `return *this;`. Copying the size matters: the check assigns a 2-element buffer to a 5-element one, and afterwards the left one must report size 2.
--- check test | A copy has its own data
[] { Buffer a(3); a.set(0, 7); Buffer b = a; b.set(0, 9); return a.get(0) == 7 && b.get(0) == 9 && b.size() == 3; }()
--- check test | Assignment copies deeply, even between sizes
[] { Buffer a(2); a.set(1, 4); Buffer b(5); b = a; a.set(1, 8); return b.size() == 2 && b.get(1) == 4; }()
--- check test | Self-assignment is harmless
[] { Buffer a(2); a.set(0, 3); Buffer& same = a; a = same; return a.size() == 2 && a.get(0) == 3; }()
--- check test | Every copy is counted
[] { Buffer::copies = 0; Buffer a(1); Buffer b = a; Buffer c(4); c = a; return Buffer::copies == 2; }()

+++ practice | A tiny string that copies deeply
--- task
`Name` keeps its letters in a `char` array on the heap and frees it in its destructor. Right now a copy shares the array with the original. Give `Name` a deep copy constructor, `Name(const Name& other)`, and a deep copy assignment operator, `Name& operator=(const Name& other)`. No `main`.

- A copy gets its own array with the same letters, so `set` on one name never changes the other.
- After `b = a;`, `b` has `a`'s length and letters, even if its old text was longer or shorter.
- Assigning a name to itself leaves it unchanged, and `a = b = c;` must work.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>

class Name {
public:
    explicit Name(const std::string& s) : size_(s.size()), text_(new char[s.size()]) {
        std::copy(s.begin(), s.end(), text_);
    }
    ~Name() { delete[] text_; }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    std::string str() const { return std::string(text_, text_ + size_); }
    void set(std::size_t i, char c) { text_[i] = c; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    char* text_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>

class Name {
public:
    explicit Name(const std::string& s) : size_(s.size()), text_(new char[s.size()]) {
        std::copy(s.begin(), s.end(), text_);
    }
    ~Name() { delete[] text_; }

    Name(const Name& other) : size_(other.size_), text_(new char[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.text_, other.text_ + other.size_, text_);
    }

    Name& operator=(const Name& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            char* fresh = new char[other.size_];
            std::copy(other.text_, other.text_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] text_;
            text_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    std::string str() const { return std::string(text_, text_ + size_); }
    void set(std::size_t i, char c) { text_[i] = c; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    char* text_;
};
--- hint
The copy constructor makes its own `char` array of `other.size_` letters in the initializer list, then copies the letters across with `std::copy`.
--- hint
Copy assignment checks `this != &other` first. Inside, make the new array and copy into it, then `delete[]` the old one, then take the new pointer and the new size. End with `return *this;`, which is what makes `a = b = c;` work.
--- check test | A copy has its own letters
[] { Name a("vega"); Name b = a; b.set(0, 'r'); return a.str() == "vega" && b.str() == "rega"; }()
--- check test | Assignment copies between lengths
[] { Name a("io"); Name b("callisto"); b = a; a.set(0, 'X'); Name c("x"); c = b; return b.str() == "io" && b.size() == 2 && c.str() == "io"; }()
--- check test | Self-assignment and chained assignment
[] { Name a("titan"); Name& same = a; a = same; Name b("b"); Name c("rhea"); b = a = c; return a.str() == "rhea" && b.str() == "rhea"; }()
--- check test | An empty name copies too
[] { Name e(""); Name f = e; Name g("moon"); g = e; return f.size() == 0 && g.str() == ""; }()

+++ practice | A grid with rows and columns
--- task
`Grid` owns `rows × cols` numbers in one heap array, stored row after row, so the number at row `r`, column `c` is at index `r * cols + c`. Its copies are shallow. Give it a deep copy constructor and a deep copy assignment operator. No `main`.

- A copy has the same shape and the same numbers, in an array of its own.
- After `b = a;`, `b.rows()` and `b.cols()` are `a`'s, whatever shape `b` had before.
- Self-assignment is harmless, and `a = b = c;` works.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Grid {
public:
    Grid(int rows, int cols) : rows_(rows), cols_(cols), cells_(new int[rows * cols]()) {}
    ~Grid() { delete[] cells_; }

    int rows() const { return rows_; }
    int cols() const { return cols_; }
    int get(int r, int c) const { return cells_[r * cols_ + c]; }
    void set(int r, int c, int value) { cells_[r * cols_ + c] = value; }

private:
    int rows_;
    int cols_;
    int* cells_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Grid {
public:
    Grid(int rows, int cols) : rows_(rows), cols_(cols), cells_(new int[rows * cols]()) {}
    ~Grid() { delete[] cells_; }

    Grid(const Grid& other) : rows_(other.rows_), cols_(other.cols_), cells_(new int[other.rows_ * other.cols_]) {
        std::copy(other.cells_, other.cells_ + rows_ * cols_, cells_);
    }

    Grid& operator=(const Grid& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int n = other.rows_ * other.cols_;
            int* fresh = new int[n];
            std::copy(other.cells_, other.cells_ + n, fresh);
            delete[] cells_;
            cells_ = fresh;
            rows_ = other.rows_;
            cols_ = other.cols_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    int rows() const { return rows_; }
    int cols() const { return cols_; }
    int get(int r, int c) const { return cells_[r * cols_ + c]; }
    void set(int r, int c, int value) { cells_[r * cols_ + c] = value; }

private:
    int rows_;
    int cols_;
    int* cells_;
};
--- hint
The number of cells is `rows * cols`. Both copy functions need an array of exactly that many `int`s, and `std::copy` over that many.
--- hint
In `operator=`, remember that both `rows_` and `cols_` change, not only the pointer. Take them from `other` after the new array is filled and the old one is deleted.
--- check test | A copy is independent
[] { Grid a(2, 3); a.set(1, 2, 8); Grid b = a; b.set(1, 2, 1); return a.get(1, 2) == 8 && b.get(1, 2) == 1 && b.rows() == 2 && b.cols() == 3; }()
--- check test | Assignment takes the other shape
[] { Grid a(1, 4); a.set(0, 3, 5); Grid b(3, 3); b = a; a.set(0, 3, 0); return b.rows() == 1 && b.cols() == 4 && b.get(0, 3) == 5; }()
--- check test | Chained and self-assignment
[] { Grid a(2, 2); Grid b(1, 1); Grid c(2, 1); c.set(1, 0, 6); a = b = c; Grid& same = a; a = same; return a.rows() == 2 && a.cols() == 1 && a.get(1, 0) == 6 && b.get(1, 0) == 6; }()

+++ practice | No block leaks, none freed twice
--- task
Every heap block in this program goes through two helpers in the starter: `alloc(n)` makes a block of `n` zeros and adds 1 to the global counter `blocks_live`, and `release(p)` frees one and takes 1 away. So `blocks_live` is how many blocks exist right now.

`Samples` holds numbers in one such block. Write its copy constructor and copy assignment operator so that each copy is deep **and** `blocks_live` stays exact: every copy made adds exactly one block, assignment frees the old block, and nothing is ever freed twice. Also write `std::vector<int> to_vector() const`, which returns the numbers in order. No `main`.

After `Samples a({1, 2, 3}); Samples b = a;` there are 2 blocks. After `Samples c({9}); c = a;` there are still 3, and when all three die, 0.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <vector>

int blocks_live = 0;
int* alloc(std::size_t n) { ++blocks_live; return new int[n](); }
void release(int* p) { --blocks_live; delete[] p; }

class Samples {
public:
    explicit Samples(const std::vector<int>& values) : size_(values.size()), data_(alloc(values.size())) {
        std::copy(values.begin(), values.end(), data_);
    }
    ~Samples() { release(data_); }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }
    std::vector<int> to_vector() const { return {}; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <vector>

int blocks_live = 0;
int* alloc(std::size_t n) { ++blocks_live; return new int[n](); }
void release(int* p) { --blocks_live; delete[] p; }

class Samples {
public:
    explicit Samples(const std::vector<int>& values) : size_(values.size()), data_(alloc(values.size())) {
        std::copy(values.begin(), values.end(), data_);
    }
    ~Samples() { release(data_); }

    Samples(const Samples& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(alloc(other.size_)) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
    }

    Samples& operator=(const Samples& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = alloc(other.size_);
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            release(data_);
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }
    std::vector<int> to_vector() const { return std::vector<int>(data_, data_ + size_); }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- hint
Use `alloc` and `release` everywhere you would write `new int[…]` and `delete[]`. The copy constructor calls `alloc` once; copy assignment calls `alloc` once and `release` once.
--- hint
Two pointers work as a range, so `std::vector<int>(data_, data_ + size_)` builds a vector from the block, in order.
--- check test | A copy is deep and adds one block
[] { blocks_live = 0; Samples a({1, 2, 3}); Samples b = a; b.set(0, 7); return blocks_live == 2 && a.to_vector() == std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3} && b.to_vector() == std::vector<int>{7, 2, 3}; }()
--- check test | Assignment frees the old block
[] { blocks_live = 0; Samples a({1, 2, 3}); Samples c({9}); c = a; bool two = blocks_live == 2; Samples d = c; return two && blocks_live == 3 && c.to_vector() == std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3}; }()
--- check test | Self-assignment neither leaks nor frees
[] { blocks_live = 0; Samples a({4, 5}); Samples& same = a; a = same; return blocks_live == 1 && a.to_vector() == std::vector<int>{4, 5}; }()
--- check test | Everything is freed at the end
[] { blocks_live = 0; { Samples a({1}); Samples b = a; Samples c({2, 2}); c = b; b = c; } return blocks_live == 0; }()

+++ practice | Copying a buffer that may hold nothing
--- task
`Bytes` holds `n` numbers on the heap, but when `n` is 0 it holds **no array at all**: its pointer is `nullptr`, and `has_storage()` returns `false`. Give it a deep copy constructor and a deep copy assignment operator that keep that rule. No `main`.

- Copying or assigning an empty `Bytes` gives an empty one with no storage. Assigning an empty one to a full one frees the full one's array.
- Copying or assigning a non-empty one gives its own array with the same numbers.
- Self-assignment leaves it unchanged, empty or not.

Remember that `delete[]` on `nullptr` does nothing, so the destructor is already fine.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Bytes {
public:
    explicit Bytes(std::size_t n) : size_(n), data_(n > 0 ? new int[n]() : nullptr) {}
    ~Bytes() { delete[] data_; }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    bool has_storage() const { return data_ != nullptr; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Bytes {
public:
    explicit Bytes(std::size_t n) : size_(n), data_(n > 0 ? new int[n]() : nullptr) {}
    ~Bytes() { delete[] data_; }

    Bytes(const Bytes& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(other.size_ > 0 ? new int[other.size_] : nullptr) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, data_);
    }

    Bytes& operator=(const Bytes& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = other.size_ > 0 ? new int[other.size_] : nullptr;
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    bool has_storage() const { return data_ != nullptr; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- hint
Decide the new pointer the same way the constructor does: `other.size_ > 0 ? new int[other.size_] : nullptr`. Copying zero elements with `std::copy` is fine, even from and to `nullptr`.
--- hint
In `operator=`, after `delete[] data_;` the new pointer may be `nullptr`. That is exactly right for an empty source.
--- check test | Copying an empty Bytes gives no storage
[] { Bytes e(0); Bytes f = e; return f.size() == 0 && !f.has_storage(); }()
--- check test | Assigning empty to full frees the array
[] { Bytes full(3); full.set(2, 9); Bytes e(0); full = e; return full.size() == 0 && !full.has_storage(); }()
--- check test | Assigning full to empty makes an array of its own
[] { Bytes full(3); full.set(2, 9); Bytes e(0); e = full; full.set(2, 1); return e.size() == 3 && e.has_storage() && e.get(2) == 9; }()
--- check test | A deep copy, and self-assignment empty or not
[] { Bytes a(2); a.set(0, 4); Bytes b = a; b.set(0, 5); Bytes e(0); Bytes& se = e; e = se; Bytes& sa = a; a = sa; return a.get(0) == 4 && b.get(0) == 5 && !e.has_storage() && a.size() == 2; }()

+++ practice | Debug: assignment that keeps the old size
--- task
**Bug report:** "After `b = a;`, `b.size()` still says `b`'s old size. When `a` is longer than `b`, only the first few of `a`'s numbers arrive in `b`."

`Series` owns an array of numbers. Its copy constructor is right. Its copy assignment operator uses the wrong size. Fix `operator=` so that after `b = a;`, `b` has `a`'s size and every one of `a`'s numbers, in an array of its own. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Series {
public:
    explicit Series(std::size_t size) : size_(size), data_(new int[size]()) {}
    ~Series() { delete[] data_; }

    Series(const Series& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(new int[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
    }

    Series& operator=(const Series& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = new int[size_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>

class Series {
public:
    explicit Series(std::size_t size) : size_(size), data_(new int[size]()) {}
    ~Series() { delete[] data_; }

    Series(const Series& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(new int[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
    }

    Series& operator=(const Series& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = new int[other.size_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- hint
Inside `operator=`, `size_` is the size of the object on the left, the one being overwritten. Whose size should the new array have?
--- hint
Use `other.size_` both for `new int[…]` and for the end of the `std::copy` range, and after swapping in the new array, set `size_ = other.size_;`.
--- check test | A longer source arrives in full
[] { Series a(5); for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) a.set(i, i + 1); Series b(2); b = a; return b.size() == 5 && b.get(0) == 1 && b.get(4) == 5; }()
--- check test | A shorter source shrinks the target
[] { Series a(1); a.set(0, 8); Series b(6); b = a; return b.size() == 1 && b.get(0) == 8; }()
--- check test | The copy is still deep
[] { Series a(3); a.set(1, 4); Series b(3); b = a; a.set(1, 0); return b.get(1) == 4; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: a telemetry array that grows
--- task
Write `class Telemetry`, a list of readings that grows by itself, like a small `std::vector<int>`, with the rule of three. It keeps a heap array of `capacity()` slots, of which the first `size()` are in use. No `main`.

- `Telemetry()` starts with size 0, capacity 0 and no array (`nullptr`).
- `void push(int x)` adds `x` at the end. When the array is full it first grows: capacity 0 becomes 1, and after that the capacity doubles (1, 2, 4, 8, …). Growing means a new array, the old numbers copied across, and the old array freed.
- `std::size_t size() const`, `std::size_t capacity() const`, and `std::vector<int> values() const`, the readings in order.
- A deep copy constructor and a deep copy assignment operator. A copy has the same size, the same capacity and the same readings, in an array of its own. Self-assignment is harmless.
- A destructor that frees the array.

After pushing 3, 1, 4, 1 and 5, `size()` is 5, `capacity()` is 8 and `values()` is `{3, 1, 4, 1, 5}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <vector>

class Telemetry {
public:
    Telemetry() {}
    void push(int x) {}
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    std::size_t capacity() const { return 0; }
    std::vector<int> values() const { return {}; }
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <vector>

class Telemetry {
public:
    Telemetry() : size_(0), capacity_(0), data_(nullptr) {}
    ~Telemetry() { delete[] data_; }

    Telemetry(const Telemetry& other)
        : size_(other.size_), capacity_(other.capacity_), data_(new int[other.capacity_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, data_);
    }

    Telemetry& operator=(const Telemetry& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = new int[other.capacity_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
            capacity_ = other.capacity_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    void push(int x) {
        if (size_ == capacity_) {
            std::size_t bigger = capacity_ == 0 ? 1 : capacity_ * 2;
            int* fresh = new int[bigger];
            std::copy(data_, data_ + size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            capacity_ = bigger;
        }
        data_[size_] = x;
        ++size_;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    std::size_t capacity() const { return capacity_; }
    std::vector<int> values() const { return std::vector<int>(data_, data_ + size_); }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    std::size_t capacity_;
    int* data_;
};
--- hint
Keep three private members: `size_`, `capacity_` and an `int* data_`. In `push`, when `size_ == capacity_`, work out the new capacity, make a new array, copy the `size_` numbers in use, free the old array and keep the new one. Then store `x` at index `size_` and add 1 to `size_`.
--- hint
Growing inside `push` and copy assignment are the same four steps: new array, `std::copy`, `delete[]` the old one, keep the new pointer. The copy functions allocate `other.capacity_` slots but copy only `other.size_` numbers.
--- check test | Pushing grows the capacity 1, 2, 4, 8
[] { Telemetry t; std::vector<std::size_t> caps; for (int x : {3, 1, 4, 1, 5}) { t.push(x); caps.push_back(t.capacity()); } return caps == std::vector<std::size_t>{1, 2, 4, 4, 8} && t.size() == 5 && t.values() == std::vector<int>{3, 1, 4, 1, 5}; }()
--- check test | A new Telemetry is empty
[] { Telemetry t; return t.size() == 0 && t.capacity() == 0 && t.values().empty(); }()
--- check test | A copy keeps size and capacity, and is deep
[] { Telemetry a; for (int i = 0; i < 3; ++i) a.push(i); Telemetry b = a; b.push(99); a.push(-1); return b.size() == 4 && b.capacity() == 4 && b.values() == std::vector<int>{0, 1, 2, 99} && a.values() == std::vector<int>{0, 1, 2, -1}; }()
--- check test | Assignment, self-assignment and copies of empty
[] { Telemetry a; a.push(7); Telemetry b; for (int i = 0; i < 9; ++i) b.push(i); b = a; Telemetry& same = b; b = same; Telemetry e; Telemetry f = e; f.push(2); a = e; return b.values() == std::vector<int>{7} && b.capacity() == 1 && f.values() == std::vector<int>{2} && a.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Ten thousand readings
[] { Telemetry t; long long sum = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 10000; ++i) t.push(i); for (int x : t.values()) sum += x; return t.size() == 10000 && t.capacity() == 16384 && sum == 49995000; }()

=== cpp3-03 | Moving, and the rule of zero
--- teach
Last lesson `Buffer` learned to copy itself deeply: a new block, and every number copied across. That is correct, but it costs something. For a big buffer, copying means asking the heap for memory and copying every element. This lesson shows a cheaper way when the original is about to disappear anyway.

### Moving house

When you move house, you do not photocopy your furniture and then burn the old set. You carry it to the new place, and the old house is left empty.

Objects are often in the same spot. The source of a copy is very often about to die: a **temporary** (a value with no name, like `Buffer(4)` passed straight into a function), or a local variable being returned from a function. Copying something that is about to be thrown away is wasted work. It is far cheaper to **steal** its memory: take its pointer, and leave the source empty. That is a **move**.

### The move constructor

```cpp
Buffer(Buffer&& other) noexcept               // move constructor
    : size_(other.size_), data_(other.data_) {
    other.size_ = 0;
    other.data_ = nullptr;                    // the source must still be safe to destroy
}
```

The new object copies two members, the size and the pointer, and no elements at all. Then it empties the source. That last step matters: the source's destructor still runs later, and it will `delete[]` whatever `data_` holds. Setting it to `nullptr` makes that harmless, because [[deleting nullptr|delete-nullptr]] does nothing. Without it, both objects would free the same block: the double free from last lesson.

### && means "about to go away"

`Buffer&&`, with two ampersands, is an **rvalue reference**: a reference that binds only to things that are about to go away, such as temporaries. (One `&` is the ordinary reference you know.) The word [[rvalue|rvalue-word]] roughly means "a value with no lasting name".

So C++ picks the move constructor on its own when the source is a temporary, and the copy constructor when the source is an ordinary named variable that will live on.

### std::move gives permission

Sometimes you have a named variable and you know you are finished with it. `std::move(x)`, from `<utility>`, says so:

```cpp
Buffer a(3);
Buffer b = std::move(a);   // the move constructor runs: b takes a's block
// now a.size() is 0
```

`std::move` does [[not move anything by itself|move-is-a-cast]]. It is a cast: it says "you may treat `x` as expendable". The move constructor does the actual stealing.

After a move, the source is valid but unspecified: you may give it a new value or let it be destroyed, nothing more. Your `Buffer` happens to leave it empty, but you should not count on any particular value in general.

### Move assignment

A move can also overwrite an object that already exists, the way copy assignment does. It follows the same shape as last lesson's `operator=`:

```cpp
Buffer& operator=(Buffer&& other) noexcept {   // move assignment
    if (this != &other) {       // moving a buffer onto itself: nothing to do
        delete[] data_;         // give back my own block first
        data_ = other.data_;    // steal the source's block
        size_ = other.size_;
        other.data_ = nullptr;  // leave the source empty
        other.size_ = 0;
    }
    return *this;
}
```

Here freeing first is safe, because nothing new is allocated: the object takes a block that already exists.

### The rule of five

Adding the move constructor and move assignment to last lesson's three (destructor, copy constructor, copy assignment) gives the **rule of five**: a class that owns a raw resource should think about all five.

### Why noexcept

Both move functions are marked `noexcept`, read "no except". C++ has a way for a function to report an error by jumping straight out of it, called throwing an **[[exception|exceptions-word]]**. `noexcept` is a promise that this function never does that.

It matters because of how a `std::vector` grows. When it runs out of room, it asks for a bigger block and [[moves its elements across|vector-growth]]. If a move could fail halfway, the vector could not put things back the way they were. So it only uses your move constructor if it is marked `noexcept`. Otherwise, to stay safe, it **copies** every element instead.

**Watch out:** forgetting `noexcept` gives no error and no warning. Everything still works; vectors of your type are silently slower, because every time they grow they copy every element. When you write a move constructor or move assignment, write `noexcept` on it.

### The rule of zero

And now the best news. A class whose members already manage themselves should declare **none** of the five. That is the **[[rule of zero|rule-of-zero-name]]**. Members like `std::string`, `std::vector`, `std::unique_ptr`, or a class like your finished `Buffer`, already know how to copy, move and clean up. The compiler writes all five for the outer class, member by member, and each member does its own job correctly:

```cpp
struct Track {
    std::string title;
    std::vector<int> samples;
};   // copies, moves and cleans up correctly with no code at all
```

So the rule of five is for the few low-level types that own a raw resource. Everything built on top of them follows the rule of zero.

::: context delete-nullptr Why nullptr is a safe leftover
The C++ rules say that `delete` and `delete[]` on a null pointer do nothing at all. That is what makes an empty moved-from object safe: its destructor runs as usual, hands `nullptr` to `delete[]`, and nothing happens. The same trick shows up all over the standard library. A moved-from `std::unique_ptr` is guaranteed to hold `nullptr`, so its destructor has nothing to delete.
:::

::: context rvalue-word Where "rvalue" comes from
The name goes back to the language C. An *lvalue* was something that could stand on the **left** of an `=`, like a variable: `x = 5;`. An *rvalue* could only stand on the **right**, like `5` or `a + b`: you cannot write `5 = x;`. Modern C++ has refined these ideas, but the rough picture still works: an lvalue has a name and lives on; an rvalue is a temporary result that disappears at the end of the line. An rvalue reference, `&&`, is the kind of reference that can bind to one of those.
:::

::: context move-is-a-cast std::move is only a label
Inside the library, `std::move(x)` is about one line long: it converts `x` to an rvalue reference, the same as writing `static_cast<Buffer&&>(x)`. No memory changes hands until a move constructor or move assignment receives that reference and does the stealing. If a type has no move functions, "moving" it quietly copies it instead. The name was chosen because of what it lets happen next, not because of what it does itself.
:::

::: context exceptions-word Exceptions, and where they come back
In much C++ code, a function that hits an error can `throw` an exception, which jumps out through every waiting function until some code `catch`es it. This app compiles with exceptions switched off, the way a lot of embedded and flight software does, so `throw` and `try` do not compile here. The lesson "Errors without exceptions", later in this course, shows the other ways C++ reports errors. You still write `noexcept`, because the standard library reads it.
:::

::: context vector-growth How a vector grows
A vector keeps its elements in one block. When the block is full and you `push_back` one more, it asks for a bigger block (usually 1.5 to 2 times the size), transfers every element across, then frees the old block. With a `noexcept` move, each transfer steals a pointer. Without it, each transfer is a full deep copy.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">old block (full)</text>
  <rect x="10" y="28" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="50" y="28" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="90" y="28" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="130" y="28" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="10" y="92" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">new block (twice as big)</text>
  <rect x="10" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="50" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="90" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="130" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="170" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="210" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <rect x="250" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <rect x="290" y="100" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <line x1="30" y1="58" x2="30" y2="96" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="70" y1="58" x2="70" y2="96" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="110" y1="58" x2="110" y2="96" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="150" y1="58" x2="150" y2="96" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="190" y="36" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">each line is a move</text>
  <text x="190" y="51" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">if it is noexcept,</text>
  <text x="190" y="66" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">a full copy if not</text>
  <text x="190" y="145" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">orange: the element being pushed</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context rule-of-zero-name Write no code, get no bugs
The name "rule of zero" became popular in 2012, from a blog post by R. Martinho Fernandes, as the natural partner of the rule of three and the rule of five. The idea is that each class should do one job: either own a resource (and then it writes the special functions carefully), or combine other types (and then it writes none). Code you never write cannot hold a double free. In well-designed code, most classes declare none of the five.
:::
--- task
Give `Buffer` a move constructor, `Buffer(Buffer&& other) noexcept`, and a move assignment operator, `Buffer& operator=(Buffer&& other) noexcept`.

- Each one adds 1 to the static counter `Buffer::moves`.
- Each one leaves the source with size 0 and no data (`nullptr`).
- Move assignment gives back the object's own array before it steals, and moving a buffer onto itself must leave it unchanged.

Then write `struct Album { std::string title; Buffer art; };` with **no** special member functions of its own: the rule of zero.

No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Buffer {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;
    static inline int moves = 0;

    explicit Buffer(std::size_t size) : size_(size), data_(new int[size]()) {}
    ~Buffer() { delete[] data_; }

    Buffer(const Buffer& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(new int[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
        ++copies;
    }

    Buffer& operator=(const Buffer& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = new int[other.size_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Buffer {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;
    static inline int moves = 0;

    explicit Buffer(std::size_t size) : size_(size), data_(new int[size]()) {}
    ~Buffer() { delete[] data_; }

    Buffer(const Buffer& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(new int[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
        ++copies;
    }

    Buffer& operator=(const Buffer& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            int* fresh = new int[other.size_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    Buffer(Buffer&& other) noexcept : size_(other.size_), data_(other.data_) {
        other.size_ = 0;
        other.data_ = nullptr;
        ++moves;
    }

    Buffer& operator=(Buffer&& other) noexcept {
        if (this != &other) {
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = other.data_;
            size_ = other.size_;
            other.data_ = nullptr;
            other.size_ = 0;
            ++moves;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int value) { data_[i] = value; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};

struct Album {
    std::string title;
    Buffer art;
};
--- hint
The move constructor takes `Buffer&& other`. In its initializer list it takes `other.size_` and `other.data_` as they are, with no `new` and no copying of elements. Then its body empties the source and counts the move.
--- hint
The move constructor's body is `other.size_ = 0; other.data_ = nullptr; ++moves;`. Move assignment follows the explanation's version: inside `if (this != &other)`, `delete[] data_;` first, then steal both members, empty the source and add 1 to `moves`. It ends with `return *this;`.
--- hint
Put `noexcept` on both, right after the closing `)` of the parameter list, or the growing-vector check will see copies. `Album` is only its two members, with nothing else declared; the compiler then moves `art` with your move constructor.
--- check test | Moving steals, the source is left empty
[] { Buffer::copies = 0; Buffer::moves = 0; Buffer a(3); a.set(2, 5); Buffer b = std::move(a); return b.size() == 3 && b.get(2) == 5 && a.size() == 0 && Buffer::moves == 1 && Buffer::copies == 0; }()
--- check test | Move assignment
[] { Buffer::moves = 0; Buffer a(2); a.set(0, 1); Buffer b(9); b = std::move(a); return b.size() == 2 && b.get(0) == 1 && a.size() == 0 && Buffer::moves == 1; }()
--- check test | Self-move leaves the buffer as it was
[] { Buffer a(2); a.set(1, 6); Buffer& same = a; a = std::move(same); return a.size() == 2 && a.get(1) == 6; }()
--- check test | A growing vector moves, never copies (noexcept)
[] { Buffer::copies = 0; std::vector<Buffer> v; for (int i = 0; i < 20; ++i) v.push_back(Buffer(4)); return Buffer::copies == 0 && v.size() == 20; }()
--- check test | Album follows the rule of zero
[] { Buffer::copies = 0; Buffer::moves = 0; Album a{"Blue", Buffer(8)}; Album b = std::move(a); Album c = b; return b.art.size() == 8 && a.art.size() == 0 && Buffer::copies == 1 && Buffer::moves >= 1 && c.title == "Blue"; }()

+++ practice | A file handle you can move but not copy
--- task
`File` is an RAII handle: its constructor opens a file and adds 1 to the global `open_count`, and its destructor closes it and takes 1 away, unless the handle holds `-1`, which means "no file". Copying is deleted. Make it **movable**, so a `File` can be returned from functions and kept in a vector. No `main`.

- `File(File&& other) noexcept` takes `other`'s file number and leaves `other` holding `-1`.
- `File& operator=(File&& other) noexcept` first closes the file it holds, if any (take 1 from `open_count`), then takes `other`'s number and leaves `other` holding `-1`. Moving a `File` onto itself changes nothing.

`open_count` must always equal the number of files really open.
--- starter
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

int open_count = 0;

class File {
public:
    explicit File(int fd) : fd_(fd) { ++open_count; }
    ~File() {
        if (fd_ != -1) --open_count;
    }
    File(const File&) = delete;
    File& operator=(const File&) = delete;

    int fd() const { return fd_; }

private:
    int fd_;
};
--- solution
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

int open_count = 0;

class File {
public:
    explicit File(int fd) : fd_(fd) { ++open_count; }
    ~File() {
        if (fd_ != -1) --open_count;
    }
    File(const File&) = delete;
    File& operator=(const File&) = delete;

    File(File&& other) noexcept : fd_(other.fd_) { other.fd_ = -1; }

    File& operator=(File&& other) noexcept {
        if (this != &other) {
            if (fd_ != -1) --open_count;
            fd_ = other.fd_;
            other.fd_ = -1;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    int fd() const { return fd_; }

private:
    int fd_;
};
--- hint
The move constructor takes `other.fd_` in its initializer list and then sets `other.fd_ = -1;`, so `other`'s destructor closes nothing.
--- hint
Move assignment is the same idea, but the object on the left may already hold a file: inside `if (this != &other)`, close it first (`if (fd_ != -1) --open_count;`), then take `other`'s number and set `other`'s to `-1`. Put `noexcept` on both functions.
--- check test | Moving hands the file over
[] { open_count = 0; File a(3); File b = std::move(a); return b.fd() == 3 && a.fd() == -1 && open_count == 1; }()
--- check test | Move assignment closes the old file
[] { open_count = 0; File a(3); File b(4); b = std::move(a); bool one = open_count == 1 && b.fd() == 3 && a.fd() == -1; return one; }()
--- check test | Self-move and moved-from handles
[] { open_count = 0; File a(5); File& same = a; a = std::move(same); File b = std::move(a); File c = std::move(a); return b.fd() == 5 && c.fd() == -1 && open_count == 1; }()
--- check test | A vector of files grows without closing any
[] { open_count = 0; { std::vector<File> v; for (int i = 0; i < 50; ++i) v.push_back(File(i)); if (open_count != 50 || v[49].fd() != 49) return false; } return open_count == 0 && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<File> && !std::is_copy_constructible_v<File>; }()

+++ practice | Delete the code: the rule of zero
--- task
`Playlist` holds a `std::string` and a `std::vector<std::string>`, which already copy, move and clean up correctly. Yet someone hand-wrote a copy constructor, a copy assignment operator and a destructor, and the copy constructor forgets the songs. Worse, because those functions are declared, the compiler no longer writes a move constructor, so every "move" of a `Playlist` is really a copy.

Make `Playlist` follow the rule of zero: keep the two members and the two-argument constructor, and remove the three hand-written functions. After that, a copy has every song, and `Playlist` can be moved without copying (`std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Playlist>` is true). No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Playlist {
    std::string name;
    std::vector<std::string> songs;

    Playlist(std::string n, std::vector<std::string> s) : name(std::move(n)), songs(std::move(s)) {}

    Playlist(const Playlist& other) : name(other.name) {}
    Playlist& operator=(const Playlist& other) {
        name = other.name;
        songs = other.songs;
        return *this;
    }
    ~Playlist() {}
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Playlist {
    std::string name;
    std::vector<std::string> songs;

    Playlist(std::string n, std::vector<std::string> s) : name(std::move(n)), songs(std::move(s)) {}
};
--- hint
Look at which members `Playlist` has. Does any of them need help to be copied, moved or destroyed?
--- hint
Delete the copy constructor, the copy assignment operator and the destructor entirely. The compiler then writes all five special functions, member by member, and each member does its own job.
--- check test | A copy has every song
[] { Playlist a("road", {"one", "two"}); Playlist b = a; return b.name == "road" && b.songs == std::vector<std::string>{"one", "two"}; }()
--- check test | Assignment copies, and the copies are independent
[] { Playlist a("road", {"one"}); Playlist b("gym", {}); b = a; a.songs.push_back("three"); return b.name == "road" && b.songs.size() == 1; }()
--- check test | Playlist can really move
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Playlist> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Playlist>
--- check source absent | No hand-written destructor is left
~\s*Playlist

+++ practice | Taking parts off a shelf without copying
--- task
The starter's `Part` counts every copy in `Part::copies`, and its move functions copy nothing. Write two functions that move parts around without making a single copy. No `main`.

- `std::optional<Part> take(std::vector<Part>& shelf, const std::string& id)` finds the first part on `shelf` whose `id` matches, moves it out into the optional, removes its slot from the vector with `erase`, and returns it. If no part matches, it returns `std::nullopt` and leaves the shelf alone.
- `void move_all(std::vector<Part>& from, std::vector<Part>& to)` moves every part of `from`, in order, onto the end of `to`. Afterwards `from` is empty (call `clear()`: a moved-from vector's contents are not promised).

The starter's versions give the right parts but copy them, or leave `from` full.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Part {
    std::string id;
    static inline int copies = 0;

    explicit Part(std::string i) : id(std::move(i)) {}
    Part(const Part& o) : id(o.id) { ++copies; }
    Part(Part&& o) noexcept : id(std::move(o.id)) {}
    Part& operator=(const Part& o) { id = o.id; ++copies; return *this; }
    Part& operator=(Part&& o) noexcept { id = std::move(o.id); return *this; }
};

std::optional<Part> take(std::vector<Part>& shelf, const std::string& id) {
    for (auto it = shelf.begin(); it != shelf.end(); ++it) {
        if (it->id == id) {
            Part found = *it;
            shelf.erase(it);
            return found;
        }
    }
    return std::nullopt;
}

void move_all(std::vector<Part>& from, std::vector<Part>& to) {
    for (const Part& p : from) to.push_back(p);
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Part {
    std::string id;
    static inline int copies = 0;

    explicit Part(std::string i) : id(std::move(i)) {}
    Part(const Part& o) : id(o.id) { ++copies; }
    Part(Part&& o) noexcept : id(std::move(o.id)) {}
    Part& operator=(const Part& o) { id = o.id; ++copies; return *this; }
    Part& operator=(Part&& o) noexcept { id = std::move(o.id); return *this; }
};

std::optional<Part> take(std::vector<Part>& shelf, const std::string& id) {
    for (auto it = shelf.begin(); it != shelf.end(); ++it) {
        if (it->id == id) {
            std::optional<Part> found = std::move(*it);
            shelf.erase(it);
            return found;
        }
    }
    return std::nullopt;
}

void move_all(std::vector<Part>& from, std::vector<Part>& to) {
    for (Part& p : from) to.push_back(std::move(p));
    from.clear();
}
--- hint
`*it` names a part that lives on inside the vector, so `Part found = *it;` copies it. You are finished with that part, so say so with `std::move(*it)`.
--- hint
In `move_all`, loop with `Part& p` (not `const`, because you are going to steal from it) and `push_back(std::move(p))`. After the loop, `from.clear();`.
--- check test | take moves the first match out, with no copies
[] { std::vector<Part> shelf; shelf.push_back(Part("bolt")); shelf.push_back(Part("gear")); shelf.push_back(Part("gear")); Part::copies = 0; auto p = take(shelf, "gear"); return p && p->id == "gear" && shelf.size() == 2 && shelf[0].id == "bolt" && shelf[1].id == "gear" && Part::copies == 0; }()
--- check test | take with no match leaves the shelf alone
[] { std::vector<Part> shelf; shelf.push_back(Part("bolt")); Part::copies = 0; auto p = take(shelf, "nut"); std::vector<Part> none; auto q = take(none, "x"); return !p && !q && shelf.size() == 1 && Part::copies == 0; }()
--- check test | move_all moves in order and empties the source
[] { std::vector<Part> a, b; a.push_back(Part("x")); a.push_back(Part("y")); b.push_back(Part("w")); Part::copies = 0; move_all(a, b); return a.empty() && b.size() == 3 && b[0].id == "w" && b[1].id == "x" && b[2].id == "y" && Part::copies == 0; }()
--- check test | move_all from an empty vector
[] { std::vector<Part> a, b; b.push_back(Part("w")); move_all(a, b); return a.empty() && b.size() == 1; }()

+++ practice | Moves that keep the block count exact
--- task
Every heap block in this program goes through the starter's `alloc` and `release`, which keep the global `blocks_live` equal to the number of blocks that exist. `release(nullptr)` does nothing. `Block` already copies correctly. Give it a move constructor and a move assignment operator, both `noexcept`, and get every edge case right. No `main`.

- A move takes the source's pointer and size, and leaves the source with size 0 and `nullptr`. Moving makes no new block.
- Move assignment releases the target's own block first. Moving a block onto itself changes nothing.
- Moving from an already moved-from `Block` gives an empty one, and moving an empty one onto a full one releases the full one's block.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <utility>

int blocks_live = 0;
int* alloc(std::size_t n) { ++blocks_live; return new int[n](); }
void release(int* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    --blocks_live;
    delete[] p;
}

class Block {
public:
    explicit Block(std::size_t n) : size_(n), data_(alloc(n)) {}
    ~Block() { release(data_); }

    Block(const Block& o) : size_(o.size_), data_(alloc(o.size_)) { std::copy(o.data_, o.data_ + size_, data_); }
    Block& operator=(const Block& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            int* fresh = alloc(o.size_);
            std::copy(o.data_, o.data_ + o.size_, fresh);
            release(data_);
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = o.size_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int v) { data_[i] = v; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <utility>

int blocks_live = 0;
int* alloc(std::size_t n) { ++blocks_live; return new int[n](); }
void release(int* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    --blocks_live;
    delete[] p;
}

class Block {
public:
    explicit Block(std::size_t n) : size_(n), data_(alloc(n)) {}
    ~Block() { release(data_); }

    Block(const Block& o) : size_(o.size_), data_(alloc(o.size_)) { std::copy(o.data_, o.data_ + size_, data_); }
    Block& operator=(const Block& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            int* fresh = alloc(o.size_);
            std::copy(o.data_, o.data_ + o.size_, fresh);
            release(data_);
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = o.size_;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    Block(Block&& o) noexcept : size_(o.size_), data_(o.data_) {
        o.size_ = 0;
        o.data_ = nullptr;
    }

    Block& operator=(Block&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            release(data_);
            data_ = o.data_;
            size_ = o.size_;
            o.data_ = nullptr;
            o.size_ = 0;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, int v) { data_[i] = v; }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* data_;
};
--- hint
A moved-from `Block` holds `nullptr` and size 0. Every edge case follows from that one state: its destructor releases nothing, and moving from it hands over `nullptr` and 0.
--- hint
In move assignment, `release(data_)` comes first, inside `if (this != &o)`. Without the `if`, moving a block onto itself would release the very block it is about to keep.
--- check test | A move makes no new block and empties the source
[] { blocks_live = 0; Block a(3); a.set(2, 8); Block b = std::move(a); return blocks_live == 1 && b.size() == 3 && b.get(2) == 8 && a.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Move assignment releases the target's block
[] { blocks_live = 0; Block a(2); Block b(5); b = std::move(a); return blocks_live == 1 && b.size() == 2 && a.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Moving onto itself changes nothing
[] { blocks_live = 0; Block a(4); a.set(0, 6); Block& same = a; a = std::move(same); return blocks_live == 1 && a.size() == 4 && a.get(0) == 6; }()
--- check test | Moving from moved-from and empty blocks
[] { blocks_live = 0; Block a(2); Block b = std::move(a); Block c = std::move(a); Block d(7); d = std::move(c); return c.size() == 0 && d.size() == 0 && blocks_live == 1 && b.size() == 2; }()
--- check test | Everything is released at the end
[] { blocks_live = 0; { Block a(1); Block b(2); Block c = std::move(a); b = std::move(c); a = std::move(b); } return blocks_live == 0; }()

+++ practice | Debug: a vector full of copies
--- task
**Bug report:** "Filling a `std::vector<Frame>` with 100 frames made well over a hundred copies, although `Frame` has a move constructor. Every time the vector grows, it copies every frame."

`Frame` counts its copies in `Frame::copies` and its moves in `Frame::moves`. Its move functions steal correctly, and nothing is wrong with their bodies. Find why the vector refuses to use them and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Frame {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;
    static inline int moves = 0;

    explicit Frame(int id) : id_(id), px_(new int[16]()) {}
    ~Frame() { delete[] px_; }

    Frame(const Frame& o) : id_(o.id_), px_(new int[16]) {
        std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + 16, px_);
        ++copies;
    }
    Frame& operator=(const Frame& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + 16, px_);
            id_ = o.id_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    Frame(Frame&& o) : id_(o.id_), px_(o.px_) {
        o.px_ = nullptr;
        ++moves;
    }
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            delete[] px_;
            px_ = o.px_;
            id_ = o.id_;
            o.px_ = nullptr;
            ++moves;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    int id() const { return id_; }

private:
    int id_;
    int* px_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Frame {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;
    static inline int moves = 0;

    explicit Frame(int id) : id_(id), px_(new int[16]()) {}
    ~Frame() { delete[] px_; }

    Frame(const Frame& o) : id_(o.id_), px_(new int[16]) {
        std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + 16, px_);
        ++copies;
    }
    Frame& operator=(const Frame& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + 16, px_);
            id_ = o.id_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    Frame(Frame&& o) noexcept : id_(o.id_), px_(o.px_) {
        o.px_ = nullptr;
        ++moves;
    }
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            delete[] px_;
            px_ = o.px_;
            id_ = o.id_;
            o.px_ = nullptr;
            ++moves;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    int id() const { return id_; }

private:
    int id_;
    int* px_;
};
--- hint
A growing `std::vector` only moves its elements when moving is promised never to fail. Otherwise it copies them to stay safe.
--- hint
The promise is one word, written after the parameter list of each move function: `noexcept`.
--- check test | 100 frames go in with no copies at all
[] { Frame::copies = 0; std::vector<Frame> v; for (int i = 0; i < 100; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(i)); return Frame::copies == 0 && v.size() == 100; }()
--- check test | The frames survive the growing, in order
[] { std::vector<Frame> v; for (int i = 0; i < 40; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(i)); bool ok = true; for (int i = 0; i < 40; ++i) ok = ok && v[i].id() == i; return ok; }()
--- check test | Moving is promised never to fail
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Frame> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Frame>

+++ practice | Stretch: double buffering with no copies
--- task
Games and flight displays draw the next picture into a hidden **back** buffer while the screen shows the **front** one, then swap them. The starter's `Frame` is complete: it counts copies in `Frame::copies` and moves in `Frame::moves`, and a moved-from `Frame` is empty.

The starter's `class Renderer` has two `Frame` members, `front_` and `back_`, and none of the five special functions of its own (the rule of zero), which is right. No `main`.

- `explicit Renderer(std::size_t pixels)` makes both frames with `pixels` pixels each.
- `void draw(int value)` fills the **back** frame with `value`.
- `void present()` swaps the front and back frames.
- `const Frame& front() const` returns the front frame.

After `draw(5); present();` every pixel of `front()` is 5. The starter's `present` gives the right picture, but it copies three whole frames to do it. Rewrite `present` so that it swaps the frames with exactly three moves and no copies, and leave the rest of `Renderer` as it is.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <utility>

class Frame {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;
    static inline int moves = 0;

    explicit Frame(std::size_t n) : size_(n), px_(new int[n]()) {}
    ~Frame() { delete[] px_; }
    Frame(const Frame& o) : size_(o.size_), px_(new int[o.size_]) {
        std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + size_, px_);
        ++copies;
    }
    Frame& operator=(const Frame& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            int* fresh = new int[o.size_];
            std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + o.size_, fresh);
            delete[] px_;
            px_ = fresh;
            size_ = o.size_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }
    Frame(Frame&& o) noexcept : size_(o.size_), px_(o.px_) {
        o.size_ = 0;
        o.px_ = nullptr;
        ++moves;
    }
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            delete[] px_;
            px_ = o.px_;
            size_ = o.size_;
            o.px_ = nullptr;
            o.size_ = 0;
            ++moves;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return px_[i]; }
    void fill(int v) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < size_; ++i) px_[i] = v;
    }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* px_;
};

class Renderer {
public:
    explicit Renderer(std::size_t pixels) : front_(pixels), back_(pixels) {}
    void draw(int value) { back_.fill(value); }
    void present() {
        Frame tmp = front_;
        front_ = back_;
        back_ = tmp;
    }
    const Frame& front() const { return front_; }

private:
    Frame front_;
    Frame back_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <utility>

class Frame {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;
    static inline int moves = 0;

    explicit Frame(std::size_t n) : size_(n), px_(new int[n]()) {}
    ~Frame() { delete[] px_; }
    Frame(const Frame& o) : size_(o.size_), px_(new int[o.size_]) {
        std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + size_, px_);
        ++copies;
    }
    Frame& operator=(const Frame& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            int* fresh = new int[o.size_];
            std::copy(o.px_, o.px_ + o.size_, fresh);
            delete[] px_;
            px_ = fresh;
            size_ = o.size_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }
    Frame(Frame&& o) noexcept : size_(o.size_), px_(o.px_) {
        o.size_ = 0;
        o.px_ = nullptr;
        ++moves;
    }
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            delete[] px_;
            px_ = o.px_;
            size_ = o.size_;
            o.px_ = nullptr;
            o.size_ = 0;
            ++moves;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int get(std::size_t i) const { return px_[i]; }
    void fill(int v) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < size_; ++i) px_[i] = v;
    }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    int* px_;
};

class Renderer {
public:
    explicit Renderer(std::size_t pixels) : front_(pixels), back_(pixels) {}
    void draw(int value) { back_.fill(value); }
    void present() {
        Frame tmp = std::move(front_);
        front_ = std::move(back_);
        back_ = std::move(tmp);
    }
    const Frame& front() const { return front_; }

private:
    Frame front_;
    Frame back_;
};
--- hint
Each line of the starter's `present` copies, because `front_`, `back_` and `tmp` are all named objects that live on. But at every step you are finished with the value you read from.
--- hint
Wrap each source in `std::move`: `Frame tmp = std::move(front_);`, then `front_ = std::move(back_);`, then `back_ = std::move(tmp);`. That is one move construction and two move assignments.
--- check test | present shows what was drawn
[] { Renderer r(4); r.draw(5); r.present(); bool shown = r.front().get(0) == 5 && r.front().get(3) == 5; r.draw(9); bool still = r.front().get(0) == 5; r.present(); return shown && still && r.front().get(2) == 9; }()
--- check test | present makes three moves and no copies
[] { Renderer r(8); r.draw(1); Frame::copies = 0; Frame::moves = 0; r.present(); r.present(); return Frame::copies == 0 && Frame::moves == 6; }()
--- check test | Both frames keep their size through a swap
[] { Renderer r(6); r.present(); r.present(); r.present(); return r.front().size() == 6; }()
--- check test | Renderer copies and moves member by member
[] { Renderer a(3); a.draw(2); a.present(); Frame::copies = 0; Frame::moves = 0; Renderer b = a; bool copied = Frame::copies == 2; Renderer c = std::move(b); return copied && Frame::moves == 2 && Frame::copies == 2 && c.front().get(1) == 2; }()

=== cpp3-04 | Smart pointers: unique_ptr and shared_ptr
--- teach
Last lesson ended with the rule of zero: build your classes from members that already look after themselves. Writing the rule of five for every owning type would be tiring. Smart pointers are those ready-made members for single objects on the heap: RAII, already written. You met `std::unique_ptr` in the basics course's pointers lesson. Now you will use it to build a tree, and meet its partner for sharing, `std::shared_ptr`. Both need `#include <memory>`.

### std::unique_ptr: exactly one owner

**`std::unique_ptr<T>`** has exactly one owner. It deletes its object when it goes away. It cannot be copied, only moved: its copy functions are deleted, like your `FileHandle`'s, and it has the move functions from last lesson. This is the smart pointer to reach for first.

```cpp
auto p = std::make_unique<Node>();        // p owns a new Node
std::unique_ptr<Node> q = std::move(p);   // ownership moves to q; p is now null
```

`std::make_unique<Node>()` makes a new `Node` on the heap, with its members set to zero or empty, and hands back a `unique_ptr` that owns it. After the move, `q` owns the `Node` and `p` holds `nullptr`. To reach a member through the pointer, use the arrow from the intermediate course's maps lesson: `q->value` means "follow `q`, then take `value`".

### A tree of owners

Picture a spacecraft's parts list. The spacecraft has subsystems; each subsystem has parts; some parts have smaller parts. That shape is a **[[tree|tree-words]]**: each item is a **node**, the top one is the **root**, the nodes hanging under a node are its **children**, and a node with no children is a **leaf**.

Trees are the classic fit for `unique_ptr`, because each parent owns its children:

```cpp
struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};
```

Read `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>>` from the outside in: a vector of unique pointers, each owning one `Node`. Each child is a `Node`, which has its own `children`, and so on down.

Destroying the root destroys the whole tree, with no cleanup code at all. The root's destructor destroys its vector; the vector destroys each `unique_ptr`; each `unique_ptr` deletes its child; and each child does the same for its own children. RAII, all the way down.

### Adding a child

```cpp
Node root{1, {}};                                   // value 1, no children yet
root.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());  // add one child
Node& kid = *root.children.back();                  // the new child
kid.value = 2;
```

- `Node root{1, {}}` fills the members in order: `value` is 1, and the empty braces `{}` give an empty `children` vector.
- `push_back` is handed a temporary `unique_ptr`, so it moves it in. No copy is needed, which is lucky, since a `unique_ptr` cannot be copied.
- `.back()` gives the **last** element of a vector: here, the `unique_ptr` that was added last. The `*` in front follows it to the `Node` it owns.
- `Node& kid` is a reference to that node, not a copy, so setting `kid.value` changes the child inside the tree.

### Walking a tree with recursion

The basics course's recursion lesson showed a function that calls itself on a smaller problem, with a base case that stops it. A tree is made for this, because its shape is itself recursive: every child is the root of a smaller tree.

To count the nodes in a tree, count this node, then add the count for each child's tree:

```cpp
int count_nodes(const Node& n) {
    int total = 1;                                  // this node
    for (const auto& child : n.children) {
        total += count_nodes(*child);               // each child's whole tree
    }
    return total;
}
```

- `*child` turns a child's `unique_ptr` into the `Node&` it owns, which is what `count_nodes` takes.
- The base case needs no `if`. A leaf has no children, so the loop runs zero times and the function returns 1. That is where the [[recursion stops|recursion-depth]].
- For the root in the example above, with one child, `count_nodes(root)` is 2.

Adding the children's answers suits a sum. Some questions need the **largest** of the children's answers instead. `std::max(a, b)`, from `<algorithm>`, gives the larger of two values: `std::max(3, 8)` is 8.

**Watch out:** in the loop, write `const auto& child`, with the `&`. Plain `auto child` would try to copy each `unique_ptr` out of the vector, and copying one is deleted, so the program does not compile. The error mentions a deleted function, the same kind of error as copying a `FileHandle` in the RAII lesson.

### std::shared_ptr: many owners, one count

Some things have no single owner. Picture a set of game sprites that all draw the same grass picture. Loading the picture once and sharing it is the sensible thing; but which sprite should delete it? The answer: whichever is last.

**`std::shared_ptr<T>`** is shared ownership. It keeps a **[[reference count|ref-count]]**: how many `shared_ptr`s currently point at the object. Copying a `shared_ptr` adds one to the count, and each one that goes away takes one off. The object is deleted when the **last** `shared_ptr` to it goes away. `use_count()` tells you the count.

```cpp
auto tex = std::make_shared<Texture>(Texture{"grass.png"});   // count 1
auto a = tex;          // count 2
auto b = tex;          // count 3
```

- `std::make_shared` makes the object and the `shared_ptr` that owns it, like `make_unique`. `Texture{"grass.png"}` builds a `Texture` with its `file` member set, and `make_shared` places that value in the new shared object.
- `auto a = tex;` copies the pointer, **not** the texture. There is still one `Texture`; now three pointers share it, and `tex.use_count()` is 3. The arrow works here too: `b->file` is `"grass.png"`.

Two cautions. Shared ownership makes it hard to tell when an object will die, so do not reach for `shared_ptr` by default; use it when there genuinely is no single owner. And two objects holding `shared_ptr`s to **each other** never reach a count of zero: a [[leak that never ends|cycles]]. You break such cycles with `std::weak_ptr`, which watches an object without owning it.

### Owning is not the same as using

Code that only *uses* an object, like `count_nodes`, takes a plain reference or raw pointer: `const Node&` or `Node*`. It does not care who owns the node. [[Ownership is the smart pointer's job|owner-vs-user]], not every function's.

::: context tree-words Trees grow upside down
Programmers draw trees with the root at the top and the leaves at the bottom, the opposite of a real tree. Trees are everywhere in software: the folders on a disk, the parts of a web page, a game's scene, and the long lists of parts that make up a spacecraft. In this picture the root has two children, one of them has a child of its own, and the longest path from the root down passes through 3 nodes.

```svg

  <circle cx="180" cy="35" r="16" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

  <text x="45" y="90" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">child</text>
  <text x="260" y="140" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">orange: leaves</text>

```

The values in it add up to 1 + 2 + 3 + 10 = 16.
:::

::: context recursion-depth How deep can it go
Each waiting call uses some of the call stack, as the basics course's recursion lesson showed. A tree a few levels deep is no problem. A tree shaped like a long chain, a million nodes each with one child, could run out of stack. This is one reason NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" rules for safety-critical code forbid recursion altogether: flight code must be able to prove how much stack it will ever need. Code written to rules like these walks its trees with loops instead. For everyday programs, recursion on trees is the clearest way to write it.
:::

::: context ref-count Counting the owners
The count lives in a small extra block that `make_shared` creates next to the object, called the control block. Every `shared_ptr` to the object points at it too. Copying a `shared_ptr` adds one; destroying or reassigning one takes one away; the one that brings it to zero deletes the object. The counting is made safe for programs that run several tasks at once, which makes copying a `shared_ptr` a little slower than copying a raw pointer. A `unique_ptr` has no count at all, and costs no more than a raw pointer.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="15" width="70" height="26" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="55" y="33" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">tex</text>
  <rect x="20" y="52" width="70" height="26" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="55" y="70" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">a</text>
  <rect x="20" y="89" width="70" height="26" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="55" y="107" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">b</text>
  <rect x="190" y="40" width="150" height="50" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="265" y="60" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">count: 3</text>
  <text x="265" y="78" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Texture "grass.png"</text>
  <line x1="90" y1="28" x2="186" y2="58" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="90" y1="65" x2="186" y2="65" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="90" y1="102" x2="186" y2="72" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="265" y="115" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">one object, three owners</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context cycles Two owners holding each other
Picture two objects, A and B. A holds a `shared_ptr` to B, and B holds one to A. When the rest of the program lets go of both, each still has a count of 1, because the other one is holding it. Neither count reaches zero, so neither is ever deleted. A `std::weak_ptr` points at an object without adding to its count. When you need the object, its `lock()` member gives you a `shared_ptr` to it, or an empty one if the object is already gone. So a child can point back at its parent with a `weak_ptr` and never keep it alive by accident.
:::

::: context owner-vs-user Borrowing, not owning
The C++ Core Guidelines, a set of rules written by Bjarne Stroustrup and Herb Sutter with many others, put it this way: for general use, take `T*` or `T&` arguments rather than smart pointers. A function that takes `const Node&` works for a node owned by a `unique_ptr`, a `shared_ptr`, or a plain local variable, and it cannot accidentally change who owns it. Take a smart pointer as a parameter only when the function really does take part in ownership, such as storing it or handing it on.
:::
--- task
The starter declares `Node` (a `value` and a vector of `children`) and `Texture` (one member, `file`). Write these five functions, with no `main` (the checker supplies it):

- `std::unique_ptr<Node> make_node(int value)`: a new node holding `value`, with no children.
- `Node& add_child(Node& parent, int value)`: makes a new node holding `value`, appends it to `parent`'s children, and returns a reference to the new child (so you can add grandchildren to it).
- `int tree_sum(const Node& root)`: the sum of every value in the tree.
- `int tree_depth(const Node& root)`: the number of nodes on the longest path from the root down to a leaf. A lone node has depth 1.
- `std::vector<std::shared_ptr<Texture>> share_texture(const std::string& file, int n)`: `n` pointers that all share **one** new `Texture` whose `file` is `file`. If `n` is 0 or less, return an empty vector.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

struct Texture {
    std::string file;
};

std::unique_ptr<Node> make_node(int value) {
    return nullptr;
}

Node& add_child(Node& parent, int value) {
    return parent;
}

int tree_sum(const Node& root) {
    return root.value;
}

int tree_depth(const Node& root) {
    return 1;
}

std::vector<std::shared_ptr<Texture>> share_texture(const std::string& file, int n) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

struct Texture {
    std::string file;
};

std::unique_ptr<Node> make_node(int value) {
    auto node = std::make_unique<Node>();
    node->value = value;
    return node;
}

Node& add_child(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(make_node(value));
    return *parent.children.back();
}

int tree_sum(const Node& root) {
    int total = root.value;
    for (const auto& child : root.children) total += tree_sum(*child);
    return total;
}

int tree_depth(const Node& root) {
    int deepest = 0;
    for (const auto& child : root.children) deepest = std::max(deepest, tree_depth(*child));
    return deepest + 1;
}

std::vector<std::shared_ptr<Texture>> share_texture(const std::string& file, int n) {
    std::vector<std::shared_ptr<Texture>> out;
    if (n <= 0) return out;
    auto tex = std::make_shared<Texture>(Texture{file});
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) out.push_back(tex);
    return out;
}
--- hint
`make_node`: make the node with `std::make_unique<Node>()`, set its value through the arrow (`node->value = value;`), and return it. `add_child` can reuse it: `parent.children.push_back(make_node(value));`, then return the new child with `*parent.children.back()`.
--- hint
`tree_sum` and `tree_depth` are recursive, like `count_nodes` in the explanation: loop over `const auto& child : root.children` and call the same function on `*child`. For the sum, start at `root.value` and add each child's sum. For the depth, keep the largest child depth with `std::max`, then return it plus 1 for this node.
--- hint
`share_texture`: if `n <= 0`, return an empty vector. Otherwise make **one** texture with `std::make_shared<Texture>(Texture{file})`, then `push_back` that same `shared_ptr` `n` times in a loop. Copying it shares the texture; it does not make a new one.
--- check test | make_node makes a leaf
[] { auto n = make_node(4); return n != nullptr && n->value == 4 && n->children.empty(); }()
--- check test | Building a tree and summing it
[] { auto root = make_node(1); Node& a = add_child(*root, 2); add_child(*root, 3); add_child(a, 10); return tree_sum(*root) == 16 && root->children.size() == 2; }()
--- check test | Depth counts the longest path
[] { auto root = make_node(0); Node& a = add_child(*root, 0); Node& b = add_child(a, 0); add_child(b, 0); add_child(*root, 0); return tree_depth(*root) == 4 && tree_depth(b) == 2; }()
--- check test | A lone node has depth 1 and its own value as sum
[] { auto n = make_node(-5); return tree_depth(*n) == 1 && tree_sum(*n) == -5; }()
--- check test | One texture, shared three ways
[] { auto v = share_texture("grass.png", 3); return v.size() == 3 && v[0].get() == v[2].get() && v[0].use_count() == 3 && v[1]->file == "grass.png"; }()
--- check test | Sharing zero times makes nothing
share_texture("x.png", 0).empty()

+++ practice | Leaves and the largest value
--- task
The starter declares the tree `Node` from the lesson (a `value` and a vector of `unique_ptr` children) and a helper `add(parent, value)` that adds a child and returns it. Write two recursive functions. No `main`.

- `int count_leaves(const Node& root)` returns how many nodes have no children. A lone node is one leaf.
- `int max_value(const Node& root)` returns the largest value anywhere in the tree. Values may be negative.

For a root 5 with children 9 and -2, where 9 has one child 12: `count_leaves` is 2 (the 12 and the -2) and `max_value` is 12.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

Node& add(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());
    parent.children.back()->value = value;
    return *parent.children.back();
}

int count_leaves(const Node& root) {
    return 1;
}

int max_value(const Node& root) {
    return root.value;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
