    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

Node& add(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());
    parent.children.back()->value = value;
    return *parent.children.back();
}

int count_leaves(const Node& root) {
    if (root.children.empty()) return 1;
    int leaves = 0;
    for (const auto& child : root.children) leaves += count_leaves(*child);
    return leaves;
}

int max_value(const Node& root) {
    int best = root.value;
    for (const auto& child : root.children) best = std::max(best, max_value(*child));
    return best;
}
--- hint
A leaf is the base case of `count_leaves`: a node with no children counts 1. Any other node's answer is the sum of its children's answers.
--- hint
For `max_value`, start from `root.value`, and for each child keep `std::max` of what you have and `max_value(*child)`. Loop with `const auto& child`, because a `unique_ptr` cannot be copied.
--- check test | The example tree
[] { Node root{5, {}}; Node& nine = add(root, 9); add(root, -2); add(nine, 12); return count_leaves(root) == 2 && max_value(root) == 12; }()
--- check test | A lone node
[] { Node n{-7, {}}; return count_leaves(n) == 1 && max_value(n) == -7; }()
--- check test | All negative, and the root is the largest
[] { Node root{-1, {}}; add(root, -5); add(add(root, -3), -9); return max_value(root) == -1 && count_leaves(root) == 2; }()
--- check test | A wide tree
[] { Node root{0, {}}; for (int i = 1; i <= 6; ++i) add(root, i * 10); return count_leaves(root) == 6 && max_value(root) == 60; }()

+++ practice | One level of the tree
--- task
With the same `Node` and `add` helper as before, write `std::vector<int> at_depth(const Node& root, int depth)`. It returns the values of every node exactly `depth` steps below `root`, from left to right: the order they appear when you go through the children in order, and each child's children in order. `at_depth(root, 0)` is just the root's value. If `depth` is negative, or deeper than the tree goes, return an empty vector. No `main`.

For a root 1 with children 2 and 3, where 2 has children 4 and 5 and 3 has child 6: `at_depth(root, 1)` is `{2, 3}` and `at_depth(root, 2)` is `{4, 5, 6}`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

Node& add(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());
    parent.children.back()->value = value;
    return *parent.children.back();
}

std::vector<int> at_depth(const Node& root, int depth) {
    return {root.value};
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

Node& add(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());
    parent.children.back()->value = value;
    return *parent.children.back();
}

void collect(const Node& node, int depth, std::vector<int>& out) {
    if (depth == 0) {
        out.push_back(node.value);
        return;
    }
    for (const auto& child : node.children) collect(*child, depth - 1, out);
}

std::vector<int> at_depth(const Node& root, int depth) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    if (depth >= 0) collect(root, depth, out);
    return out;
}
--- hint
Write a helper that takes the vector to fill by reference: `void collect(const Node& node, int depth, std::vector<int>& out)`. Each step down into a child makes the remaining depth one smaller.
--- hint
The base case is `depth == 0`: push this node's value and stop. Otherwise call `collect(*child, depth - 1, out)` for each child in order. `at_depth` makes the empty vector, calls `collect` only when `depth >= 0`, and returns it.
--- check case | Depth 2 of the example
[] { Node r{1, {}}; Node& a = add(r, 2); Node& b = add(r, 3); add(a, 4); add(a, 5); add(b, 6); return at_depth(r, 2); }()
=> std::vector<int>{4, 5, 6}
--- check case | Depth 1 and depth 0
[] { Node r{1, {}}; add(r, 2); add(r, 3); std::vector<int> both = at_depth(r, 0); for (int v : at_depth(r, 1)) both.push_back(v); return both; }()
=> std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3}
--- check case | Deeper than the tree
[] { Node r{1, {}}; add(r, 2); return at_depth(r, 5); }()
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | A negative depth
[] { Node r{8, {}}; return at_depth(r, -1); }()
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | A hangar that owns its craft
--- task
Write `class Hangar`, which owns spacecraft through a private `std::map<std::string, std::unique_ptr<Craft>>`, keyed by each craft's name. The starter's `Craft` counts how many craft exist in `Craft::alive`. No `main`.

- `bool park(std::unique_ptr<Craft> craft)` stores the craft under its name and returns `true`. If `craft` is `nullptr`, or a craft with that name is already parked, it returns `false` and stores nothing (the rejected craft is destroyed when the function ends).
- `std::unique_ptr<Craft> release(const std::string& name)` takes the named craft out of the hangar and hands ownership to the caller, removing its key (`bays_.erase(it)` removes the entry an iterator `it` points at). If there is no such craft, it returns `nullptr`.
- `int count() const` is how many craft are parked, and `std::vector<std::string> names() const` lists their names in alphabetical order.

When a `Hangar` dies, every craft still parked in it dies too.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Craft {
    std::string name;
    static inline int alive = 0;
    explicit Craft(std::string n) : name(std::move(n)) { ++alive; }
    ~Craft() { --alive; }
};

class Hangar {
public:
    bool park(std::unique_ptr<Craft> craft) { return false; }
    std::unique_ptr<Craft> release(const std::string& name) { return nullptr; }
    int count() const { return 0; }
    std::vector<std::string> names() const { return {}; }
};
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Craft {
    std::string name;
    static inline int alive = 0;
    explicit Craft(std::string n) : name(std::move(n)) { ++alive; }
    ~Craft() { --alive; }
};

class Hangar {
public:
    bool park(std::unique_ptr<Craft> craft) {
        if (!craft || bays_.count(craft->name) > 0) return false;
        std::string name = craft->name;
        bays_[name] = std::move(craft);
        return true;
    }

    std::unique_ptr<Craft> release(const std::string& name) {
        auto it = bays_.find(name);
        if (it == bays_.end()) return nullptr;
        std::unique_ptr<Craft> out = std::move(it->second);
        bays_.erase(it);
        return out;
    }

    int count() const { return static_cast<int>(bays_.size()); }

    std::vector<std::string> names() const {
        std::vector<std::string> out;
        for (const auto& [name, craft] : bays_) out.push_back(name);
        return out;
    }

private:
    std::map<std::string, std::unique_ptr<Craft>> bays_;
};
--- hint
A `unique_ptr` can only be moved, so storing it is `bays_[name] = std::move(craft);`. Copy the name into a local first: after the move, `craft` is null and `craft->name` can no longer be read.
--- hint
`release` finds the entry with `bays_.find(name)`. If found, move `it->second` into a local `unique_ptr`, `erase(it)`, and return the local.
--- check test | Parking and counting
[] { Craft::alive = 0; Hangar h; bool a = h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("vega")); bool b = h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("apex")); return a && b && h.count() == 2 && Craft::alive == 2 && h.names() == std::vector<std::string>{"apex", "vega"}; }()
--- check test | A taken name or nullptr is refused, and the rejected craft dies
[] { Craft::alive = 0; Hangar h; h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("vega")); bool dup = h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("vega")); bool none = h.park(nullptr); return !dup && !none && h.count() == 1 && Craft::alive == 1; }()
--- check test | release hands ownership to the caller
[] { Craft::alive = 0; Hangar h; h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("vega")); std::unique_ptr<Craft> c = h.release("vega"); bool out = c && c->name == "vega" && h.count() == 0 && Craft::alive == 1; c.reset(); return out && Craft::alive == 0 && h.release("vega") == nullptr; }()
--- check test | Craft still parked die with the hangar
[] { Craft::alive = 0; { Hangar h; h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("a")); h.park(std::make_unique<Craft>("b")); } return Craft::alive == 0; }()

+++ practice | A texture cache that shares
--- task
Write `class TextureCache`, which loads each picture once and shares it. It keeps a private `std::map<std::string, std::shared_ptr<Texture>>`. No `main`.

- `std::shared_ptr<Texture> get(const std::string& file)` returns the shared texture for `file`. The first time a file is asked for, it makes a new `Texture` with that `file` and keeps it in the map. Later calls for the same file return the **same** texture. For an empty `file`, return `nullptr` and load nothing.
- `int loaded() const` is how many different textures the cache holds.
- `long users(const std::string& file) const` is how many `shared_ptr`s **outside** the cache point at that texture: its `use_count()` minus the one the cache holds. For a file never loaded, it is 0.

After `auto a = cache.get("sky.png"); auto b = cache.get("sky.png");`, `a` and `b` point at one texture, `loaded()` is 1 and `users("sky.png")` is 2.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>

struct Texture {
    std::string file;
};

class TextureCache {
public:
    std::shared_ptr<Texture> get(const std::string& file) {
        return std::make_shared<Texture>(Texture{file});
    }
    int loaded() const { return 0; }
    long users(const std::string& file) const { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>

struct Texture {
    std::string file;
};

class TextureCache {
public:
    std::shared_ptr<Texture> get(const std::string& file) {
        if (file.empty()) return nullptr;
        auto it = cache_.find(file);
        if (it != cache_.end()) return it->second;
        auto tex = std::make_shared<Texture>(Texture{file});
        cache_[file] = tex;
        return tex;
    }

    int loaded() const { return static_cast<int>(cache_.size()); }

    long users(const std::string& file) const {
        auto it = cache_.find(file);
        if (it == cache_.end()) return 0;
        return it->second.use_count() - 1;
    }

private:
    std::map<std::string, std::shared_ptr<Texture>> cache_;
};
--- hint
Look the file up with `cache_.find(file)`. If it is there, return the stored `shared_ptr`: returning it copies the pointer, not the texture. Otherwise make one with `std::make_shared`, store it, and return it.
--- hint
`users` finds the entry and returns `it->second.use_count() - 1`, because the map's own copy counts as one. Use `find`, not `[ ]`: this function is `const`, and it must not add a key.
--- check test | The same file gives the same texture
[] { TextureCache c; auto a = c.get("sky.png"); auto b = c.get("sky.png"); return a == b && a->file == "sky.png" && c.loaded() == 1 && c.users("sky.png") == 2; }()
--- check test | Users drop as pointers go away
[] { TextureCache c; auto a = c.get("sky.png"); { auto b = c.get("sky.png"); } long one = c.users("sky.png"); a = nullptr; return one == 1 && c.users("sky.png") == 0 && c.loaded() == 1; }()
--- check test | Different files, and a file never loaded
[] { TextureCache c; auto a = c.get("a.png"); auto b = c.get("b.png"); return a != b && c.loaded() == 2 && c.users("zzz.png") == 0 && c.loaded() == 2; }()
--- check test | An empty name loads nothing
[] { TextureCache c; auto e = c.get(""); return e == nullptr && c.loaded() == 0 && c.users("") == 0; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the detached child that stays behind
--- task
**Bug report:** "After `detach(root, 0)`, `root.children.size()` is still 2, and `count_nodes(root)` crashes."

`detach(parent, i)` should take the child at index `i` out of `parent`, hand it to the caller as a `std::unique_ptr<Node>`, and remove its slot, so the parent is left with one child fewer. The detached child keeps its own children. `i` is always a valid index. Fix `detach`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <memory>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

Node& add(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());
    parent.children.back()->value = value;
    return *parent.children.back();
}

int count_nodes(const Node& n) {
    int total = 1;
    for (const auto& child : n.children) total += count_nodes(*child);
    return total;
}

std::unique_ptr<Node> detach(Node& parent, std::size_t i) {
    std::unique_ptr<Node> out = std::move(parent.children[i]);
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <memory>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Node {
    int value;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> children;
};

Node& add(Node& parent, int value) {
    parent.children.push_back(std::make_unique<Node>());
    parent.children.back()->value = value;
    return *parent.children.back();
}

int count_nodes(const Node& n) {
    int total = 1;
    for (const auto& child : n.children) total += count_nodes(*child);
    return total;
}

std::unique_ptr<Node> detach(Node& parent, std::size_t i) {
    std::unique_ptr<Node> out = std::move(parent.children[i]);
    parent.children.erase(parent.children.begin() + i);
    return out;
}
--- hint
Moving out of a `unique_ptr` leaves it holding `nullptr`, but the slot is still in the vector. What does `count_nodes` do when it reaches a null child?
--- hint
After the move, remove the slot with `erase`. The position of index `i` is `parent.children.begin() + i`.
--- check test | The parent has one child fewer
[] { Node root{0, {}}; add(root, 1); add(root, 2); auto kid = detach(root, 0); return root.children.size() == 1 && root.children[0]->value == 2 && kid->value == 1; }()
--- check test | count_nodes works on both halves
[] { Node root{0, {}}; Node& a = add(root, 1); add(a, 11); add(a, 12); add(root, 2); auto kid = detach(root, 0); return count_nodes(root) == 2 && count_nodes(*kid) == 3; }()
--- check test | Detaching the last child
[] { Node root{0, {}}; add(root, 1); add(root, 2); add(root, 3); auto kid = detach(root, 2); return kid->value == 3 && count_nodes(root) == 3 && root.children.back()->value == 2; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: a spacecraft parts list
--- task
A spacecraft is a tree of parts. Each `Part` has a name, its own mass in kilograms, and the smaller parts attached to it, owned through `unique_ptr`. The starter's `attach(parent, name, mass)` adds a part and returns it. Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int total_mass(const Part& p)` is `p`'s own mass plus the total mass of everything attached below it.
- `const Part* find(const Part& root, const std::string& name)` returns a pointer to the part with that name, or `nullptr` if there is none. Search the part itself first, then its parts in order, each one's whole subtree before the next; the first match wins.
- `std::vector<std::string> report(const Part& root)` returns one line per part, in that same order. Each line is two spaces for every level below the root, then the name, a space and the part's **total** mass.

For a `rover` of 100 kg with an `arm` of 20 kg (which holds a `drill` of 5 kg) and `wheels` of 40 kg, `report` is:

```
rover 165
  arm 25
    drill 5
  wheels 40
```
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Part {
    std::string name;
    int mass;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>> parts;
};

Part& attach(Part& parent, const std::string& name, int mass) {
    parent.parts.push_back(std::make_unique<Part>());
    Part& p = *parent.parts.back();
    p.name = name;
    p.mass = mass;
    return p;
}

int total_mass(const Part& p) {
    return p.mass;
}

const Part* find(const Part& root, const std::string& name) {
    return nullptr;
}

std::vector<std::string> report(const Part& root) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Part {
    std::string name;
    int mass;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>> parts;
};

Part& attach(Part& parent, const std::string& name, int mass) {
    parent.parts.push_back(std::make_unique<Part>());
    Part& p = *parent.parts.back();
    p.name = name;
    p.mass = mass;
    return p;
}

int total_mass(const Part& p) {
    int total = p.mass;
    for (const auto& child : p.parts) total += total_mass(*child);
    return total;
}

const Part* find(const Part& root, const std::string& name) {
    if (root.name == name) return &root;
    for (const auto& child : root.parts) {
        if (const Part* hit = find(*child, name)) return hit;
    }
    return nullptr;
}

void report_into(const Part& p, int depth, std::vector<std::string>& lines) {
    std::string indent;
    for (int i = 0; i < depth; ++i) indent += "  ";
    lines.push_back(indent + p.name + " " + std::to_string(total_mass(p)));
    for (const auto& child : p.parts) report_into(*child, depth + 1, lines);
}

std::vector<std::string> report(const Part& root) {
    std::vector<std::string> lines;
    report_into(root, 0, lines);
    return lines;
}
--- hint
`find` checks `root.name` first and returns `&root` on a match. Otherwise it asks each child in turn, and returns the first answer that is not `nullptr`.
--- hint
For `report`, write a recursive helper that also takes the depth and the vector of lines by reference. Build the indent with a loop that adds `"  "` once per level, then push `indent + name + " " + std::to_string(total_mass(p))`, then call the helper on each child with `depth + 1`.
--- check case | The rover report
[] { Part rover{"rover", 100, {}}; Part& arm = attach(rover, "arm", 20); attach(arm, "drill", 5); attach(rover, "wheels", 40); return report(rover); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"rover 165", "  arm 25", "    drill 5", "  wheels 40"}
--- check test | total_mass of the whole and of a leaf
[] { Part rover{"rover", 100, {}}; Part& arm = attach(rover, "arm", 20); Part& drill = attach(arm, "drill", 5); return total_mass(rover) == 125 && total_mass(drill) == 5; }()
--- check test | find returns a pointer into the tree, or nullptr
[] { Part rover{"rover", 100, {}}; Part& arm = attach(rover, "arm", 20); Part& drill = attach(arm, "drill", 5); return find(rover, "drill") == &drill && find(rover, "rover") == &rover && find(rover, "laser") == nullptr && find(arm, "rover") == nullptr; }()
--- check test | find takes the first match, depth first
[] { Part root{"sat", 1, {}}; Part& a = attach(root, "boom", 2); Part& deep = attach(a, "panel", 3); attach(root, "panel", 4); return find(root, "panel") == &deep; }()
--- check case | A lone part
[] { Part p{"cube", 7, {}}; return report(p); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"cube 7"}

=== cpp3-05 | Function templates
--- teach
Last lesson, `std::make_unique<Node>()` and `std::shared_ptr<Texture>` both took a type in angle brackets `< >`. You have been filling in types like that since the basics course: `std::vector<int>`, `std::map<std::string, int>`. Each of those is a **template**: a pattern the compiler completes with the type you hand it. This lesson and the next show you how to write your own. This one is about functions.

### The same function, three times

Say you need the largest number in a list of `int`s:

```cpp
int largest(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    int best = v[0];
    for (const int& x : v) if (best < x) best = x;
    return best;
}
```

Then you need it for a list of `double`s. Then for a list of `std::string`s, where the "largest" string is the one that comes last in dictionary order. Each time you would copy the function and change the type in three places. The steps never change; only the type does. And copies drift apart: fix a bug in one, and the others keep it.

### A function with a blank for the type

Picture a form letter: "Dear ______, your parcel has shipped." You write the letter once and fill in the blank for each person. A **function template** is a function written once, with a blank where the type goes:

```cpp
template <typename T>
T largest(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    T best = v[0];
    for (const T& x : v) if (best < x) best = x;
    return best;
}
```

Read the first line as "a template, for any type T". Word by word:

- `template` says that what follows is a pattern, not yet a real function.
- The angle brackets `< >` hold the **template parameters**: the blanks.
- `typename T` makes one blank, named `T`, that stands for [[a type|typename-word]]. `T` is only a name, the way a function's parameters have names. Calling it `T`, for "type", is the custom.

After that line, write `T` everywhere the type would go: the vector's element type, the local variable `best`, the loop variable and the return type.

### The compiler fills in the blank

You call a template like any other function:

```cpp
largest(std::vector<int>{3, 8, 1});            // 8: T is int
largest(std::vector<std::string>{"b", "a"});   // "b": T is std::string
```

The compiler looks at the argument, sees a `std::vector<int>`, and works out that `T` must be `int`. Working out the type from the arguments is called **deducing** it. The compiler then writes a real `largest` for `int`, compiled exactly like a function you typed by hand. Called with strings, it writes a second, separate `largest` for `std::string`. Each real function made from a template is called an **[[instantiation|instantiation]]**: one real case of the pattern.

### What T has to be able to do

The template never lists which types are allowed. It asks only for what its body does with `T`. Here that is two things: a `T` can be copied (`T best = v[0];`), and two `T`s can be compared with `<`. An `int`, a `double` and a `std::string` can all do both.

Call it with a type that has no `<`, and you get a compile error [[pointing inside the template|template-errors]]:

```cpp
struct Point { int x, y; };
largest(std::vector<Point>{{1, 2}});   // compile error: no < for two Points
```

C++20 has *concepts*, which let a template state its requirements up front and make those errors much easier to read. They come in the expert course.

### Deduction must agree

Deduction has one trap. Every place `T` appears must deduce the **same** type. Here is a template that counts how many items equal `x`:

```cpp
template <typename T>
int count_equal(const std::vector<T>& v, const T& x);

std::vector<std::string> names = {"ada", "lin"};
count_equal(names, "ada");   // error: T is std::string from names,
                             // but an array of 4 chars from "ada"
```

From `names`, `T` must be `std::string`. But `"ada"` is not a `std::string`. It is a string literal, and as the overloading lesson in the intermediate course showed, its type is [[an array of chars|literal-type]]. Two different answers for one blank, so the compiler gives up. It does not try turning `"ada"` into a `std::string` first: deduction only looks for a type that fits exactly.

### Two ways out

**A second blank.** Give the second parameter its own template parameter, `U`:

```cpp
template <typename T, typename U>
int count_equal(const std::vector<T>& v, const U& x);
```

Now `T` is `std::string` and `U` is the array type, and the two never have to agree. Inside, `item == x` still works, because a `std::string` can be compared with a string literal using `==`.

**Tell the compiler not to deduce from `x`.** `std::type_identity_t<T>`, from `<type_traits>`, names the very same type as `T`, but the compiler [[never deduces through it|type-identity]]:

```cpp
template <typename T>
int count_equal(const std::vector<T>& v, const std::type_identity_t<T>& x);
```

Now `T` comes only from the vector, so it is `std::string`. Once `T` is known, `x` is a `const std::string&`, and `"ada"` converts to a `std::string` the ordinary way, as it does for any function that takes one.

### Naming the type yourself

You can also skip deduction and write the type in angle brackets after the function's name:

```cpp
largest<double>({3, 2.5, 1});   // 3: T is double, so the braces become a std::vector<double>
```

Without `<double>` this call does not compile: a list in braces `{ }` has no type of its own, so there is nothing to deduce `T` from.

### Changing a vector in place

A template can also change the vector it is given. Take the vector by plain reference, `std::vector<T>&`, with no `const`, and loop with `T&` so each change lands in the vector itself, not in a copy:

```cpp
template <typename T>
void double_all(std::vector<T>& v) {
    for (T& x : v) x = x + x;   // {1, 5} becomes {2, 10}; {"ab"} becomes {"abab"}
}
```

**Watch out:** the deduction trap strikes whenever the same `T` appears in two or more parameters. Take `template <typename T> void clamp_all(std::vector<T>& v, const T& lo, const T& hi)`. With a `std::vector<int>` called `v`, `clamp_all(v, 0, 10)` works, but `clamp_all(v, 0, 10.5)` does not compile: `T` is `int` from `v` and from `0`, but `double` from `10.5`. When you see "deduced conflicting types" or "no matching function", check that every argument gives `T` the same type.

::: context typename-word Why typename, and sometimes class
`typename T` says "`T` stands for a type". You will also meet `template <class T>`. In this spot the two words mean exactly the same thing, and `T` can still be `int`, which is not a class at all. `class` came first: Bjarne Stroustrup, who created C++, reused an existing keyword rather than add a new one. `typename` arrived with the first official standard, C++98. Many programmers write `typename` because it says what it means. Template parameters can also be values instead of types; the next lesson shows how.
:::

::: context instantiation One pattern, many real functions
A template works like a cookie cutter. The cutter is not a cookie; each time you press it into dough you get a real one. `largest<int>` and `largest<std::string>` end up as two separate functions in the finished program, each as fast as one written by hand, because all the filling-in happens while compiling. There is a cost: each type you use adds its own copy of the code, and heavy use of templates makes compiling slower. A type nobody uses costs nothing, because no code is made for it.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="100" y="12" width="160" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="6 4"/>
  <text x="180" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">template &lt;typename T&gt;</text>
  <text x="180" y="45" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">T largest(...)</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="52" x2="95" y2="92" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="95,92 99,82 105,88" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="210" y1="52" x2="265" y2="92" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="265,92 255,88 261,82" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="10" y="94" width="165" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="92" y="116" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">int largest(...)</text>
  <rect x="185" y="94" width="165" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="267" y="116" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">std::string largest(...)</text>
  <text x="180" y="145" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">dashed: the pattern, not code yet. Filled: real functions</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context template-errors Reading an error from inside a template
The compiler only discovers the missing `<` when it writes out `largest` for `Point` and reaches the line `best < x`. So the first error points into the template's body, not at your call. The next lines lead back to you: g++ adds a line ending "required from here", and clang adds a note ending "requested here", each naming the line in your code that asked for this instantiation. With the standard library's own templates these messages can run to dozens of lines. Read the first error, then find the "required from" or "requested here" line that names your own file.
:::

::: context literal-type What "ada" really is
In memory, `"ada"` is four `char`s in a row: `'a'`, `'d'`, `'a'` and the hidden zero byte that marks the end. So its type is "array of 4 const chars", written `const char[4]`. In the overloading lesson it arrived in a function as a `const char*`, a pointer to its first letter, because an array passed by value turns into a pointer. A reference parameter like `const T&` keeps the array as it is, so deduction sees the array type. Either way it is not a `std::string`, and deduction will not pretend it is.
:::

::: context type-identity A type the compiler will not look through
`std::type_identity` is a tiny struct from C++20 whose only content says "my type is `T`". `std::type_identity_t<T>` is short for "the type inside `std::type_identity<T>`"; the standard library ends such shortcuts in `_t`. To deduce `T` from it, the compiler would have to guess which `T` produces a matching type inside, and the language rules say it must not guess. A parameter like that is called a **non-deduced context**. `T` is decided by the other parameters, and the argument is then converted to it. Before C++20, programmers wrote this same two-line struct themselves.
:::
--- task
Write three function templates. No `main`: the checker supplies it. The starter's `largest` works only for `int`s; turn it into a template, and add the other two.

- `T largest(const std::vector<T>& v)` returns the largest element, comparing with `<`. `v` is never empty. It must work for `int`, `double` and `std::string`: the largest of `{"pear", "apple", "zucchini", "fig"}` is `"zucchini"`.
- `count_equal(v, x)` returns how many elements of the vector `v` are equal to `x` (compare with `==`), as an `int`. It must work for `count_equal(names, "ada")`, where `names` is a `std::vector<std::string>`, and also for a `std::vector<int>` with an `int`.
- `void clamp_all(std::vector<T>& v, const T& lo, const T& hi)` changes the vector in place. "Clamp" means hold a value inside limits: every element below `lo` becomes `lo`, and every element above `hi` becomes `hi`. So `{-5, 3, 12, 10}` clamped between `0` and `10` becomes `{0, 3, 10, 10}`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

int largest(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return v[0];
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
T largest(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    T best = v[0];
    for (const T& x : v) {
        if (best < x) best = x;
    }
    return best;
}

template <typename T>
int count_equal(const std::vector<T>& v, const std::type_identity_t<T>& x) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const T& item : v) {
        if (item == x) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}

template <typename T>
void clamp_all(std::vector<T>& v, const T& lo, const T& hi) {
    for (T& x : v) {
        if (x < lo) x = lo;
        else if (hi < x) x = hi;
    }
}
--- hint
Put `template <typename T>` on the line above each function, and write `T` wherever the element type goes. For `largest`, that turns the starter's `int`s into `T`s.
--- hint
For `count_equal`, make the second parameter `const std::type_identity_t<T>& x`, so `T` is deduced from the vector only and `"ada"` becomes a `std::string`. Count with a loop: `if (item == x) ++n;`.
--- hint
`clamp_all` needs `for (T& x : v)`: the `&` makes each change land in the vector. Inside the loop: `if (x < lo) x = lo; else if (hi < x) x = hi;`.
--- check case | largest of ints
largest(std::vector<int>{3, 8, -1, 8})
=> 8
--- check test | largest of doubles
std::abs(largest(std::vector<double>{1.5, -2.0, 9.25}) - 9.25) < 1e-12
--- check case | largest of strings
largest(std::vector<std::string>{"pear", "apple", "zucchini", "fig"})
=> "zucchini"
--- check case | count_equal with a string literal
[] { std::vector<std::string> names{"ada", "lin", "ada"}; return count_equal(names, "ada"); }()
=> 2
--- check case | count_equal with ints
count_equal(std::vector<int>{1, 2, 1, 1}, 1)
=> 3
--- check test | clamp_all works in place
[] { std::vector<int> v{-5, 3, 12, 10}; clamp_all(v, 0, 10); std::vector<double> d{0.5, 2.5}; clamp_all(d, 1.0, 2.0); return v == std::vector<int>{0, 3, 10, 10} && d == std::vector<double>{1.0, 2.0}; }()

+++ practice | Reversed, and read the same both ways
--- task
The starter's two functions only work for `int`s. Turn each into a function template that works for any element type that can be copied and compared with `==`. No `main`.

- `std::vector<T> reversed(const std::vector<T>& v)` returns a new vector with the elements in the opposite order. `v` itself is not changed.
- `bool is_palindrome(const std::vector<T>& v)` returns `true` when the vector reads the same from both ends: `{'r', 'a', 'd', 'a', 'r'}` and `{"go", "no", "go"}` do. An empty vector and a vector of one element are palindromes too.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> reversed(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return v;
}

bool is_palindrome(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
std::vector<T> reversed(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    std::vector<T> out;
    for (std::size_t i = v.size(); i > 0; --i) out.push_back(v[i - 1]);
    return out;
}

template <typename T>
bool is_palindrome(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size() / 2; ++i) {
        if (!(v[i] == v[v.size() - 1 - i])) return false;
    }
    return true;
}
--- hint
Put `template <typename T>` above each function and write `T` wherever the element type goes, including inside `std::vector<…>` and for any local variable.
--- hint
For `reversed`, walk the indexes from `v.size()` down to 1 and push `v[i - 1]`; counting down this way never goes below 0. For `is_palindrome`, compare `v[i]` with `v[v.size() - 1 - i]` for the first half of the indexes.
--- check case | reversed ints
reversed(std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3})
=> std::vector<int>{3, 2, 1}
--- check case | reversed strings
reversed(std::vector<std::string>{"a", "b"})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"b", "a"}
--- check test | Palindromes of chars and strings
is_palindrome(std::vector<char>{'r', 'a', 'd', 'a', 'r'}) && is_palindrome(std::vector<std::string>{"go", "no", "go"}) && !is_palindrome(std::vector<char>{'a', 'b'})
--- check test | Empty and single vectors are palindromes
is_palindrome(std::vector<double>{}) && is_palindrome(std::vector<int>{7}) && reversed(std::vector<int>{}).empty()
--- check test | Even length, one wrong in the middle
is_palindrome(std::vector<int>{4, 1, 1, 4}) && !is_palindrome(std::vector<int>{4, 1, 2, 4})

+++ practice | Zip two lists together
--- task
Write two function templates with **two** type parameters each. No `main`.

- `zip(a, b)` takes a `const std::vector<T>&` and a `const std::vector<U>&` and returns a `std::vector<std::pair<T, U>>`, pairing the first element of each, then the second of each, and so on. It stops at the end of the shorter vector.
- `seconds(v)` takes a `const std::vector<std::pair<T, U>>&` and returns a `std::vector<U>` of the second item of every pair, in order.

`zip(std::vector<std::string>{"ada", "lin"}, std::vector<int>{36, 41, 99})` is `{{"ada", 36}, {"lin", 41}}`, and `seconds` of that is `{36, 41}`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::pair<std::string, int>> zip(const std::vector<std::string>& a, const std::vector<int>& b) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

template <typename T, typename U>
std::vector<std::pair<T, U>> zip(const std::vector<T>& a, const std::vector<U>& b) {
    std::vector<std::pair<T, U>> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < a.size() && i < b.size(); ++i) out.push_back({a[i], b[i]});
    return out;
}

template <typename T, typename U>
std::vector<U> seconds(const std::vector<std::pair<T, U>>& v) {
    std::vector<U> out;
    for (const auto& p : v) out.push_back(p.second);
    return out;
}
--- hint
Start each with `template <typename T, typename U>`. The two blanks are deduced separately, one from each vector, so they never have to agree.
--- hint
In `zip`, loop while `i < a.size() && i < b.size()` and `push_back({a[i], b[i]})`. In `seconds`, push `p.second` for every pair `p`.
--- check test | Names and ages, the longer list cut short
[] { auto z = zip(std::vector<std::string>{"ada", "lin"}, std::vector<int>{36, 41, 99}); return z.size() == 2 && z[0] == std::pair<std::string, int>{"ada", 36} && z[1].second == 41; }()
--- check test | Doubles with chars, the first list shorter
[] { auto z = zip(std::vector<double>{1.5}, std::vector<char>{'x', 'y'}); return z.size() == 1 && z[0].first == 1.5 && z[0].second == 'x'; }()
--- check test | Zipping with an empty list gives nothing
zip(std::vector<int>{}, std::vector<int>{1, 2}).empty() && zip(std::vector<int>{3}, std::vector<std::string>{}).empty()
--- check case | seconds takes the second of each pair
seconds(zip(std::vector<std::string>{"ada", "lin"}, std::vector<int>{36, 41}))
=> std::vector<int>{36, 41}

+++ practice | Tally anything, find the most common
--- task
Write two function templates that work for any element type that can be compared with `<`. No `main`.

- `std::map<T, int> tally(const std::vector<T>& v)` counts how many times each element appears.
- `T most_common(const std::vector<T>& v)` returns the element that appears most often. If several tie, return the smallest of them (by `<`). `v` is never empty.

`most_common(std::vector<std::string>{"burn", "coast", "burn", "coast", "abort"})` is `"burn"`: `burn` and `coast` both appear twice, and `"burn" < "coast"`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::map<int, int> tally(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return {};
}

int most_common(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return v[0];
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
std::map<T, int> tally(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    std::map<T, int> counts;
    for (const T& x : v) ++counts[x];
    return counts;
}

template <typename T>
T most_common(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    T best = v[0];
    int best_count = 0;
    for (const auto& [value, count] : tally(v)) {
        if (count > best_count) {
            best = value;
            best_count = count;
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
`tally` is the counting loop from the maps lessons, with `T` as the key type: `++counts[x];` for every element.
--- hint
A `std::map` visits its keys smallest first. So walk `tally(v)` and replace your best only when a count is strictly bigger: the first key to reach the top count, the smallest, stays the winner.
--- check test | tally counts every element
[] { auto t = tally(std::vector<char>{'a', 'b', 'a'}); return t.size() == 2 && t['a'] == 2 && t['b'] == 1; }()
--- check case | most_common of strings, a tie broken by <
most_common(std::vector<std::string>{"burn", "coast", "burn", "coast", "abort"})
=> "burn"
--- check case | most_common of ints
most_common(std::vector<int>{7, 3, 7, 3, 3})
=> 3
--- check case | One element
most_common(std::vector<double>{2.5})
=> 2.5
--- check test | tally of nothing is empty
tally(std::vector<int>{}).empty()

+++ practice | Maybe a largest, maybe an index
--- task
Write two function templates that cope with the cases a quick version gets wrong. No `main`.

- `std::optional<T> max_of(const std::vector<T>& v)` returns the largest element (by `<`), or `std::nullopt` when `v` is empty.
- `index_of(v, x)` returns a `std::optional<std::size_t>`: the index of the **first** element equal to `x` (by `==`), or `std::nullopt` if there is none. It must work for `index_of(names, "vega")`, where `names` is a `std::vector<std::string>`, as well as for a vector of `int` with an `int`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
std::optional<T> max_of(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    T best = v[0];
    for (const T& x : v) if (best < x) best = x;
    return best;
}

template <typename T>
std::optional<std::size_t> index_of(const std::vector<T>& v, const T& x) {
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) if (v[i] == x) return i;
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
std::optional<T> max_of(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return std::nullopt;
    T best = v[0];
    for (const T& x : v) if (best < x) best = x;
    return best;
}

template <typename T>
std::optional<std::size_t> index_of(const std::vector<T>& v, const std::type_identity_t<T>& x) {
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) if (v[i] == x) return i;
    return std::nullopt;
}
--- hint
`max_of` reads `v[0]` before checking anything. Check `v.empty()` first and return `std::nullopt`. In `index_of`, "not found" must be `std::nullopt`, not `0`, because 0 is a real index.
--- hint
`index_of(names, "vega")` deduces `T` twice and gets two answers. Make the second parameter `const std::type_identity_t<T>& x` so `T` comes from the vector only.
--- check test | max_of a list, and of nothing
max_of(std::vector<int>{-4, -1, -9}) == -1 && max_of(std::vector<int>{}) == std::nullopt
--- check test | index_of finds the first of duplicates
[] { std::vector<std::string> names{"apex", "vega", "vega"}; return index_of(names, "vega") == std::size_t(1); }()
--- check test | Found at index 0 is not the same as not found
[] { std::vector<int> v{5, 6}; return index_of(v, 5) == std::size_t(0) && index_of(v, 7) == std::nullopt; }()
--- check test | index_of in an empty vector
[] { std::vector<std::string> none; return index_of(none, "x") == std::nullopt; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the range that will not compile
--- task
**Bug report:** "`count_between(temps, 0, 30)` does not compile when `temps` is a `std::vector<double>`. Clang says: 'deduced conflicting types for parameter T (double vs. int)'."

`count_between(v, lo, hi)` should return how many elements of `v` lie from `lo` to `hi`, both ends included, as an `int`. The loop is right. Change only the function's parameters so that `T` is deduced from the vector alone. Then `count_between(temps, 0, 30)` works for doubles, and `count_between(names, "b", "m")` works for a vector of strings. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
int count_between(const std::vector<T>& v, const T& lo, const T& hi) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const T& x : v) {
        if (!(x < lo) && !(hi < x)) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
int count_between(const std::vector<T>& v, const std::type_identity_t<T>& lo, const std::type_identity_t<T>& hi) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const T& x : v) {
        if (!(x < lo) && !(hi < x)) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
`T` appears in three parameters. From `temps` it is `double`, but from `0` and `30` it is `int`: two answers for one blank.
--- hint
Wrap the type of `lo` and `hi` in `std::type_identity_t<T>`, so the compiler never deduces from them. Once `T` is `double`, the `0` and `30` convert as usual.
--- check case | Doubles with int limits
[] { std::vector<double> temps{-3.5, 0.0, 12.25, 30.0, 30.5}; return count_between(temps, 0, 30); }()
=> 3
--- check case | Strings with literal limits
[] { std::vector<std::string> names{"ada", "bo", "lin", "mo", "zed"}; return count_between(names, "b", "m"); }()
=> 2
--- check case | Ints still work, and an empty vector counts 0
[] { std::vector<int> none; return count_between(std::vector<int>{1, 5, 5, 9}, 5, 5) * 10 + count_between(none, 0, 1); }()
=> 20

+++ practice | Stretch: merge two sorted logs
--- task
Two instruments each log their readings in sorted order. Write two function templates for any type compared with `<`. No `main`.

- `std::vector<T> merge_sorted(const std::vector<T>& a, const std::vector<T>& b)`: both inputs are sorted smallest first. Return one sorted vector holding every element of both, repeats kept. Do it in one pass, walking both vectors side by side and taking the smaller front element each time; when two fronts are equal, take the one from `a` first. Do not sort the result afterwards.
- `std::vector<T> without_repeats(const std::vector<T>& sorted)`: the input is sorted. Return it with every run of equal elements kept only once.

`merge_sorted(std::vector<int>{1, 4, 4, 9}, std::vector<int>{2, 4, 10})` is `{1, 2, 4, 4, 4, 9, 10}`, and `without_repeats` of that is `{1, 2, 4, 9, 10}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
std::vector<T> merge_sorted(const std::vector<T>& a, const std::vector<T>& b) {
    return a;
}

template <typename T>
std::vector<T> without_repeats(const std::vector<T>& sorted) {
    return sorted;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
std::vector<T> merge_sorted(const std::vector<T>& a, const std::vector<T>& b) {
    std::vector<T> out;
    std::size_t i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < a.size() && j < b.size()) {
        if (b[j] < a[i]) {
            out.push_back(b[j]);
            ++j;
        } else {
            out.push_back(a[i]);
            ++i;
        }
    }
    for (; i < a.size(); ++i) out.push_back(a[i]);
    for (; j < b.size(); ++j) out.push_back(b[j]);
    return out;
}

template <typename T>
std::vector<T> without_repeats(const std::vector<T>& sorted) {
    std::vector<T> out;
    for (const T& x : sorted) {
        if (out.empty() || out.back() < x) out.push_back(x);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Keep one index into each vector. While both have elements left, push the smaller front one and move that index on. When one vector runs out, copy what is left of the other.
--- hint
Taking `a`'s element first on a tie means taking `b[j]` only when `b[j] < a[i]`. For `without_repeats`, push `x` only when the output is empty or its last element is smaller than `x`: in sorted input, an equal element is a repeat.
--- check case | Merging with repeats across both
merge_sorted(std::vector<int>{1, 4, 4, 9}, std::vector<int>{2, 4, 10})
=> std::vector<int>{1, 2, 4, 4, 4, 9, 10}
--- check case | Removing repeats
without_repeats(std::vector<int>{1, 2, 4, 4, 4, 9, 10})
=> std::vector<int>{1, 2, 4, 9, 10}
--- check case | Strings, one list empty
merge_sorted(std::vector<std::string>{}, std::vector<std::string>{"a", "c"})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"a", "c"}
--- check test | Every element of one list comes before the other
merge_sorted(std::vector<double>{5.5, 6.5}, std::vector<double>{1.0, 2.0}) == std::vector<double>{1.0, 2.0, 5.5, 6.5} && without_repeats(std::vector<double>{}).empty()
--- check test | A million readings merge and stay sorted
[] { std::vector<int> a, b; for (int i = 0; i < 500000; ++i) { a.push_back(2 * i); b.push_back(2 * i + 1); } auto m = merge_sorted(a, b); bool sorted = m.size() == 1000000; for (int i = 0; sorted && i < 1000000; ++i) sorted = m[i] == i; return sorted && without_repeats(merge_sorted(a, a)).size() == 500000; }()

=== cpp3-06 | Class templates
--- teach
Last lesson you wrote functions with a blank for the type. A class can have a blank too. You have used plenty of classes like that: `std::vector<T>` from the basics course, `std::optional<T>` from the intermediate course, `std::unique_ptr<T>` from the smart pointers lesson. Now you will write your own.

### A class with a blank for the type

A **class template** is a blueprint for a whole family of classes. You write the class once, with `T` for the type, and each `Box<int>` or `Box<std::string>` you use becomes a real, separate class:

```cpp
template <typename T>
class Box {
public:
    explicit Box(T value) : value_(std::move(value)) {}
    const T& get() const { return value_; }
private:
    T value_;
};
```

The first line is the same as for a function template: "a template, for any type T". Inside the class, `T` is used like any type:

- `T value_;` is a member that holds one `T`.
- The constructor takes a `T` called `value`. `std::move(value)` is the move from the moving lesson: `value` is the constructor's own copy, about to go away, so its insides can be handed to `value_` instead of copied a second time.
- `get()` hands back a `const T&`, a read-only reference, so looking at a big `T` costs no copy.

### Using it

```cpp
Box<int> a(5);              // a Box holding an int
Box<std::string> b("hi");   // a Box holding a std::string
Box c(2.5);                 // C++17: T deduced as double from the constructor
```

Usually you write the type in angle brackets, as with `std::vector<int>`. Since C++17 the compiler can also [[work it out from the constructor|ctad]], as in `Box c(2.5)`.

Member functions written inside the class body need no extra syntax: no second `template` line, no `<T>` after their names.

### Only what you call gets built

The compiler writes out a member function for a particular `T` only when some code calls it. So a `Box<T>` whose `print()` needs `<<` still works for a `T` that has no `<<`, as long as nobody calls `print()`:

```cpp
// inside Box, one more member:
void print() const { std::cout << value_; }

struct Point { int x, y; };
Box<Point> p(Point{1, 2});   // fine
p.get();                     // fine
// p.print();                // would not compile: there is no << for a Point
```

### A value as a template parameter

Template parameters do not have to be types. A parameter can also be a **value** that is known while compiling:

```cpp
template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class Recent { /* keeps at most N items */ };

Recent<int, 3> last_three;
```

Read `std::size_t N` as "a whole number N that cannot be negative", the same `std::size_t` that `v.size()` gives back. The number becomes [[part of the type|value-parameter]]: `Recent<int, 3>` and `Recent<int, 4>` are two different classes, as different as `Box<int>` and `Box<double>`.

### Catching a nonsense size

A `Recent` that keeps zero items makes no sense. Because `N` is known while compiling, the compiler itself can refuse it. **`static_assert`** is a [[check made while compiling|static-assert]]: when its condition is false, the compile stops with your message.

```cpp
template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class Recent {
    static_assert(N > 0, "Recent needs room for at least one item");
    // ...
};

Recent<int, 0> r;   // compile error: static assertion failed: Recent needs room for at least one item
```

The message is optional: `static_assert(N > 0);` works too, with a plainer error.

### Taking from something that might be empty

The task's first class is a **stack**: a container you add to and take from only at the top, like a [[stack of plates|lifo]]. The last item put on is the first one taken off.

"Take the top item" can fail: the stack might be empty. Returning **`std::optional<T>`** is a clean design, because the caller cannot forget that there might be nothing. Here is the same idea in a small function template that takes the last item of a vector:

```cpp
template <typename T>
std::optional<T> take_last(std::vector<T>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return std::nullopt;   // nothing to give
    T last = std::move(v.back());         // move the item out first
    v.pop_back();                         // then remove its slot
    return last;                          // arrives wrapped in an optional
}
```

### Looking without taking

"Look at the top" should not remove anything, and should not copy either. Return a **`const T*`**: a pointer to the item, read-only, or `nullptr` when there is nothing to see. `&v.back()` is "the address of the last element", with the address-of `&` from the pointers lesson:

```cpp
template <typename T>
const T* peek_last(const std::vector<T>& v) {
    return v.empty() ? nullptr : &v.back();
}
```

The caller checks the pointer before using it: `if (const int* p = peek_last(v)) { ... *p ... }`. Why [[a pointer and not an optional|pointer-not-optional]]? An optional would hold its own copy of the item; the pointer only points at the one already there.

### A container that is cheap at both ends

The second class keeps the most recent items and drops the oldest. Taking from the front of a `std::vector` is slow, because every other element has to shift down one place. One more standard container fits better. **`std::deque<T>`**, from `<deque>` and said "deck", is like a vector that is also cheap to change at the **front**:

```cpp
std::deque<int> q = {1, 2, 3};
q.push_back(4);    // 1 2 3 4
q.pop_front();     // 2 3 4: the oldest is gone
q.front();         // 2
q.back();          // 4
```

To hand the items back as a `std::vector`, build one from the deque's range, as the maps and sets lesson did from a set: `std::vector<int>(q.begin(), q.end())` keeps their order, front first.

**Watch out:** in a function that removes the top item, read the item **before** you remove it. `pop_back()` destroys the last element, so reading `back()` after it gives a different element, or, if that was the only one, undefined behavior. And calling `back()` or `pop_back()` on an empty vector is undefined behavior too, which is why `take_last` checks `empty()` first.

::: context ctad Deducing a class's type
The feature is called **class template argument deduction**, CTAD for short, and it arrived in C++17. The compiler looks at the constructor's parameters, as it would for a function template. It can surprise you: `Box d("hi");` gives a `Box<const char*>`, holding a pointer, not a `Box<std::string>`, because a string literal passed by value arrives as a pointer. When the type matters, write it out: `Box<std::string> b("hi");`. Before C++17 you always had to, which is why helper functions like `std::make_pair` existed: functions could deduce, classes could not.
:::

::: context value-parameter Sizes fixed before launch
The standard library's `std::array<T, N>` works this way: `std::array<double, 3>` is exactly three doubles, stored inside the object, with no heap memory at all. Flight software likes sizes fixed like this. NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" rules for safety-critical code forbid allocating memory on the heap after the program has started up, because a heap can run out or break into scattered pieces in the middle of a mission. A container whose size is part of its type can never grow past what was planned and tested.
:::

::: context static-assert Failing on the ground, not in flight
An ordinary check runs while the program runs, so a mistake is only found if that line happens to run during a test. A `static_assert` is checked every time the program is compiled. If it fails, there is no program at all, so the mistake can never reach a spacecraft. It arrived in C++11, and it works on anything the compiler knows while compiling: template parameters, sizes of types, `constexpr` values. `static_assert(sizeof(int) == 4);` is a common one, guarding code that assumes an `int` is four bytes.
:::

::: context lifo Last in, first out
Put plates 1, 2 and 3 on a pile. The one you can take is 3, the last one added. Programmers call this **LIFO**, "last in, first out". Adding is traditionally called *push* and removing *pop*, the same words `std::vector` uses for `push_back` and `pop_back`. Your program runs on a stack too: each function call puts its local variables on top of the **call stack**, and they come off when it returns.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="110" y="110" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="126" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1 (first in)</text>
  <rect x="110" y="84" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <rect x="110" y="58" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="74" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3 (top)</text>
  <line x1="60" y1="20" x2="60" y2="56" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="55,50 60,60 65,50" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="60" y="14" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">push</text>
  <line x1="300" y1="56" x2="300" y2="20" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="295,26 300,16 305,26" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="300" y="72" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">pop</text>
  <text x="180" y="146" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">both happen at the top only</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context pointer-not-optional Why peeking returns a pointer
`std::optional<T>` keeps its value inside itself, so returning one means copying the top item into it. For a stack of big strings that is wasted work, only to look. A reference, `const T&`, would avoid the copy, but a reference must always refer to something, and an empty stack has nothing to refer to. A pointer can do both jobs: it points at the real item, and `nullptr` means "nothing there". The catch is the one from the pointers lesson: the pointer is only good while the item is still in the stack. After a `pop`, it points at nothing valid.
:::
--- task
Write two class templates. No `main`: the checker supplies it. The starter's `Stack` has the right member functions, but none of them do anything yet, and there is no `Recent` at all.

`Stack<T>`, a last-in-first-out stack:

- `void push(const T& x)` puts `x` on top.
- `std::optional<T> pop()` removes the top item and returns it, or returns `std::nullopt` when the stack is empty.
- `const T* top() const` gives a pointer to the top item without removing it, or `nullptr` when the stack is empty.
- `std::size_t size() const` returns how many items it holds, and `bool empty() const` says whether it holds none.

`Recent<T, N>`, which keeps only the last `N` items added:

- `void add(const T& x)` adds `x`. When it already holds `N` items, the oldest is dropped first.
- `std::vector<T> items() const` returns the items it holds, oldest first. After adding 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 to a `Recent<int, 3>`, `items()` is `{3, 4, 5}`. With fewer than `N` added, it returns all of them.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <deque>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class Stack {
public:
    void push(const T& x) {}
    std::optional<T> pop() { return std::nullopt; }
    const T* top() const { return nullptr; }
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    bool empty() const { return true; }
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <deque>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class Stack {
public:
    void push(const T& x) { items_.push_back(x); }
    std::optional<T> pop() {
        if (items_.empty()) return std::nullopt;
        T top = std::move(items_.back());
        items_.pop_back();
        return top;
    }
    const T* top() const { return items_.empty() ? nullptr : &items_.back(); }
    std::size_t size() const { return items_.size(); }
    bool empty() const { return items_.empty(); }
private:
    std::vector<T> items_;
};

template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class Recent {
    static_assert(N > 0, "Recent needs room for at least one item");
public:
    void add(const T& x) {
        if (items_.size() == N) items_.pop_front();
        items_.push_back(x);
    }
    std::vector<T> items() const { return std::vector<T>(items_.begin(), items_.end()); }
private:
    std::deque<T> items_;
};
--- hint
Give `Stack` a private member `std::vector<T> items_;`. The back of the vector is the top of the stack, so `push` is `push_back`, and `size()` and `empty()` ask the vector.
--- hint
`pop` works like `take_last` in the explanation: check for empty first, move the back element out, `pop_back()`, then return it. `top` works like `peek_last`: `items_.empty() ? nullptr : &items_.back()`.
--- hint
`Recent` starts with `template <typename T, std::size_t N>` and holds a private `std::deque<T> items_;`. In `add`, if `items_.size() == N`, call `pop_front()` before `push_back(x)`. `items()` returns `std::vector<T>(items_.begin(), items_.end())`.
--- check test | Push, top and pop, last in first out
[] { Stack<int> s; s.push(1); s.push(2); bool top2 = s.top() && *s.top() == 2; auto a = s.pop(); auto b = s.pop(); return top2 && a == 2 && b == 1 && s.empty(); }()
--- check test | An empty stack says so
[] { Stack<std::string> s; return s.pop() == std::nullopt && s.top() == nullptr && s.size() == 0 && s.empty(); }()
--- check test | Stack works with strings
[] { Stack<std::string> s; s.push("a"); s.push("b"); s.push("c"); s.pop(); return s.size() == 2 && *s.top() == "b"; }()
--- check case | Recent keeps the last three
[] { Recent<int, 3> r; for (int i = 1; i <= 5; ++i) r.add(i); return r.items(); }()
=> std::vector<int>{3, 4, 5}
--- check case | Recent with fewer than N items
[] { Recent<std::string, 4> r; r.add("x"); r.add("y"); return r.items(); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"x", "y"}

+++ practice | The smallest and largest seen so far
--- task
Write a class template `MinMax<T>` that remembers every value added and can point at the smallest and the largest, for any `T` that can be copied and compared with `<`. No `main`.

- `void add(const T& x)` stores `x`.
- `const T* min() const` points at the smallest value stored, and `const T* max() const` at the largest. Both return `nullptr` when nothing has been added.
- `std::size_t count() const` is how many values have been added.

After adding 4, -2 and 9 to a `MinMax<int>`, `*min()` is -2, `*max()` is 9 and `count()` is 3. For strings, "smallest" means first in dictionary order.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class MinMax {
public:
    void add(int x) {}
    const int* min() const { return nullptr; }
    const int* max() const { return nullptr; }
    std::size_t count() const { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class MinMax {
public:
    void add(const T& x) { seen_.push_back(x); }

    const T* min() const {
        if (seen_.empty()) return nullptr;
        const T* best = &seen_[0];
        for (const T& x : seen_) if (x < *best) best = &x;
        return best;
    }

    const T* max() const {
        if (seen_.empty()) return nullptr;
        const T* best = &seen_[0];
        for (const T& x : seen_) if (*best < x) best = &x;
        return best;
    }

    std::size_t count() const { return seen_.size(); }

private:
    std::vector<T> seen_;
};
--- hint
Put `template <typename T>` above the class and keep the values in a private `std::vector<T>`. Every `int` that stands for a stored value becomes `T`.
--- hint
`min()` returns `nullptr` for an empty vector. Otherwise keep a `const T*` that starts at `&seen_[0]`, and point it at any element that is smaller: `if (x < *best) best = &x;`, looping with `const T& x` so `&x` is the element inside the vector.
--- check test | Ints: smallest, largest and count
[] { MinMax<int> m; m.add(4); m.add(-2); m.add(9); return m.min() && *m.min() == -2 && *m.max() == 9 && m.count() == 3; }()
--- check test | Nothing added gives nullptr
[] { MinMax<double> m; return m.min() == nullptr && m.max() == nullptr && m.count() == 0; }()
--- check test | Strings in dictionary order
[] { MinMax<std::string> m; m.add("mars"); m.add("ceres"); m.add("venus"); return *m.min() == "ceres" && *m.max() == "venus"; }()
--- check test | One value is both
[] { MinMax<int> m; m.add(7); return m.min() == m.max() && *m.min() == 7; }()

+++ practice | A queue: first in, first out
--- task
A **queue** is the opposite of a stack: items leave in the order they arrived, like people at a ticket desk. Write the class template `Queue<T>`, keeping its items in a private `std::deque<T>`. No `main`.

- `void push(const T& x)` adds `x` at the back.
- `std::optional<T> pop()` removes the item at the front and returns it, or returns `std::nullopt` when the queue is empty.
- `const T* front() const` points at the front item without removing it, or returns `nullptr` when empty.
- `std::size_t size() const` and `bool empty() const`.

After pushing `"a"`, `"b"` and `"c"`, `pop()` gives `"a"` and then `"b"`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <deque>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

template <typename T>
class Queue {
public:
    void push(const T& x) {}
    std::optional<T> pop() { return std::nullopt; }
    const T* front() const { return nullptr; }
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    bool empty() const { return true; }
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <deque>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

template <typename T>
class Queue {
public:
    void push(const T& x) { items_.push_back(x); }

    std::optional<T> pop() {
        if (items_.empty()) return std::nullopt;
        T first = std::move(items_.front());
        items_.pop_front();
        return first;
    }

    const T* front() const { return items_.empty() ? nullptr : &items_.front(); }
    std::size_t size() const { return items_.size(); }
    bool empty() const { return items_.empty(); }

private:
    std::deque<T> items_;
};
--- hint
Items go in at the back with `push_back` and leave from the front with `pop_front`. A deque is cheap at both ends.
--- hint
`pop` checks `empty()` first, moves `items_.front()` into a local, then calls `pop_front()`, then returns the local. Read the item before you remove it.
--- check test | First in, first out
[] { Queue<std::string> q; q.push("a"); q.push("b"); q.push("c"); auto x = q.pop(); auto y = q.pop(); return x == "a" && y == "b" && q.size() == 1 && *q.front() == "c"; }()
--- check test | An empty queue says so
[] { Queue<int> q; return q.pop() == std::nullopt && q.front() == nullptr && q.empty() && q.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Emptied and filled again
[] { Queue<int> q; q.push(1); q.pop(); bool was_empty = q.empty(); q.push(2); q.push(3); return was_empty && q.pop() == 2 && q.pop() == 3 && q.pop() == std::nullopt; }()

+++ practice | A counter for any kind of key
--- task
Write the class template `Counter<K>`, which counts how often each key is seen, keeping the counts in a private `std::map<K, int>`. `K` can be any type compared with `<`. No `main`.

- `void add(const K& key)` counts one more `key`.
- `int count(const K& key) const` is how many times `key` was added, 0 if never. It must not add the key.
- `std::vector<K> top(std::size_t n) const` returns the `n` most frequent keys, most frequent first. Keys with equal counts go smallest first. If `n` is more than the number of keys, return them all.

After adding `"go"`, `"stop"`, `"go"`, `"wait"` and `"stop"`, `top(2)` is `{"go", "stop"}`: both were seen twice, and `"go" < "stop"`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

template <typename K>
class Counter {
public:
    void add(const K& key) {}
    int count(const K& key) const { return 0; }
    std::vector<K> top(std::size_t n) const { return {}; }
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

template <typename K>
class Counter {
public:
    void add(const K& key) { ++counts_[key]; }

    int count(const K& key) const {
        auto it = counts_.find(key);
        return it == counts_.end() ? 0 : it->second;
    }

    std::vector<K> top(std::size_t n) const {
        std::vector<std::pair<K, int>> all(counts_.begin(), counts_.end());
        std::sort(all.begin(), all.end(), [](const std::pair<K, int>& a, const std::pair<K, int>& b) {
            if (a.second != b.second) return a.second > b.second;
            return a.first < b.first;
        });
        std::vector<K> out;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n && i < all.size(); ++i) out.push_back(all[i].first);
        return out;
    }

private:
    std::map<K, int> counts_;
};
--- hint
`add` is `++counts_[key];`. `count` must use `find`, because `[ ]` would add the key, and it is not allowed in a `const` function anyway.
--- hint
For `top`, copy the map into a `std::vector<std::pair<K, int>>` (a vector can be built from the map's `begin()` and `end()`), then `std::sort` it with a lambda: a bigger count comes first, and on equal counts the smaller key comes first. Then take the first `n` keys.
--- check case | The two most frequent words
[] { Counter<std::string> c; for (const char* w : {"go", "stop", "go", "wait", "stop"}) c.add(w); return c.top(2); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"go", "stop"}
--- check test | count, including a key never added
[] { Counter<int> c; c.add(3); c.add(3); c.add(8); return c.count(3) == 2 && c.count(8) == 1 && c.count(5) == 0 && c.top(10).size() == 2; }()
--- check case | Asking for more than there are
[] { Counter<char> c; c.add('b'); c.add('a'); c.add('b'); return c.top(9); }()
=> std::vector<char>{'b', 'a'}
--- check test | An empty counter and top(0)
[] { Counter<std::string> c; bool none = c.top(3).empty(); c.add("x"); return none && c.top(0).empty(); }()

+++ practice | A stack with a fixed number of slots
--- task
Write the class template `FixedStack<T, N>`, a stack that can never hold more than `N` items. `N` is a `std::size_t` template parameter. No `main`.

- `static_assert` that `N` is greater than 0, with any message.
- `bool push(const T& x)` puts `x` on top and returns `true`, or returns `false` and changes nothing when the stack is already full.
- `std::optional<T> pop()` removes and returns the top item, or `std::nullopt` when empty.
- `const T* top() const` points at the top item, or is `nullptr` when empty.
- `std::size_t size() const`, `bool full() const`, and `std::size_t capacity() const`, which returns `N`.

A `FixedStack<int, 2>` takes 1 and 2, refuses 3, and then pops 2.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class FixedStack {
public:
    bool push(const T& x) { items_.push_back(x); return true; }
    std::optional<T> pop() {
        T top = items_.back();
        items_.pop_back();
        return top;
    }
    const T* top() const { return &items_.back(); }
    std::size_t size() const { return items_.size(); }
    bool full() const { return false; }
    std::size_t capacity() const { return N; }
private:
    std::vector<T> items_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class FixedStack {
    static_assert(N > 0, "FixedStack needs at least one slot");
public:
    bool push(const T& x) {
        if (full()) return false;
        items_.push_back(x);
        return true;
    }
    std::optional<T> pop() {
        if (items_.empty()) return std::nullopt;
        T top = std::move(items_.back());
        items_.pop_back();
        return top;
    }
    const T* top() const { return items_.empty() ? nullptr : &items_.back(); }
    std::size_t size() const { return items_.size(); }
    bool full() const { return items_.size() == N; }
    std::size_t capacity() const { return N; }
private:
    std::vector<T> items_;
};
--- hint
Three functions forget the edges: `push` never checks for full, and `pop` and `top` never check for empty. `full()` is simply `items_.size() == N`.
--- hint
Put `static_assert(N > 0, "…");` inside the class, before `public:`. In `push`, `if (full()) return false;` comes first. `pop` and `top` check `items_.empty()` before touching `back()`.
--- check test | Refuses to go past N
[] { FixedStack<int, 2> s; bool a = s.push(1); bool b = s.push(2); bool c = s.push(3); return a && b && !c && s.full() && s.size() == 2 && *s.top() == 2 && s.capacity() == 2; }()
--- check test | Empty: nothing to pop or see
[] { FixedStack<std::string, 3> s; return s.pop() == std::nullopt && s.top() == nullptr && !s.full() && s.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | One slot: full, popped, room again
[] { FixedStack<int, 1> s; s.push(7); bool refused = !s.push(8); auto x = s.pop(); bool again = s.push(9); return refused && x == 7 && again && *s.top() == 9; }()
--- check test | Last in, first out
[] { FixedStack<char, 3> s; s.push('a'); s.push('b'); s.push('c'); return s.pop() == 'c' && s.pop() == 'b' && s.pop() == 'a' && s.pop() == std::nullopt; }()

+++ practice | Debug: pop returns the wrong item
--- task
**Bug report:** "`Pile::pop()` returns the item **under** the top instead of the top. After pushing 1, 2 and 3, the first `pop()` gives 2. With only one item on the pile, it sometimes crashes."

`Pile<T>` is a last-in-first-out stack. `pop()` should remove the top item and return it, or return `std::nullopt` when the pile is empty. Fix `pop()`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class Pile {
public:
    void push(const T& x) { items_.push_back(x); }
    std::optional<T> pop() {
        if (items_.empty()) return std::nullopt;
        items_.pop_back();
        return items_.back();
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return items_.size(); }
private:
    std::vector<T> items_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class Pile {
public:
    void push(const T& x) { items_.push_back(x); }
    std::optional<T> pop() {
        if (items_.empty()) return std::nullopt;
        T top = std::move(items_.back());
        items_.pop_back();
        return top;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return items_.size(); }
private:
    std::vector<T> items_;
};
--- hint
`pop_back()` destroys the last element. What is `items_.back()` after that?
--- hint
Read the top item into a local first, then call `pop_back()`, then return the local.
--- check test | The first pop gives the last push
[] { Pile<int> p; p.push(1); p.push(2); p.push(3); return p.pop() == 3 && p.size() == 2; }()
--- check test | Pops come out in reverse order
[] { Pile<std::string> p; p.push("x"); p.push("y"); auto a = p.pop(); auto b = p.pop(); return a == "y" && b == "x" && p.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | A pile of one, then empty
[] { Pile<int> p; p.push(42); auto a = p.pop(); return a == 42 && p.pop() == std::nullopt; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: undo and redo
--- task
Write the class template `History<T>`, which holds one current value of any copyable type and lets you undo and redo changes to it, like the undo button of an editor. No `main`.

- `explicit History(const T& initial)` starts with `initial` as the current value and nothing to undo or redo.
- `void set(const T& value)` makes `value` current. The old current value can be undone back to. Setting a value also throws away everything that could have been redone.
- `const T& get() const` returns the current value.
- `bool undo()` goes back to the value before the current one and returns `true`, or returns `false` and changes nothing if there is nothing to undo.
- `bool redo()` goes forward again after an `undo` and returns `true`, or returns `false` and changes nothing if there is nothing to redo.

Starting from `"a"`: `set("ab")`, `set("abc")`, `undo()` gives `"ab"`, `undo()` gives `"a"`, `redo()` gives `"ab"`. Then `set("aX")` makes `redo()` return `false`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class History {
public:
    explicit History(const T& initial) : current_(initial) {}
    void set(const T& value) { current_ = value; }
    const T& get() const { return current_; }
    bool undo() { return false; }
    bool redo() { return false; }
private:
    T current_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

template <typename T>
class History {
public:
    explicit History(const T& initial) : current_(initial) {}

    void set(const T& value) {
        undo_.push_back(current_);
        current_ = value;
        redo_.clear();
    }

    const T& get() const { return current_; }

    bool undo() {
        if (undo_.empty()) return false;
        redo_.push_back(current_);
        current_ = undo_.back();
        undo_.pop_back();
        return true;
    }

    bool redo() {
        if (redo_.empty()) return false;
        undo_.push_back(current_);
        current_ = redo_.back();
        redo_.pop_back();
        return true;
    }

private:
    T current_;
    std::vector<T> undo_;
    std::vector<T> redo_;
};
--- hint
Use two stacks as private `std::vector<T>` members: one of values you can go back to, one of values you can go forward to. The most recent sits at the back of each.
--- hint
`set` pushes the current value onto the undo stack, takes the new value, and clears the redo stack. `undo` pushes the current value onto the redo stack and takes the back of the undo stack (then pops it). `redo` is the mirror image.
--- check test | The example from the task
[] { History<std::string> h("a"); h.set("ab"); h.set("abc"); bool u1 = h.undo() && h.get() == "ab"; bool u2 = h.undo() && h.get() == "a"; bool r1 = h.redo() && h.get() == "ab"; h.set("aX"); return u1 && u2 && r1 && !h.redo() && h.get() == "aX"; }()
--- check test | Nothing to undo or redo at the start
[] { History<int> h(5); return !h.undo() && !h.redo() && h.get() == 5; }()
--- check test | Undo everything, redo everything
[] { History<int> h(0); for (int i = 1; i <= 4; ++i) h.set(i * 10); int undos = 0; while (h.undo()) ++undos; bool back = h.get() == 0; int redos = 0; while (h.redo()) ++redos; return undos == 4 && back && redos == 4 && h.get() == 40; }()
--- check test | An undo after a set that cleared redo
[] { History<int> h(1); h.set(2); h.undo(); h.set(3); bool no_redo = !h.redo(); bool u = h.undo() && h.get() == 1; return no_redo && u && !h.undo(); }()

=== cpp3-07 | Operator overloading: a Fraction type
--- teach
Last lesson your `Stack` and `Recent` worked for any type. But to add, compare or print a type you write yourself, you still need named functions: `add(a, b)`, `equal(a, b)`. An `int` gets `+`, `==`, `<` and `std::cout << x` for free. This lesson gives your own types the same symbols, and ends with a fraction type that does exact arithmetic, with no rounding at all.

### An operator is a function with a special name

On a calculator, the `+` key works for whole numbers and decimals alike. C++ lets you teach `+` what to do for your own type too. **Operator overloading** means writing what a symbol like `+` or `==` does for your type. You write it as a function whose name is the word `operator` followed by the symbol:

```cpp
struct Vec2 { double x, y; };   // for example a velocity: an x part and a y part

Vec2 operator+(const Vec2& a, const Vec2& b) {
    return {a.x + b.x, a.y + b.y};
}

Vec2 v{3, 4};
Vec2 w{1, -2};
Vec2 sum = v + w;   // (4, 2)
```

When the compiler sees `v + w` with two `Vec2`s, it [[calls operator+(v, w)|operator-call]] for you. The function takes both sides by `const` reference, so nothing is copied or changed. `return {a.x + b.x, a.y + b.y};` builds the new `Vec2` from the values in braces, in the order the members are listed.

### Comparing with ==

`==` is an operator like any other. It answers with a `bool`:

```cpp
bool operator==(const Vec2& a, const Vec2& b) {
    return a.x == b.x && a.y == b.y;
}
```

In C++20, writing `==` also gives you `!=` for free: the compiler rewrites `a != b` as "not `a == b`".

### Printing with <<
