`<<` is read "put to". To make `std::cout << v` work, write an `operator<<` whose left side is a stream:

```cpp
std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Vec2& v) {
    return out << "(" << v.x << ", " << v.y << ")";
}

std::cout << v << "\n";   // (3, 4)
```

`std::ostream`, from `<ostream>`, means "output stream". It is [[the type behind std::cout|ostream-family]], and behind `std::ostringstream` too, so this one function prints to both. It takes the stream by reference and **returns the stream**. That is what lets calls chain: `out << a << b` runs as `(out << a) << b`, so the second `<<` puts `b` into whatever the first one gave back, which is `out` again.

### Mean what the symbol means

The rule that keeps operators pleasant: `+` adds, `==` compares, `<` puts things in order. Someone reading `a + b` expects a sum. An `operator+` that prints, or a `<` that secretly changes something, is a trap for every reader after you.

### A whole number can turn into your type

The rest of the lesson builds a `Fraction`. A constructor like this one has **default arguments**, as in the overloading lesson of the intermediate course:

```cpp
class Fraction {
public:
    Fraction(long long num = 0, long long den = 1);   // Fraction(3) is 3/1
    // ...
};
```

`long long` is a whole number at least 64 bits wide, far bigger than an `int`. In the intermediate course you put `explicit` on one-value constructors to stop surprise conversions. Here the constructor is left **not** `explicit` on purpose: a whole number really is a fraction, 3 is 3/1, so C++ may turn `1` into `Fraction(1)` whenever a `Fraction` is needed.

### Write two-sided operators outside the class

An operator with two sides, like `+` or `==`, is called a **binary operator**. You could write one as a member function, with the left side as the object:

```cpp
// as a member: the left side is the object itself
Fraction operator+(const Fraction& b) const;

Fraction half(1, 2);

half + 1;   // fine: 1 converts to Fraction(1)
1 + half;   // compile error: 1 is an int, and an int has no member operator+
```

The left side of a member call is [[never converted|left-side]]. Written as a **non-member** function, outside the class, both sides are ordinary parameters, and both can be converted:

```cpp
Fraction operator+(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b);

half + 1;   // fine
1 + half;   // fine: 1 becomes Fraction(1)
```

So write binary operators as non-member functions. They can reach the data through public members like `num()` and `den()`.

### An invariant makes == easy

In the intermediate course, a class kept an **invariant**: a rule that is true of every object, always. For a fraction, choose this rule: it is always stored **in lowest terms, with a positive denominator**. Then 2/4 and 1/2 are stored identically, as 1 and 2, and `==` only has to compare two pairs of numbers.

The constructor makes the rule true. First the sign: if the denominator is negative, flip the sign of both parts, which does not change the value. Then reduce: divide both parts by their **greatest common divisor**, the biggest whole number that divides both. `std::gcd`, from `<numeric>`, [[works it out|gcd]]:

```cpp
std::gcd(6, 4);    // 2
std::gcd(-6, 4);   // 2: it ignores signs
std::gcd(0, 5);    // 5, so 0/5 becomes 0/1
```

So 6/−4 becomes −6/4 after the sign step, and −3/2 after dividing both by 2.

### Arithmetic on fractions

Each operator builds a new `Fraction` from the parts and lets the constructor tidy it up. These are the school rules, with `b` and `d` the denominators:

- add: a/b + c/d = (a·d + c·b) / (b·d)
- subtract: a/b − c/d = (a·d − c·b) / (b·d)
- multiply: a/b · c/d = (a·c) / (b·d)
- divide: a/b ÷ c/d = (a·d) / (b·c), multiplying by the flipped fraction

For 1/2 + 1/3: (1·3 + 1·2) / (2·3) = 5/6. The products in these formulas [[grow fast|overflow]], which is one more reason to reduce every answer as soon as it is made. Dividing by a negative fraction gives a negative denominator, and the constructor flips it, so the invariant holds whatever the operator produces.

### Comparing without decimals

Is 1/3 less than 1/2? Turning both into decimals means rounding, and rounding can make two different fractions look equal. Instead, **cross-multiply**. With positive denominators, a/b < c/d is true exactly when a·d < c·b:

```cpp
// is 1/3 < 1/2 ?   1·2 = 2  and  1·3 = 3,  and 2 < 3, so yes
// in code, for two fractions x and y:
x.num() * y.den() < y.num() * x.den()
```

This is exact: only whole numbers, no rounding. It needs the denominators to be [[positive|cross-multiply]], which your invariant already promises.

C++20 can also generate all six comparisons, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `==` and `!=`, from one operator, `operator<=>`, nicknamed the [[spaceship operator|spaceship]]. But writing `==` and `<` yourself first is the best way to see what they mean.

**Watch out:** a simple `==` that compares the parts is only right because every `Fraction` is reduced. If the constructor skips the reducing, `Fraction(2, 4) == Fraction(1, 2)` is false although both are one half. Make every new fraction, including every operator's answer, go through the constructor, so no unreduced fraction can exist at all.

::: context operator-call What a + b really is
To the compiler, `v + w` is a call to a function named `operator+`. You can even write the call out in full, `operator+(v, w)`, and it does the same thing. There are limits. You cannot invent new symbols, like `**` for powers. You cannot change how many sides an operator has, or which one goes first: `a + b * c` still does the `*` first, whatever your types are. And at least one side must be a class or an enum, so nobody can change what `1 + 2` means.
:::

::: context ostream-family One function for every output stream
`std::cout` is a `std::ostream`. So is a `std::ostringstream`, the string stream from the intermediate course, and so is a `std::ofstream`, which writes to a file. The last two are kinds of `std::ostream`, built from it; the virtual functions lesson shows how one class is built from another. That is why one `operator<<` taking `std::ostream&` works for all of them. The stream is passed and returned by reference because a stream cannot be copied: two copies of `std::cout` writing to one screen would make no sense.
:::

::: context left-side Why the left side is never converted
For a member function, `half + 1` means `half.operator+(1)`: the left side is the object the function belongs to. To call a member function, C++ must already have an object of the right class; it will not build a temporary `Fraction` out of `1` only to find a member on it. The argument in the parentheses is different: it is an ordinary parameter, and ordinary parameters get conversions. A non-member puts both sides in the parentheses, so both get the same treatment. Mathematics says `1 + half` and `half + 1` are equal, and the non-member version keeps that true.
:::

::: context gcd The oldest algorithm still in use
`std::gcd` uses the idea in Euclid's *Elements*, written about 300 BC. Replace the pair (a, b) with (b, a % b), and repeat until the second number is 0; the first is then the answer. For 48 and 18: 48 % 18 is 12, 18 % 12 is 6, 12 % 6 is 0, so the answer is 6.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="30" width="72" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="46" y="52" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">48, 18</text>
  <rect x="102" y="30" width="72" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="138" y="52" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">18, 12</text>
  <rect x="194" y="30" width="72" height="34" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="230" y="52" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">12, 6</text>
  <rect x="286" y="30" width="64" height="34" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="318" y="52" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">6, 0</text>
  <line x1="82" y1="47" x2="100" y2="47" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="174" y1="47" x2="192" y2="47" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="266" y1="47" x2="284" y2="47" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="91" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">% gives 12</text>
  <text x="183" y="86" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">% gives 6</text>
  <text x="275" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">% gives 0</text>
  <text x="318" y="86" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">gcd = 6</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context overflow Big enough, until it is not
Cross-multiplying and adding make numbers grow. The largest `long long` is 9,223,372,036,854,775,807, about 9.2 × 10¹⁸, and a product of two parts near 10¹⁰ passes it. Signed overflow is undefined behavior, as the overflow lesson in the intermediate course showed. Real software has failed this way: in 1996, Ariane 5 flight 501 broke up about 37 seconds after lift-off when a 64-bit floating-point value was converted to a 16-bit whole number too small to hold it. For the numbers in this lesson, `long long` is plenty.
:::

::: context cross-multiply Why the denominators must be positive
Cross-multiplying is multiplying both sides of a/b < c/d by b and by d. Multiplying both sides of an inequality by a positive number keeps it true. Multiplying by a negative number flips it: 2 < 3, but −2 > −3. So with a negative denominator the test would give the wrong answer. Try 1/−2 against 1/3: −1/2 is less than 1/3, but 1·3 < 1·(−2) says 3 < −2, which is false. The positive-denominator invariant means you never have to think about this again.
:::

::: context spaceship The spaceship operator
`<=>` is officially the **three-way comparison** operator, nicknamed "spaceship" for its shape. It answers "less, equal or greater" in one go, and from it the compiler can write the four ordering comparisons for you; asking for a default one, `auto operator<=>(const Fraction&) const = default;`, gives `==` and `!=` as well. For a fraction the default would compare `num_` first and then `den_`. That is ordering by parts, not by value, and it would say 1/2 < 1/3, because the tops are equal and 2 < 3. That is exactly why you write `<` yourself here.
:::
--- task
Finish `class Fraction`. No `main`: the checker supplies it. The starter has the constructor, `num()` and `den()`, but it stores the numbers exactly as given and has no operators.

- `Fraction(long long num = 0, long long den = 1)`, **not** `explicit`, stores the fraction in lowest terms with a positive denominator. `den` is never 0. So `Fraction(6, -4)` has `num()` −3 and `den()` 2, and `Fraction(0, 5)` has `den()` 1.
- Non-member operators `+`, `-`, `*` and `/` that each return a new `Fraction`. For `/`, the fraction you divide by is never zero. `Fraction(1, 2) + Fraction(1, 3)` is 5/6, and `1 + Fraction(1, 2)` must work too.
- Non-member `==` and `<`, each returning a `bool`. `<` compares by value: `Fraction(1, 3) < Fraction(1, 2)` is true.
- A non-member `operator<<` that prints `num/den`, like `-3/2`, or only `num` when the denominator is 1, like `2`. It returns the stream.
--- starter
#include <numeric>
#include <ostream>

class Fraction {
public:
    Fraction(long long num = 0, long long den = 1) : num_(num), den_(den) {}
    long long num() const { return num_; }
    long long den() const { return den_; }
private:
    long long num_;
    long long den_;
};
--- solution
#include <numeric>
#include <ostream>

class Fraction {
public:
    Fraction(long long num = 0, long long den = 1) : num_(num), den_(den) {
        if (den_ < 0) {
            num_ = -num_;
            den_ = -den_;
        }
        long long g = std::gcd(num_, den_);
        if (g > 1) {
            num_ /= g;
            den_ /= g;
        }
    }
    long long num() const { return num_; }
    long long den() const { return den_; }
private:
    long long num_;
    long long den_;
};

Fraction operator+(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b) {
    return Fraction(a.num() * b.den() + b.num() * a.den(), a.den() * b.den());
}
Fraction operator-(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b) {
    return Fraction(a.num() * b.den() - b.num() * a.den(), a.den() * b.den());
}
Fraction operator*(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b) {
    return Fraction(a.num() * b.num(), a.den() * b.den());
}
Fraction operator/(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b) {
    return Fraction(a.num() * b.den(), a.den() * b.num());
}
bool operator==(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b) {
    return a.num() == b.num() && a.den() == b.den();
}
bool operator<(const Fraction& a, const Fraction& b) {
    return a.num() * b.den() < b.num() * a.den();
}
std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Fraction& f) {
    out << f.num();
    if (f.den() != 1) out << "/" << f.den();
    return out;
}
--- hint
Put the invariant in the constructor's body: if `den_` is negative, flip the signs of both `num_` and `den_`; then divide both by `std::gcd(num_, den_)` when it is bigger than 1.
--- hint
Each arithmetic operator returns a new `Fraction` built from the school formulas and lets the constructor reduce it. For `+`: `return Fraction(a.num() * b.den() + b.num() * a.den(), a.den() * b.den());`.
--- hint
Because every fraction is stored reduced, `==` compares `num()` with `num()` and `den()` with `den()`. `<` cross-multiplies: `a.num() * b.den() < b.num() * a.den()`. In `operator<<`, print `f.num()`, then `"/"` and `f.den()` only if `f.den() != 1`, and `return out;`.
--- check test | Stored in lowest terms with a positive denominator
Fraction(6, -4).num() == -3 && Fraction(6, -4).den() == 2 && Fraction(0, 5).den() == 1
--- check case | 1/2 + 1/3 is 5/6
Fraction(1, 2) + Fraction(1, 3)
=> Fraction(5, 6)
--- check case | Subtraction can go negative
Fraction(1, 4) - Fraction(1, 2)
=> Fraction(-1, 4)
--- check case | Multiply and divide
(Fraction(2, 3) * Fraction(3, 4)) / Fraction(1, 2)
=> Fraction(1, 1)
--- check test | A whole number on the left converts
1 + Fraction(1, 2) == Fraction(3, 2)
--- check test | Ordering by value, not by parts
Fraction(1, 3) < Fraction(1, 2) && !(Fraction(1, 2) < Fraction(2, 4)) && Fraction(-1, 2) < Fraction(1, 100)
--- check case | Printing, including whole numbers
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Fraction(6, -4) << " " << Fraction(8, 4) << " " << Fraction(0, 3); return o.str(); }()
=> "-3/2 2 0"

+++ practice | Money that adds and prints itself
--- task
The starter's `struct Money` holds an amount in whole cents, so $12.05 is `Money{1205}`. Give it non-member operators. No `main`.

- `+` and `-` return a new `Money`.
- `==` and `<` compare the amounts and return a `bool`.
- `operator<<` prints dollars, a dot and exactly two digits of cents, with a `$` in front: `Money{1205}` prints `$12.05`, `Money{7}` prints `$0.07` and `Money{100}` prints `$1.00`. A negative amount starts with a minus sign before the `$`: `Money{-50}` prints `-$0.50`. It returns the stream.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <ostream>
#include <vector>

struct Money {
    long long cents;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <ostream>
#include <vector>

struct Money {
    long long cents;
};

Money operator+(const Money& a, const Money& b) { return {a.cents + b.cents}; }
Money operator-(const Money& a, const Money& b) { return {a.cents - b.cents}; }
bool operator==(const Money& a, const Money& b) { return a.cents == b.cents; }
bool operator<(const Money& a, const Money& b) { return a.cents < b.cents; }

std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Money& m) {
    long long c = m.cents;
    if (c < 0) {
        out << "-";
        c = -c;
    }
    out << "$" << c / 100 << ".";
    if (c % 100 < 10) out << "0";
    return out << c % 100;
}
--- hint
Each operator is a function outside the struct named `operator` plus the symbol, taking two `const Money&`. The arithmetic ones return `{a.cents + b.cents}`.
--- hint
In `operator<<`, work with the size of the amount: print `-` and flip the sign first if it is negative. Then dollars are `c / 100` and cents `c % 100`; print an extra `0` when the cents are below 10.
--- check case | Printing ordinary amounts
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Money{1205} << " " << Money{7} << " " << Money{100} << " " << Money{0}; return o.str(); }()
=> "$12.05 $0.07 $1.00 $0.00"
--- check case | Printing negative amounts
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Money{-50} << " " << Money{-1999}; return o.str(); }()
=> "-$0.50 -$19.99"
--- check test | Adding, subtracting and comparing
Money{250} + Money{75} == Money{325} && Money{100} - Money{150} == Money{-50} && Money{99} < Money{100} && !(Money{5} < Money{5})
--- check test | std::sort uses your <
[] { std::vector<Money> v{{300}, {-20}, {45}}; std::sort(v.begin(), v.end()); return v[0] == Money{-20} && v[2] == Money{300}; }()

+++ practice | A 3D vector that scales both ways
--- task
Write `struct Vec3 { double x, y, z; };` and these non-member operators. No `main`.

- `+` and `-` work part by part: `Vec3{1, 2, 3} + Vec3{1, 1, 1}` is `Vec3{2, 3, 4}`.
- Scaling by a number works with the number on **either** side: `Vec3{1, 2, 3} * 2.0` and `2.0 * Vec3{1, 2, 3}` are both `Vec3{2, 4, 6}`. That needs two `operator*` functions.
- `==` is true when all three parts are equal.
- `operator<<` prints `(x, y, z)` with the default number format, like `(2, 4, 6)` or `(0.5, -1, 3)`.
--- starter
#include <ostream>

struct Vec3 {
    double x, y, z;
};

Vec3 operator*(const Vec3& v, double k) {
    return {v.x * k, v.y * k, v.z * k};
}
--- solution
#include <ostream>

struct Vec3 {
    double x, y, z;
};

Vec3 operator+(const Vec3& a, const Vec3& b) { return {a.x + b.x, a.y + b.y, a.z + b.z}; }
Vec3 operator-(const Vec3& a, const Vec3& b) { return {a.x - b.x, a.y - b.y, a.z - b.z}; }
Vec3 operator*(const Vec3& v, double k) { return {v.x * k, v.y * k, v.z * k}; }
Vec3 operator*(double k, const Vec3& v) { return v * k; }
bool operator==(const Vec3& a, const Vec3& b) { return a.x == b.x && a.y == b.y && a.z == b.z; }

std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Vec3& v) {
    return out << "(" << v.x << ", " << v.y << ", " << v.z << ")";
}
--- hint
`2.0 * v` has the number on the left, so it calls `operator*(double, const Vec3&)`, a different function from the one in the starter. Write it too; it can simply return `v * k`.
--- hint
`operator<<` returns `out << "(" << v.x << ", " << v.y << ", " << v.z << ")";`, which prints and hands back the stream in one line.
--- check test | Adding and subtracting
Vec3{1, 2, 3} + Vec3{1, 1, 1} == Vec3{2, 3, 4} && Vec3{1, 2, 3} - Vec3{1, 2, 3} == Vec3{0, 0, 0}
--- check test | Scaling with the number on either side
Vec3{1, 2, 3} * 2.0 == Vec3{2, 4, 6} && 2.0 * Vec3{1, 2, 3} == Vec3{2, 4, 6} && -1.0 * Vec3{1, 0, -2} == Vec3{-1, 0, 2}
--- check case | Printing
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Vec3{0.5, -1, 3} << " " << 2.0 * Vec3{1, 2, 3}; return o.str(); }()
=> "(0.5, -1, 3) (2, 4, 6)"
--- check test | != comes free with ==
Vec3{1, 2, 3} != Vec3{1, 2, 4} && !(Vec3{1, 2, 3} != Vec3{1, 2, 3})

+++ practice | Version numbers that sort correctly
--- task
Software versions like `1.10.0` sort by number, part by part, not as text: `1.9.9` comes before `1.10.0`. Write `struct Version { int major, minor, patch; };` with these, all outside the struct. No `main`.

- `==` and `<`: `<` compares `major` first, then `minor` when the majors are equal, then `patch`.
- `operator<<` prints `major.minor.patch`, like `1.10.0`.
- `std::optional<Version> parse_version(const std::string& text)` reads text like `"1.4.2"`: three whole numbers, none negative, separated by single dots, with nothing else before, between or after. Anything else gives `std::nullopt`: `"1.2"`, `"1.2.3.4"`, `"a.b.c"`, `"1.2.3x"`, `"1.-2.3"`.
- `std::vector<Version> sorted(std::vector<Version> v)` returns the versions in order, oldest first, using `std::sort` with your `<`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <optional>
#include <ostream>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Version {
    int major, minor, patch;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <optional>
#include <ostream>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Version {
    int major, minor, patch;
};

bool operator==(const Version& a, const Version& b) {
    return a.major == b.major && a.minor == b.minor && a.patch == b.patch;
}

bool operator<(const Version& a, const Version& b) {
    if (a.major != b.major) return a.major < b.major;
    if (a.minor != b.minor) return a.minor < b.minor;
    return a.patch < b.patch;
}

std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Version& v) {
    return out << v.major << "." << v.minor << "." << v.patch;
}

std::optional<Version> parse_version(const std::string& text) {
    std::istringstream in(text);
    Version v;
    char dot1 = ' ', dot2 = ' ';
    if (!(in >> v.major >> dot1 >> v.minor >> dot2 >> v.patch)) return std::nullopt;
    if (dot1 != '.' || dot2 != '.') return std::nullopt;
    if (v.major < 0 || v.minor < 0 || v.patch < 0) return std::nullopt;
    char extra;
    if (in >> extra) return std::nullopt;
    return v;
}

std::vector<Version> sorted(std::vector<Version> v) {
    std::sort(v.begin(), v.end());
    return v;
}
--- hint
`<` goes key by key: if the majors differ, they decide; otherwise if the minors differ, they decide; otherwise the patches decide.
--- hint
To parse, read `int`, `char`, `int`, `char`, `int` from a `std::istringstream`, then check both chars are `'.'`, no number is negative, and that reading one more `char` fails, which means nothing is left over.
--- check test | Ordering by number, not by text
Version{1, 9, 9} < Version{1, 10, 0} && Version{0, 9, 0} < Version{1, 0, 0} && !(Version{2, 0, 1} < Version{2, 0, 1}) && Version{2, 0, 0} == Version{2, 0, 0}
--- check test | Parsing good text
parse_version("1.4.2") == Version{1, 4, 2} && parse_version("0.0.0") == Version{0, 0, 0} && parse_version("10.20.30") == Version{10, 20, 30}
--- check test | Parsing bad text
!parse_version("1.2") && !parse_version("1.2.3.4") && !parse_version("a.b.c") && !parse_version("1.2.3x") && !parse_version("1.-2.3") && !parse_version("")
--- check case | Sorting and printing
[] { std::ostringstream o; for (const Version& v : sorted({{1, 10, 0}, {1, 2, 3}, {0, 9, 12}, {1, 9, 9}})) o << v << " "; return o.str(); }()
=> "0.9.12 1.2.3 1.9.9 1.10.0 "

+++ practice | An angle that always stays in range
--- task
Write `class Angle`, a whole number of degrees that keeps one invariant: it is always stored from 0 up to 359. No `main`.

- `Angle(int degrees = 0)`, **not** `explicit`, stores the angle turned into that range: `Angle(-90)` is 270, `Angle(720)` is 0, `Angle(-1)` is 359 and `Angle(365)` is 5. It must work for any `int`, even the most negative one.
- `int degrees() const` returns the stored value.
- Non-member `+` and `-` return a new `Angle`: `Angle(350) + Angle(20)` is 10, `Angle(10) - Angle(20)` is 350. `Angle(350) + 20` and `20 + Angle(350)` must work too.
- Non-member `==`, and `operator<<` that prints like `270deg`.
--- starter
#include <ostream>

class Angle {
public:
    Angle(int degrees = 0) : deg_(degrees % 360) {}
    int degrees() const { return deg_; }
private:
    int deg_;
};
--- solution
#include <ostream>

class Angle {
public:
    Angle(int degrees = 0) : deg_((degrees % 360 + 360) % 360) {}
    int degrees() const { return deg_; }
private:
    int deg_;
};

Angle operator+(const Angle& a, const Angle& b) { return Angle(a.degrees() + b.degrees()); }
Angle operator-(const Angle& a, const Angle& b) { return Angle(a.degrees() - b.degrees()); }
bool operator==(const Angle& a, const Angle& b) { return a.degrees() == b.degrees(); }

std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Angle& a) {
    return out << a.degrees() << "deg";
}
--- hint
In C++, `%` keeps the sign of the left side: `-90 % 360` is `-90`, not 270. Adding 360 to that and taking `% 360` once more lands every value from 0 to 359, and never overflows, because `degrees % 360` is already small.
--- hint
Let the constructor do all the tidying. The operators just build `Angle(a.degrees() + b.degrees())` and so on; written outside the class, both sides can be converted from an `int`.
--- check test | Negative angles wrap round
Angle(-90).degrees() == 270 && Angle(-1).degrees() == 359 && Angle(-360).degrees() == 0 && Angle(-725).degrees() == 355
--- check test | Big angles wrap round
Angle(720).degrees() == 0 && Angle(365).degrees() == 5 && Angle(2147483647).degrees() == 127 && Angle(-2147483647 - 1).degrees() == 232
--- check test | Adding and subtracting past the ends
Angle(350) + Angle(20) == Angle(10) && Angle(10) - Angle(20) == Angle(350) && Angle(350) + 20 == Angle(10) && 20 + Angle(350) == Angle(10)
--- check case | Printing
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Angle(-90) << " " << Angle(); return o.str(); }()
=> "270deg 0deg"

+++ practice | Debug: minus one half is not less than zero
--- task
**Bug report:** "`Ratio(1, -2) == Ratio(-1, 2)` is `false`, and `Ratio(1, -2) < Ratio(0, 1)` is `false`, although minus one half is less than zero."

`Ratio` is meant to keep the same invariant as the lesson's `Fraction`: every ratio stored in lowest terms **with a positive denominator**. The operators rely on it: `==` compares the parts, and `<` cross-multiplies, which is only right when both denominators are positive. Find what the constructor forgets and fix it. `den` is never 0. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <numeric>

class Ratio {
public:
    Ratio(long long num = 0, long long den = 1) : num_(num), den_(den) {
        long long g = std::gcd(num_, den_);
        if (g > 1) {
            num_ /= g;
            den_ /= g;
        }
    }
    long long num() const { return num_; }
    long long den() const { return den_; }
private:
    long long num_;
    long long den_;
};

bool operator==(const Ratio& a, const Ratio& b) { return a.num() == b.num() && a.den() == b.den(); }
bool operator<(const Ratio& a, const Ratio& b) { return a.num() * b.den() < b.num() * a.den(); }
--- solution
#include <numeric>

class Ratio {
public:
    Ratio(long long num = 0, long long den = 1) : num_(num), den_(den) {
        if (den_ < 0) {
            num_ = -num_;
            den_ = -den_;
        }
        long long g = std::gcd(num_, den_);
        if (g > 1) {
            num_ /= g;
            den_ /= g;
        }
    }
    long long num() const { return num_; }
    long long den() const { return den_; }
private:
    long long num_;
    long long den_;
};

bool operator==(const Ratio& a, const Ratio& b) { return a.num() == b.num() && a.den() == b.den(); }
bool operator<(const Ratio& a, const Ratio& b) { return a.num() * b.den() < b.num() * a.den(); }
--- hint
Print the parts of `Ratio(1, -2)`. Which part carries the minus sign, and which part should?
--- hint
Before reducing, if `den_` is negative, flip the sign of both `num_` and `den_`. That does not change the value, and it makes the denominator positive.
--- check test | The sign moves to the top
Ratio(1, -2).num() == -1 && Ratio(1, -2).den() == 2 && Ratio(-3, -6).num() == 1 && Ratio(-3, -6).den() == 2
--- check test | Equal values compare equal
Ratio(1, -2) == Ratio(-1, 2) && Ratio(4, -8) == Ratio(-1, 2)
--- check test | Ordering with negative denominators
Ratio(1, -2) < Ratio(0, 1) && Ratio(-1, 3) < Ratio(1, -4) && !(Ratio(0, 1) < Ratio(1, -2))

+++ practice | Stretch: polynomials
--- task
Write `class Poly`, a polynomial with whole-number coefficients, like 3x² + 2x − 1. It keeps its coefficients in a `std::vector<long long>` where index `i` holds the coefficient of xⁱ, so 3x² + 2x − 1 is `{-1, 2, 3}`. Invariant: the last coefficient is never 0, so the zero polynomial is an empty vector. No `main`.

- `explicit Poly(std::vector<long long> coefs)` stores the coefficients, dropping any zeros at the end.
- `const std::vector<long long>& coefs() const` returns them, and `long long eval(long long x) const` works out the value at `x`.
- Non-member `+` and `*` return a new `Poly`, and `==` compares coefficients.
- `operator<<` prints terms from the highest power down, leaving out zero terms. A term is the coefficient, then `x` for power 1 or `x^k` for power k of 2 or more. A coefficient of 1 or −1 is written without the 1, except in the constant term. The first term carries a `-` right in front if it is negative; each later term is joined by `" + "` or `" - "` and written without its sign. The zero polynomial prints `0`.

So `{-1, 2, 3}` prints `3x^2 + 2x - 1`, `{0, -1}` prints `-x`, `{1, 0, -1}` prints `-x^2 + 1`, and `{5}` prints `5`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <ostream>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Poly {
public:
    explicit Poly(std::vector<long long> coefs) : c_(std::move(coefs)) {}
    const std::vector<long long>& coefs() const { return c_; }
    long long eval(long long x) const { return 0; }
private:
    std::vector<long long> c_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <ostream>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Poly {
public:
    explicit Poly(std::vector<long long> coefs) : c_(std::move(coefs)) {
        while (!c_.empty() && c_.back() == 0) c_.pop_back();
    }
    const std::vector<long long>& coefs() const { return c_; }
    long long eval(long long x) const {
        long long total = 0;
        for (std::size_t i = c_.size(); i > 0; --i) total = total * x + c_[i - 1];
        return total;
    }
private:
    std::vector<long long> c_;
};

Poly operator+(const Poly& a, const Poly& b) {
    const std::vector<long long>& x = a.coefs();
    const std::vector<long long>& y = b.coefs();
    std::vector<long long> sum(x.size() > y.size() ? x.size() : y.size(), 0);
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < x.size(); ++i) sum[i] += x[i];
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < y.size(); ++i) sum[i] += y[i];
    return Poly(sum);
}

Poly operator*(const Poly& a, const Poly& b) {
    const std::vector<long long>& x = a.coefs();
    const std::vector<long long>& y = b.coefs();
    if (x.empty() || y.empty()) return Poly({});
    std::vector<long long> product(x.size() + y.size() - 1, 0);
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < x.size(); ++i) {
        for (std::size_t j = 0; j < y.size(); ++j) product[i + j] += x[i] * y[j];
    }
    return Poly(product);
}

bool operator==(const Poly& a, const Poly& b) { return a.coefs() == b.coefs(); }

std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Poly& p) {
    const std::vector<long long>& c = p.coefs();
    if (c.empty()) return out << "0";
    bool first = true;
    for (std::size_t k = c.size(); k > 0; --k) {
        std::size_t power = k - 1;
        long long coef = c[power];
        if (coef == 0) continue;
        long long size = coef < 0 ? -coef : coef;
        if (first) {
            if (coef < 0) out << "-";
        } else {
            out << (coef < 0 ? " - " : " + ");
        }
        if (size != 1 || power == 0) out << size;
        if (power == 1) out << "x";
        if (power >= 2) out << "x^" << power;
        first = false;
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
The constructor keeps the invariant: `while (!c_.empty() && c_.back() == 0) c_.pop_back();`. Every operator builds its answer as a vector and passes it to the constructor, so every answer is trimmed too.
--- hint
For `*`, the product of the xⁱ term of one and the xʲ term of the other lands at power `i + j`, so a double loop adds `x[i] * y[j]` into `product[i + j]`. For `eval`, start from the highest coefficient and repeat `total = total * x + next`.
--- hint
In `operator<<`, walk the powers from the top down, skipping zeros. Print the sign part first (a bare `-` for the first term, `" + "` or `" - "` for later ones), then the size of the coefficient unless it is 1 with a power above 0, then `x` or `x^k`.
--- check case | Printing the example polynomials
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Poly({-1, 2, 3}) << " | " << Poly({0, -1}) << " | " << Poly({1, 0, -1}) << " | " << Poly({5}) << " | " << Poly({0, 0, 1}); return o.str(); }()
=> "3x^2 + 2x - 1 | -x | -x^2 + 1 | 5 | x^2"
--- check case | Zeros at the end are dropped, and zero prints 0
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Poly({0, 0, 0}) << " " << Poly({}) << " " << Poly({4, 0, 0}).coefs().size(); return o.str(); }()
=> "0 0 1"
--- check test | Adding can cancel the top term
Poly({1, 2, 3}) + Poly({0, 0, -3}) == Poly({1, 2}) && (Poly({1, 1}) + Poly({-1, -1})).coefs().empty()
--- check test | Multiplying: (x + 1)(x - 1) is x^2 - 1
Poly({1, 1}) * Poly({-1, 1}) == Poly({-1, 0, 1}) && (Poly({2}) * Poly({})).coefs().empty()
--- check test | eval
Poly({-1, 2, 3}).eval(2) == 15 && Poly({-1, 2, 3}).eval(0) == -1 && Poly({}).eval(9) == 0 && Poly({0, 0, 0, 1}).eval(-3) == -27

=== cpp3-08 | Virtual functions and interfaces
--- teach
Last lesson your `Fraction` behaved like a built-in number. A template, like your `Stack<T>`, works for many types, but each `Stack<int>` or `Stack<std::string>` is its own class, fixed while compiling, and holds only one type. Sometimes one piece of code must handle a **mix** of kinds at once: "add up the area of these shapes", whatever shapes they turn out to be.

Picture a spacecraft's power system asking every device on board, "how much power do you need?" The camera, the heater and the radio each work out their answer in their own way. The question is the same; the answers depend on who is asked. Code that asks one question and gets the right answer from each kind of object, chosen while the program runs, is called **runtime [[polymorphism|polymorphism-word]]**. In C++ it takes three ingredients: a base class, classes built on it, and calls made through a pointer or reference. This lesson adds them one at a time.

### One class built on another

A class can be built on top of another one, taking everything it has and adding more:

```cpp
class Vehicle {
public:
    std::string id() const { return "V-1"; }
};

class Rover : public Vehicle {
public:
    int wheels() const { return 6; }
};

Rover r;
r.id();       // "V-1": Rover got this from Vehicle
r.wheels();   // 6: Rover's own
```

Read `class Rover : public Vehicle` as "a Rover is a kind of Vehicle". `Rover` **derives from** `Vehicle`: it has every member `Vehicle` has, plus its own. `Vehicle` is the **base class**, and `Rover` is a **derived class**. The word `public` there means the whole program may [[treat a Rover as a Vehicle|is-a]].

### A base reference can refer to a derived object

Because a `Rover` is a kind of `Vehicle`, a `Vehicle` reference or pointer may refer to one:

```cpp
Vehicle& v = r;    // v refers to the Rover r
Vehicle* p = &r;   // p points at it
```

This is what lets one piece of code take "any vehicle": it takes a `Vehicle&`, and you hand it a rover, a lander, whatever derives from `Vehicle`.

### Without virtual, the base version runs

Now give both classes a `name()` and call it through a base reference:

```cpp
class Shape {
public:
    std::string name() const { return "shape"; }
};

class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    std::string name() const { return "circle"; }
};

Circle c;
const Shape& s = c;
s.name();   // "shape"! The compiler only knows s is a Shape.
```

The compiler picks the function from the type it can see, `Shape`, while compiling. It never looks at what `s` really refers to. The task's starter behaves exactly like this.

### virtual: ask the real object

Mark the function **`virtual`** in the base class:

```cpp
class Shape {
public:
    virtual std::string name() const { return "shape"; }
};
```

Now `s.name()` gives `"circle"`. A **virtual function** is one where a call through a base pointer or reference runs the version for the object's *real* type, [[looked up while the program runs|vtable]]. That is the whole trick of runtime polymorphism.

### override: let the compiler check your spelling

In the derived class, write **`override`** after the function's parameters:

```cpp
class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    std::string name() const override { return "circle"; }
};
```

`override` asks the compiler to check that there really is a virtual function with exactly this name, these parameters and this `const` in the base. Misspell the name, or forget the `const`, and it is a compile error, "marked 'override', but does not override". Without `override`, the mistake would compile quietly as a [[brand-new function|override-history]] that no call through a `Shape` ever reaches.

### Pure virtual: a promise with no body

Some base classes have no sensible answer of their own. What is the area of "a shape"? Write `= 0` instead of a body:

```cpp
class Shape {
public:
    virtual ~Shape() = default;               // essential, see below
    virtual double area() const = 0;
    virtual std::string name() const = 0;
};
```

Read `= 0` as "no body here: every derived class must supply one". A function like that is **pure virtual**. A class with one or more is **abstract**: you cannot make an object of it, so `Shape s;` does not compile. A base class made of pure virtual functions is an **interface**: it only states what every shape can do, and each derived class says how. This is how [[flight software is often organized|interfaces-in-flight]].

### Implementing the interface

A class that derives from `Shape` and overrides every pure virtual function is a real, complete class you can create:

```cpp
class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    explicit Circle(double r) : r_(r) {}
    double area() const override { return 3.141592653589793 * r_ * r_; }
    std::string name() const override { return "circle"; }
private:
    double r_;
};
```

A derived class that leaves any pure virtual function without a body is still abstract, and still cannot be created.

### A virtual destructor

The line `virtual ~Shape() = default;` in the interface is essential. `= default` means "the compiler writes the usual body", which here is empty. The `virtual` is what matters.

You will hold shapes as `std::unique_ptr<Shape>`, and a `unique_ptr<Shape>` deletes its object through a `Shape*`. With a **virtual destructor** in the base, deleting a `Circle` through a `Shape*` runs `Circle`'s destructor too, then `Shape`'s. Without it, that delete is [[undefined behavior|virtual-destructor]]. The rule: a class meant to be used through base pointers gets a virtual destructor.

### Deriving from a derived class

A derived class can itself be a base. A square *is a* rectangle with equal sides, so `Square` can derive from `Rectangle`, reuse its constructor, and override only `name()`. A derived constructor passes arguments to its base's constructor in the initializer list, where members usually go:

```cpp
class Square : public Rectangle {
public:
    explicit Square(double side) : Rectangle(side, side) {}
    std::string name() const override { return "square"; }
};
```

`Square` gets `Rectangle`'s `area()` and `perimeter()` without writing them again: a 4 by 4 rectangle already knows its area is 16.

### A collection of mixed shapes

Now the payoff. One vector holds circles, rectangles and squares side by side, each owned by a `std::unique_ptr<Shape>`:

```cpp
std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> shapes;
shapes.push_back(std::make_unique<Circle>(1.0));   // a unique_ptr<Circle> becomes a unique_ptr<Shape>
shapes.push_back(std::make_unique<Square>(3.0));

double total = 0;
for (const auto& s : shapes) total += s->area();   // Circle::area, then Rectangle::area
```

`s->area()` reads through the pointer, as in the smart pointers lesson, and `virtual` makes it run the right `area` for each shape. To keep track of one shape without owning it, use a plain pointer: `s.get()` gives the `Shape*` a `unique_ptr` holds, as in `const Shape* best = s.get();`.

**Watch out:** if you forget `virtual` in the base class, everything still compiles, and every call through a `Shape` quietly runs the base version: the starter's `"shape"` and `0`. Your guard is `override`. Put it on every function in a derived class that is meant to replace a base one; if the base function is not virtual, or the two do not match exactly, the compiler stops you. The next lesson shows one more way a derived object can [[lose its real type|square-rectangle]].

::: context polymorphism-word Many shapes
"Polymorphism" is Greek for "many shapes": *poly*, many, and *morphe*, shape. One call, `s->area()`, takes many shapes of behavior depending on the object. C++ has two kinds. Templates give **compile-time** polymorphism: the compiler makes a separate function for each type, and each call is fixed before the program runs. Virtual functions give **runtime** polymorphism: one function call, and the choice is made each time it runs. Use the first when the types are known while compiling, and the second when a collection mixes kinds.
:::

::: context is-a "Is a kind of"
Deriving says "every Rover is a Vehicle". Test a design by saying it aloud: "a circle is a shape" is true, so `Circle` may derive from `Shape`. "A rover is a wheel" is false: a rover *has* wheels, so a rover should hold them as members instead. The `public` in `: public Vehicle` makes the "is a" relationship visible to everyone, so a `Rover` can be passed wherever a `Vehicle&` is wanted. C++ also has `private` inheritance, where only the class itself knows, but it is rare; nearly every base you meet is public. The task's classes make a small family tree, each arrow pointing from a class to its base:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="130" y="10" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="6 4"/>
  <text x="180" y="31" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Shape</text>
  <rect x="30" y="72" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="93" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Circle</text>
  <rect x="230" y="72" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

```
:::

::: context vtable How a virtual call finds its function
The C++ standard does not say how, but every major compiler does it in the same basic way. Each class with virtual functions gets a table of the addresses of its versions, the **vtable**. Each object carries a hidden pointer to its class's table. A virtual call follows the object's pointer to the table and calls the address found there. That is why a virtual call costs a little more than a plain one, and why `sizeof` grows: with g++ on a 64-bit machine, a struct holding one `double` is 8 bytes, and 16 once it has a virtual function.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="40" width="100" height="70" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="60" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">a Circle object</text>
  <rect x="20" y="50" width="80" height="24" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="60" y="66" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">hidden ptr</text>
  <rect x="20" y="78" width="80" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="60" y="94" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">r_ = 1.0</text>
  <line x1="100" y1="62" x2="160" y2="62" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="158,57 168,62 158,67" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="170" y="40" width="180" height="84" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="260" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Circle's vtable</text>
  <text x="182" y="62" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">~Circle</text>
  <text x="182" y="84" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">area   → Circle::area</text>
  <text x="182" y="106" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">name → Circle::name</text>
  <text x="180" y="148" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">every Circle shares one table; a Square points at Square's</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context override-history A keyword added after the bugs
`override` arrived in C++11, along with `final`, which forbids any further overriding. Before that, a derived function that did not quite match its base, a missing `const` or an `int` where the base had a `long`, compiled as a separate function with the same name. Every call through a base pointer then ran the base version, and nothing said why. Large codebases collected these bugs for years. Many coding standards now require `override` on every overriding function, and compilers can warn when it is missing.
:::

::: context interfaces-in-flight Interfaces in real flight software
NASA JPL's open-source flight software framework, F Prime, flew on the Ingenuity helicopter on Mars. It is written in C++, and its pieces are built around this pattern: a tool generates a base class for each software component, and engineers write a class that derives from it and fills in its virtual functions. Components talk to each other only through typed connections called ports, never by reaching inside each other. So a component can be swapped for another with the same ports, for example a simulated sensor during ground tests, without changing the rest.
:::

::: context virtual-destructor Half a destruction
Without a virtual destructor, deleting through a `Shape*` picks the destructor the way a non-virtual call does: from the type the compiler sees. So only `~Shape` would be considered, and whatever `Circle` owns, a buffer or a file, would never be released. The standard goes further and calls the whole delete undefined behavior, so anything can happen. With `-Wall`, compilers warn in some of these cases, for example clang when a `unique_ptr` deletes such a class, but not all of them, so do not count on a warning. Write the virtual destructor.
:::

::: context square-rectangle Where this comes back
The next lesson is about **object slicing**: copying a derived object into a variable of the base type keeps only the base part, and virtual calls stop working. That is one reason polymorphic objects live behind pointers, as in your vector of `unique_ptr<Shape>`. The square-is-a-rectangle design also has a famous catch. It works here because a shape never changes after it is made. Add `set_width` to `Rectangle`, and a `Square` could be given a width different from its height, breaking what a square is. The design lesson at the end of this course, "composition over inheritance", takes that up.
:::
--- task
Complete the shapes. No `main`: the checker supplies it. Use `3.141592653589793` for π.

- `Shape` is the interface in the starter. Make `area()`, `perimeter()` and `name()` pure virtual (keep them `const`), and give `Shape` a virtual destructor.
- `Circle(double r)` derives from `Shape`. Its area is π·r·r, its perimeter 2·π·r, and its name `"circle"`.
- `Rectangle(double w, double h)` derives from `Shape`. Its area is w·h, its perimeter 2·(w + h), and its name `"rectangle"`.
- `Square(double side)` derives from `Rectangle`, and its name is `"square"`. A `Square(4)` has area 16 and perimeter 16.
- `double total_area(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes)` returns the sum of all their areas (0 for an empty vector).
- `std::string largest_name(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes)` returns the name of the shape with the largest area. On a tie, the first of them wins. With no shapes, it returns `""`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

class Shape {
public:
    double area() const { return 0; }
    double perimeter() const { return 0; }
    std::string name() const { return "shape"; }
};
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

class Shape {
public:
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
    virtual double area() const = 0;
    virtual double perimeter() const = 0;
    virtual std::string name() const = 0;
};

constexpr double kPi = 3.141592653589793;

class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    explicit Circle(double r) : r_(r) {}
    double area() const override { return kPi * r_ * r_; }
    double perimeter() const override { return 2 * kPi * r_; }
    std::string name() const override { return "circle"; }
private:
    double r_;
};

class Rectangle : public Shape {
public:
    Rectangle(double w, double h) : w_(w), h_(h) {}
    double area() const override { return w_ * h_; }
    double perimeter() const override { return 2 * (w_ + h_); }
    std::string name() const override { return "rectangle"; }
private:
    double w_;
    double h_;
};

class Square : public Rectangle {
public:
    explicit Square(double side) : Rectangle(side, side) {}
    std::string name() const override { return "square"; }
};

double total_area(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes) {
    double total = 0;
    for (const auto& s : shapes) total += s->area();
    return total;
}

std::string largest_name(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes) {
    const Shape* best = nullptr;
    for (const auto& s : shapes) {
        if (!best || s->area() > best->area()) best = s.get();
    }
    return best ? best->name() : "";
}
--- hint
In `Shape`, each function becomes pure virtual, like `virtual double area() const = 0;`, and add `virtual ~Shape() = default;`.
--- hint
Each derived class starts `class Circle : public Shape {` and writes its versions as `double area() const override { … }`. The `const` must match the base exactly, or `override` reports an error. Keep the sizes in private members set by the constructor.
--- hint
`Square(double side) : Rectangle(side, side) {}` reuses the rectangle; it only needs its own `name()`. For `largest_name`, keep `const Shape* best = nullptr;`, and in the loop set `best = s.get();` when `best` is null or `s->area() > best->area()`. Using `>` and not `>=` keeps the first on a tie.
--- check test | Shape is an abstract interface with a virtual destructor
std::is_abstract_v<Shape> && std::has_virtual_destructor_v<Shape>
--- check test | Areas and perimeters
[] { Circle c(1); Rectangle r(2, 3); Square s(4); return std::abs(c.area() - 3.141592653589793) < 1e-9 && std::abs(c.perimeter() - 6.283185307179586) < 1e-9 && r.area() == 6 && r.perimeter() == 10 && s.area() == 16 && s.perimeter() == 16; }()
--- check test | Names through a base pointer
[] { std::unique_ptr<Shape> s = std::make_unique<Square>(2); std::unique_ptr<Shape> r = std::make_unique<Rectangle>(1, 2); return s->name() == "square" && r->name() == "rectangle"; }()
--- check test | total_area over a mixed collection
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Rectangle>(2, 5)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Square>(3)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Circle>(1)); return std::abs(total_area(v) - (19 + 3.141592653589793)) < 1e-9; }()
--- check test | largest_name picks the biggest, first on a tie
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Rectangle>(2, 8)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Square>(4)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Circle>(1)); std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> none; return largest_name(v) == "rectangle" && largest_name(none) == ""; }()

+++ practice | A power budget for mixed devices
--- task
A spacecraft asks every device how much power it draws. Build the interface and three devices. No `main`.

- `Device` is the interface: pure virtual `int watts() const` and `std::string name() const`, and a virtual destructor.
- `Heater(int level)` draws `100 * level` watts and is named `"heater"`.
- `Camera()` draws 15 watts and is named `"camera"`.
- `Radio(bool transmitting)` draws 40 watts while transmitting and 5 otherwise, and is named `"radio"`.
- `int total_watts(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>>& devices)` adds up every device's watts (0 for none).
- `int count_over(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>>& devices, int limit)` counts the devices that draw **more** than `limit` watts.

A `Heater(2)`, a `Camera()` and a `Radio(true)` draw 255 watts in all, and two of them draw more than 20.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

class Device {
public:
    int watts() const { return 0; }
    std::string name() const { return "device"; }
};
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <vector>

class Device {
public:
    virtual ~Device() = default;
    virtual int watts() const = 0;
    virtual std::string name() const = 0;
};

class Heater : public Device {
public:
    explicit Heater(int level) : level_(level) {}
    int watts() const override { return 100 * level_; }
    std::string name() const override { return "heater"; }
private:
    int level_;
};

class Camera : public Device {
public:
    int watts() const override { return 15; }
    std::string name() const override { return "camera"; }
};

class Radio : public Device {
public:
    explicit Radio(bool transmitting) : transmitting_(transmitting) {}
    int watts() const override { return transmitting_ ? 40 : 5; }
    std::string name() const override { return "radio"; }
private:
    bool transmitting_;
};

int total_watts(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>>& devices) {
    int total = 0;
    for (const auto& d : devices) total += d->watts();
    return total;
}

int count_over(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>>& devices, int limit) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const auto& d : devices) {
        if (d->watts() > limit) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
Make both functions in `Device` pure virtual with `= 0`, keep their `const`, and add `virtual ~Device() = default;`.
--- hint
Each device is `class Heater : public Device { … };` with its own versions marked `const override`. Keep the level or the transmitting flag in a private member set by the constructor. The two free functions loop with `const auto& d` and call `d->watts()`.
--- check test | Device is an interface with a virtual destructor
std::is_abstract_v<Device> && std::has_virtual_destructor_v<Device>
--- check test | Each device answers for itself
[] { Heater h(3); Camera c; Radio on(true); Radio off(false); const Device& d = h; return d.watts() == 300 && d.name() == "heater" && c.watts() == 15 && on.watts() == 40 && off.watts() == 5 && off.name() == "radio"; }()
--- check test | The mixed budget
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Heater>(2)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Camera>()); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Radio>(true)); return total_watts(v) == 255 && count_over(v, 20) == 2 && count_over(v, 200) == 0; }()
--- check test | No devices, and exactly at the limit
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>> none; std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Device>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Camera>()); return total_watts(none) == 0 && count_over(none, 0) == 0 && count_over(v, 15) == 0 && count_over(v, 14) == 1; }()

+++ practice | One report, three kinds of sensor
--- task
The starter's `Sensor` base class is finished. Its `report()` is **not** virtual: it calls the virtual functions `label()`, `read()` and `unit()`, so each sensor's report comes out right without writing `report` again. `unit()` has a default, the empty string, which means "no unit". Write three classes that derive from `Sensor`. No `main`.

- `Thermometer(double celsius)`: label `"temp"`, reads its `celsius`, unit `"C"`.
- `Barometer(double kpa)`: label `"pressure"`, reads its `kpa`, unit `"kPa"`.
- `Tally(int count)`: label `"count"`, reads its `count`, and has no unit, so it does **not** override `unit()`.

Through a `const Sensor&`, a `Thermometer(21.5)` reports `"temp: 21.5 C"` and a `Tally(3)` reports `"count: 3"`.
--- starter
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

class Sensor {
public:
    virtual ~Sensor() = default;
    virtual std::string label() const = 0;
    virtual double read() const = 0;
    virtual std::string unit() const { return ""; }

    std::string report() const {
        std::ostringstream out;
        out << label() << ": " << read();
        if (!unit().empty()) out << " " << unit();
        return out.str();
    }
};
--- solution
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

class Sensor {
public:
    virtual ~Sensor() = default;
    virtual std::string label() const = 0;
    virtual double read() const = 0;
    virtual std::string unit() const { return ""; }

    std::string report() const {
        std::ostringstream out;
        out << label() << ": " << read();
        if (!unit().empty()) out << " " << unit();
        return out.str();
    }
};

class Thermometer : public Sensor {
public:
    explicit Thermometer(double celsius) : celsius_(celsius) {}
    std::string label() const override { return "temp"; }
    double read() const override { return celsius_; }
    std::string unit() const override { return "C"; }
private:
    double celsius_;
};

class Barometer : public Sensor {
public:
    explicit Barometer(double kpa) : kpa_(kpa) {}
    std::string label() const override { return "pressure"; }
    double read() const override { return kpa_; }
    std::string unit() const override { return "kPa"; }
private:
    double kpa_;
};

class Tally : public Sensor {
public:
    explicit Tally(int count) : count_(count) {}
    std::string label() const override { return "count"; }
    double read() const override { return count_; }
private:
    int count_;
};
--- hint
Each class must override the two pure virtual functions, `label()` and `read()`, or it stays abstract and cannot be made. Overriding `unit()` is optional: leave it out where the default is right.
--- hint
Mark each override `const override`, exactly matching the base. `Tally::read()` returns a `double`, and returning the `int` member there converts it.
--- check test | Reports through a base reference
[] { Thermometer t(21.5); Tally n(3); const Sensor& a = t; const Sensor& b = n; return a.report() == "temp: 21.5 C" && b.report() == "count: 3"; }()
--- check test | The barometer
[] { Barometer b(101.3); const Sensor& s = b; return s.report() == "pressure: 101.3 kPa" && s.unit() == "kPa"; }()
--- check test | Tally keeps the default unit
[] { Tally n(0); const Sensor& s = n; return s.unit() == "" && s.report() == "count: 0"; }()
--- check test | A mixed vector of sensors
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Sensor>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Thermometer>(-4)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Tally>(12)); std::string all; for (const auto& s : v) all += s->report() + ";"; return all == "temp: -4 C;count: 12;"; }()

+++ practice | Sorting mixed tasks by priority
--- task
Write the `Task` interface, three kinds of task, and a scheduler that orders them. No `main`.

- `Task` has pure virtual `int priority() const` and `std::string label() const`, and a virtual destructor.
- `Burn(int seconds)`: priority 3, label like `"burn 30s"`.
- `Photo()`: priority 1, label `"photo"`.
- `Downlink(int megabytes)`: priority 2, label like `"downlink 12MB"`.
- `std::vector<std::string> schedule(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Task>>& tasks)` returns the labels, highest priority first. Tasks with equal priority keep the order they had in `tasks`. It must not change `tasks`.

For a `Photo()`, a `Burn(30)`, a `Downlink(12)` and a `Burn(5)`, `schedule` is `{"burn 30s", "burn 5s", "downlink 12MB", "photo"}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Task {
public:
    virtual ~Task() = default;
    virtual int priority() const = 0;
    virtual std::string label() const = 0;
};

std::vector<std::string> schedule(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Task>>& tasks) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    for (const auto& t : tasks) out.push_back(t->label());
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Task {
public:
    virtual ~Task() = default;
    virtual int priority() const = 0;
    virtual std::string label() const = 0;
};

class Burn : public Task {
public:
    explicit Burn(int seconds) : seconds_(seconds) {}
    int priority() const override { return 3; }
    std::string label() const override { return "burn " + std::to_string(seconds_) + "s"; }
private:
    int seconds_;
};

class Photo : public Task {
public:
    int priority() const override { return 1; }
    std::string label() const override { return "photo"; }
};

class Downlink : public Task {
public:
    explicit Downlink(int megabytes) : megabytes_(megabytes) {}
    int priority() const override { return 2; }
    std::string label() const override { return "downlink " + std::to_string(megabytes_) + "MB"; }
private:
    int megabytes_;
};

std::vector<std::string> schedule(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Task>>& tasks) {
    std::vector<const Task*> order;
    for (const auto& t : tasks) order.push_back(t.get());
    std::stable_sort(order.begin(), order.end(), [](const Task* a, const Task* b) {
        return a->priority() > b->priority();
    });
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    for (const Task* t : order) out.push_back(t->label());
    return out;
}
--- hint
`tasks` is a `const` reference, and a `unique_ptr` cannot be copied, so do not try to sort `tasks` itself. Make a `std::vector<const Task*>` of plain pointers with `t.get()`, and sort that.
--- hint
Sort the pointers with `std::stable_sort` and a lambda that returns `a->priority() > b->priority()`. "Stable" keeps equal priorities in their original order. Then collect each `t->label()`.
--- check case | The example schedule
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Task>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Photo>()); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Burn>(30)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Downlink>(12)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Burn>(5)); return schedule(v); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"burn 30s", "burn 5s", "downlink 12MB", "photo"}
--- check test | Equal priorities keep their order, and tasks is unchanged
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Task>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Downlink>(1)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Downlink>(2)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Downlink>(3)); auto s = schedule(v); return s == std::vector<std::string>{"downlink 1MB", "downlink 2MB", "downlink 3MB"} && v[0]->label() == "downlink 1MB"; }()
--- check test | Nothing to schedule
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Task>> none; return schedule(none).empty(); }()

+++ practice | Accounts with different rules
--- task
Write an `Account` interface and two kinds of account whose withdrawal rules differ. No `main`.

- `Account` has pure virtual `bool withdraw(int amount)` and `int balance() const`, and a virtual destructor.
- `Checking(int balance, int overdraft)` may go below zero, down to `-overdraft` exactly and no further.
- `Savings(int balance)` never goes below zero, and allows at most **three** successful withdrawals in its life. A fourth is refused even if there is money.
- For both: `withdraw` subtracts `amount` and returns `true` when allowed. When not allowed it changes nothing and returns `false`. An `amount` of 0 or less is never allowed, and a refused withdrawal does not count as one of the three.
- `int withdraw_all(std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Account>>& accounts, int amount)` tries to withdraw `amount` from every account and returns how many succeeded.

`Checking(10, 50)` allows withdrawing 60 (the balance becomes -50), then refuses 1.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

class Account {
public:
    virtual ~Account() = default;
    virtual bool withdraw(int amount) = 0;
    virtual int balance() const = 0;
};

class Checking : public Account {
public:
    Checking(int balance, int overdraft) : balance_(balance), overdraft_(overdraft) {}
    bool withdraw(int amount) override {
        balance_ -= amount;
        return true;
    }
    int balance() const override { return balance_; }
private:
    int balance_;
    int overdraft_;
};
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

class Account {
public:
    virtual ~Account() = default;
    virtual bool withdraw(int amount) = 0;
    virtual int balance() const = 0;
};

class Checking : public Account {
public:
    Checking(int balance, int overdraft) : balance_(balance), overdraft_(overdraft) {}
    bool withdraw(int amount) override {
        if (amount <= 0 || balance_ - amount < -overdraft_) return false;
        balance_ -= amount;
        return true;
    }
    int balance() const override { return balance_; }
private:
    int balance_;
    int overdraft_;
};

class Savings : public Account {
public:
    explicit Savings(int balance) : balance_(balance) {}
    bool withdraw(int amount) override {
        if (amount <= 0 || amount > balance_ || used_ >= 3) return false;
        balance_ -= amount;
        ++used_;
        return true;
    }
    int balance() const override { return balance_; }
private:
    int balance_;
    int used_ = 0;
};

int withdraw_all(std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Account>>& accounts, int amount) {
    int ok = 0;
    for (auto& a : accounts) {
        if (a->withdraw(amount)) ++ok;
    }
    return ok;
}
--- hint
Put every refusal at the top of `withdraw` as one `if` that returns `false`: a bad amount, going past the limit, or (for `Savings`) three withdrawals already made. Only after it do you change anything.
--- hint
`Checking` refuses when `balance_ - amount < -overdraft_`, so landing exactly on `-overdraft_` is allowed. `Savings` keeps a counter `int used_ = 0;` and adds 1 only after a withdrawal really happens.
--- check test | Checking goes down to the overdraft limit exactly
[] { Checking c(10, 50); bool a = c.withdraw(60); bool b = c.withdraw(1); return a && !b && c.balance() == -50; }()
--- check test | Zero and negative amounts are refused
[] { Checking c(10, 0); Savings s(10); return !c.withdraw(0) && !c.withdraw(-5) && !s.withdraw(0) && c.balance() == 10 && s.balance() == 10; }()
--- check test | Savings: never below zero, only three withdrawals
[] { Savings s(100); bool over = s.withdraw(101); bool a = s.withdraw(10), b = s.withdraw(10), c = s.withdraw(10); bool fourth = s.withdraw(10); return !over && a && b && c && !fourth && s.balance() == 70; }()
--- check test | Refusals do not use up a Savings withdrawal
[] { Savings s(30); s.withdraw(50); s.withdraw(0); bool a = s.withdraw(10), b = s.withdraw(10), c = s.withdraw(10); return a && b && c && s.balance() == 0; }()
--- check test | withdraw_all through base pointers
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Account>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Checking>(0, 20)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Savings>(5)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Savings>(50)); int n = withdraw_all(v, 20); return n == 2 && v[0]->balance() == -20 && v[1]->balance() == 5 && v[2]->balance() == 30; }()

+++ practice | Debug: every fire alarm says alarm
--- task
**Bug report:** "`sound_all` should give `"fire flood"` for a fire alarm and a flood alarm, but it gives `"alarm flood"`. Calling `message()` straight on a `FireAlarm` object says `"fire"`, so the class seems fine."

`sound_all` joins the `message()` of every alarm with single spaces. Find why `FireAlarm`'s version is skipped when it is called through an `Alarm` pointer, and fix it in `FireAlarm`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Alarm {
public:
    virtual ~Alarm() = default;
    virtual std::string message() const { return "alarm"; }
};

class FireAlarm : public Alarm {
public:
    std::string message() { return "fire"; }
};

class FloodAlarm : public Alarm {
public:
    std::string message() const override { return "flood"; }
};

std::string sound_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Alarm>>& alarms) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& a : alarms) {
        if (!out.empty()) out += " ";
        out += a->message();
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Alarm {
public:
    virtual ~Alarm() = default;
    virtual std::string message() const { return "alarm"; }
};

class FireAlarm : public Alarm {
public:
    std::string message() const override { return "fire"; }
};

class FloodAlarm : public Alarm {
public:
    std::string message() const override { return "flood"; }
};

std::string sound_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Alarm>>& alarms) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& a : alarms) {
        if (!out.empty()) out += " ";
        out += a->message();
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Put `FireAlarm::message` next to `Alarm::message` and compare them word by word. A function only overrides when everything matches exactly, including `const`.
--- hint
Add `const` after `message()` in `FireAlarm`, and add `override` too: then the compiler will stop you if the two ever stop matching.
--- check case | The mixed alarms
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Alarm>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<FireAlarm>()); v.push_back(std::make_unique<FloodAlarm>()); return sound_all(v); }()
=> "fire flood"
--- check case | Through a base reference
[] { FireAlarm f; const Alarm& a = f; return a.message(); }()
=> "fire"
--- check case | A plain alarm still says alarm
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Alarm>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Alarm>()); v.push_back(std::make_unique<FireAlarm>()); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Alarm>()); return sound_all(v); }()
=> "alarm fire alarm"

+++ practice | Stretch: an expression tree
--- task
An arithmetic expression like `2 + 3 * 4` is a tree: numbers at the leaves, operations above them. Build it with an interface and `unique_ptr`. No `main`.

- `Expr` has pure virtual `double eval() const` and `std::string show() const`, and a virtual destructor.
- `Num(double value)`: `eval` is the value, and `show` prints it the way a default `std::ostringstream` does (`2`, `2.5`, `-1`).
- `Add(std::unique_ptr<Expr> left, std::unique_ptr<Expr> right)` and `Mul(…)` with the same two parameters: `eval` adds or multiplies the two sides; `show` is `"(" + left + " + " + right + ")"`, with `" * "` for `Mul`.
- `Neg(std::unique_ptr<Expr> inner)`: `eval` is minus the inner value; `show` is `"-"` followed by the inner `show`.

`Add(Num(2), Mul(Num(3), Num(4)))` evaluates to 14 and shows as `(2 + (3 * 4))`. Each node owns the nodes below it.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

class Expr {
public:
    virtual ~Expr() = default;
    virtual double eval() const = 0;
    virtual std::string show() const = 0;
};
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

class Expr {
public:
    virtual ~Expr() = default;
    virtual double eval() const = 0;
    virtual std::string show() const = 0;
};

class Num : public Expr {
public:
    explicit Num(double value) : value_(value) {}
    double eval() const override { return value_; }
    std::string show() const override {
        std::ostringstream out;
        out << value_;
        return out.str();
    }
private:
    double value_;
};

class Add : public Expr {
public:
    Add(std::unique_ptr<Expr> left, std::unique_ptr<Expr> right) : left_(std::move(left)), right_(std::move(right)) {}
    double eval() const override { return left_->eval() + right_->eval(); }
    std::string show() const override { return "(" + left_->show() + " + " + right_->show() + ")"; }
private:
    std::unique_ptr<Expr> left_;
    std::unique_ptr<Expr> right_;
};

class Mul : public Expr {
public:
    Mul(std::unique_ptr<Expr> left, std::unique_ptr<Expr> right) : left_(std::move(left)), right_(std::move(right)) {}
    double eval() const override { return left_->eval() * right_->eval(); }
    std::string show() const override { return "(" + left_->show() + " * " + right_->show() + ")"; }
private:
    std::unique_ptr<Expr> left_;
    std::unique_ptr<Expr> right_;
};

class Neg : public Expr {
public:
    explicit Neg(std::unique_ptr<Expr> inner) : inner_(std::move(inner)) {}
    double eval() const override { return -inner_->eval(); }
    std::string show() const override { return "-" + inner_->show(); }
private:
    std::unique_ptr<Expr> inner_;
};
--- hint
Each operation keeps its sides as private `std::unique_ptr<Expr>` members and takes them in its constructor with `std::move`, because a `unique_ptr` cannot be copied: `left_(std::move(left))`.
--- hint
`eval` and `show` call the same function on the children through the arrow, like `left_->eval() + right_->eval()`. That is recursion through virtual calls: each child answers for its own kind.
--- check test | 2 + 3 * 4
[] { auto e = std::make_unique<Add>(std::make_unique<Num>(2), std::make_unique<Mul>(std::make_unique<Num>(3), std::make_unique<Num>(4))); return e->eval() == 14 && e->show() == "(2 + (3 * 4))"; }()
--- check test | Negation and decimals
[] { std::unique_ptr<Expr> e = std::make_unique<Mul>(std::make_unique<Neg>(std::make_unique<Num>(2.5)), std::make_unique<Num>(4)); return e->eval() == -10 && e->show() == "(-2.5 * 4)"; }()
--- check test | A single number
[] { std::unique_ptr<Expr> e = std::make_unique<Num>(-1); return e->eval() == -1 && e->show() == "-1"; }()
--- check test | A deeper tree
[] { auto e = std::make_unique<Neg>(std::make_unique<Add>(std::make_unique<Add>(std::make_unique<Num>(1), std::make_unique<Num>(2)), std::make_unique<Neg>(std::make_unique<Num>(10)))); return e->eval() == 7 && e->show() == "-((1 + 2) + -10)"; }()

=== cpp3-09 | Debugging: object slicing
--- teach
Last lesson, virtual functions let one loop ask every shape for its area and get the right answer for each: circle, rectangle or square. That worked because the loop reached the shapes through a pointer or a reference. This lesson is about the bug you get when you forget that part.

### A dog that forgets it is a dog

Picture a photocopier loaded with small paper. You feed in a big poster, and out comes only the top corner. Nothing jams and no light blinks. The copy is smaller than the original, and whatever did not fit is gone.

C++ can do the same to an object. Here is a bug that confuses many people who come to C++ from Java, Python or C#:

```cpp
Dog rex("Rex");
Animal a = rex;        // copies only the Animal part of rex
a.sound();             // "..." — Animal's version, not Dog's
```

`Dog` derives from `Animal` and overrides `sound()` to say `"woof"`. Yet `a.sound()` runs `Animal`'s version and says `"..."`.

This is **object slicing**: copying a derived object into a variable of the base type, so that only the base part survives. A variable of type `Animal` has room for exactly an `Animal`. Copying `rex` into it keeps the `Animal` part (the name) and [[slices off the rest|slicing-picture]]. The copy is built as a plain `Animal`, so it also loses the "I am really a Dog" information that [[virtual calls rely on|how-virtual-works]].

No error, no warning: the program compiles cleanly and quietly calls the wrong function.

### Where slicing hides

The copy is not always as easy to see as `Animal a = rex;`. Slicing hides in three places.

**By-value parameters.** A parameter without `&` is a fresh copy of what the caller passed in, as you saw in the intermediate course's lesson on [[the function that changed a copy|copy-again]]. So this function slices every `Dog` handed to it:

```cpp
void describe(Animal a);     // a is a new Animal, copied from the argument
describe(Dog("Rex"));        // inside, a.sound() is "..."
```

**Containers of the base type.** A `std::vector<Animal>` stores `Animal`s, and nothing bigger fits in its slots. `push_back` copies the dog in, and the copy is sliced:

```cpp
std::vector<Animal> zoo;
zoo.push_back(Dog("Rex"));   // zoo[0] is a plain Animal
```

**Assignments** like `Animal a = rex;` from the start of the lesson.

### Finding it: the usual debugging method

Debugging slicing follows the same steps as the debugging lessons in the intermediate course.

1. **Reproduce** it with the smallest case: one `Dog`, one call.
2. **Check your assumption.** You are assuming "this call is virtual, so it will use `Dog`'s version." That is only true when the call goes through a **pointer or reference** to the object.
3. **Search** for every place a derived object is converted to a base **value**: a parameter with no `&`, a `std::vector<Animal>`, an `Animal x = …`.

### The fix: pointers and references

Keep objects that should act polymorphically behind references and pointers. A reference is another name for the same object, so nothing is copied and nothing is sliced.

For a parameter, add `const` and `&`, read "a const reference to an Animal":

```cpp
void describe(const Animal& a);   // a is the caller's own object
describe(Dog("Rex"));             // a.sound() is "woof"
```

For a collection, store owning pointers, as in the smart pointers lesson. A `std::unique_ptr<Animal>` can point at a `Dog`, and `std::make_unique<Dog>("Rex")` builds the dog directly on the heap, with no copy:

```cpp
std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> zoo;
zoo.push_back(std::make_unique<Dog>("Rex"));
zoo[0]->sound();                  // "woof"
```

The `->`, read "arrow", calls a member through a pointer: `zoo[0]->sound()` means "the object `zoo[0]` points at, its `sound()`". When you loop over such a vector, `const auto& a` gives you each pointer by reference, and `a->sound()` calls through it.

### Making the compiler catch it

To stop slicing from coming back, a base class meant for polymorphism can [[forbid copying|delete-copy]]. Write the copy constructor and mark it `= delete`, read "deleted: this function does not exist":

```cpp
class Animal {
public:
    Animal(const Animal&) = delete;   // no copying an Animal
    // ...
};
```

Now `Animal a = rex;`, a by-value `Animal` parameter and `push_back` into a `std::vector<Animal>` are all compile errors, so an accidental slice can no longer slip through. `std::make_unique<Dog>("Rex")` still works, because it builds a new dog rather than copying one.

**Watch out:** the loop variable of a range-for is a copy too, unless you add `&`. With `std::vector<Dog> dogs`, the loop `for (Animal a : dogs)` slices every dog. Write `for (const Animal& a : dogs)` instead.

::: context slicing-picture What gets sliced off
A `Dog` object is built in layers: first the `Animal` part (here, the name), then anything `Dog` adds on top. Every object also carries a hidden note of its real type, which the compiler sets when the object is built.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="80" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Dog rex</text>
  <rect x="20" y="28" width="120" height="84" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="30" y="38" width="100" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="58" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Animal part</text>
  <rect x="30" y="74" width="100" height="30" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="94" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Dog part</text>
  <text x="80" y="132" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">real type: Dog</text>
  <line x1="148" y1="53" x2="206" y2="53" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="214,53 204,48 204,58" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="44" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">copy</text>
  <text x="280" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Animal a</text>
  <rect x="220" y="28" width="120" height="48" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="230" y="38" width="100" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="280" y="58" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Animal part</text>
  <text x="280" y="96" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">real type: Animal</text>
  <text x="280" y="150" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">Dog part: gone</text>
</svg>
```
