The copy gets only the `Animal` part, and because it is built as an `Animal`, its hidden note says `Animal`. Even a `Dog` that adds no data of its own is sliced this way: the part that is lost is the knowledge that it was a dog.
:::

::: context how-virtual-works How a virtual call finds the right function
Every major compiler does it the same way. For each class with virtual functions, it makes a table listing which function to run for each virtual call: one table for `Animal`, one for `Dog`. Each object carries a hidden pointer to its class's table, set by the constructor that built it.

A call like `a.sound()` through a reference or pointer follows that hidden pointer to the table and runs whatever function is listed there. A `Dog` points at the `Dog` table, so you get `"woof"`. When you copy a dog into an `Animal` variable, the `Animal` copy constructor builds the new object, so its hidden pointer points at the `Animal` table. That is why the sliced copy says `"..."`.
:::

::: context copy-again The same root as an earlier bug
In the intermediate course, a function changed its parameter and the caller's variable stayed the same, because the parameter was a copy. Slicing is that bug's cousin. Both come from one C++ rule: a parameter or variable without `&` holds its own object, made by copying.

Java, Python and C# behave differently. There, a variable of a class type holds a reference to an object, so passing a `Dog` around never copies it and never slices it. People coming from those languages expect C++ to act the same way, which is why this bug catches them.
:::

::: context delete-copy Why polymorphic classes forbid copying
`= delete` works on any function: the function is declared, but any code that would call it fails to compile. In the copying lesson you met the copy constructor as one of the rule of three. Deleting it is one more way of taking charge of copying.

The C++ Core Guidelines, a widely used rule book edited by Bjarne Stroustrup and Herb Sutter, say it directly (rule C.67): a polymorphic class should suppress public copy and move. A copy of a base-class object can only ever be a base-class object, so copying is almost always a slice waiting to happen. Deleting the copy constructor also means the class gets no automatic move constructor, so a move cannot slice either.
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** "`describe(Dog("Rex"))` returns `"Rex says ..."` instead of `"Rex says woof"`, and `chorus()` returns `"..., ..., ..."` instead of `"woof, meow, ..."`."

Find where the dogs and cats lose their type and fix it:

- `describe` takes its `Animal` by value. Make it take a `const Animal&` instead, so it returns `"Rex says woof"` for a dog, `"Tom says meow"` for a cat and `"Blob says ..."` for a plain animal.
- `chorus` stores the animals in a `std::vector<Animal>`. Store them as `std::unique_ptr<Animal>` instead, built with `std::make_unique`, so it returns `"woof, meow, ..."`.

Keep the classes as they are, and keep the names and return types of `describe` and `chorus`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Animal {
public:
    explicit Animal(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
    virtual std::string sound() const { return "..."; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Dog(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "woof"; }
};

class Cat : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Cat(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "meow"; }
};

std::string describe(Animal a) {
    return a.name() + " says " + a.sound();
}

std::string chorus() {
    std::vector<Animal> zoo;
    zoo.push_back(Dog("Rex"));
    zoo.push_back(Cat("Tom"));
    zoo.push_back(Animal("Blob"));
    std::string out;
    for (const Animal& a : zoo) {
        if (!out.empty()) out += ", ";
        out += a.sound();
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Animal {
public:
    explicit Animal(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
    virtual std::string sound() const { return "..."; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Dog(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "woof"; }
};

class Cat : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Cat(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "meow"; }
};

std::string describe(const Animal& a) {
    return a.name() + " says " + a.sound();
}

std::string chorus() {
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> zoo;
    zoo.push_back(std::make_unique<Dog>("Rex"));
    zoo.push_back(std::make_unique<Cat>("Tom"));
    zoo.push_back(std::make_unique<Animal>("Blob"));
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& a : zoo) {
        if (!out.empty()) out += ", ";
        out += a->sound();
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
`describe` takes an `Animal` by value. What happens to a `Dog` when it is copied into an `Animal`? One change to the parameter keeps the caller's own dog.
--- hint
`std::vector<Animal>` can only hold `Animal`s. Store owning pointers instead: `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>`, and add each animal with `std::make_unique<Dog>("Rex")`, `std::make_unique<Cat>("Tom")` and `std::make_unique<Animal>("Blob")`.
--- hint
The loop now walks over pointers: write `for (const auto& a : zoo)` and call the sound with the arrow, `a->sound()`.
--- check case | describe keeps the dog a dog
describe(Dog("Rex"))
=> "Rex says woof"
--- check case | describe keeps the cat a cat
describe(Cat("Tom"))
=> "Tom says meow"
--- check case | A plain animal still says ...
describe(Animal("Blob"))
=> "Blob says ..."
--- check case | chorus has every voice
chorus()
=> "woof, meow, ..."

+++ practice | Labels that keep the real kind
--- task
**Bug report:** "`label(Valve("V1"))` returns `"V1 [part]"` instead of `"V1 [valve]"`."

`Part` has a virtual `kind()` that `Valve` and `Pump` override. Fix `label` so it keeps each part's real kind, keeping its name and return type. Then write `std::string label_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>>& parts)`, which joins the label of every part with `", "`, in order, and returns `""` for no parts. No `main`.

For a `Valve("V1")`, a `Pump("P2")` and a plain `Part("X")`, `label_all` is `"V1 [valve], P2 [pump], X [part]"`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Part {
public:
    explicit Part(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Part() = default;
    virtual std::string kind() const { return "part"; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Valve : public Part {
public:
    explicit Valve(std::string name) : Part(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string kind() const override { return "valve"; }
};

class Pump : public Part {
public:
    explicit Pump(std::string name) : Part(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string kind() const override { return "pump"; }
};

std::string label(Part p) {
    return p.name() + " [" + p.kind() + "]";
}

std::string label_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>>& parts) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Part {
public:
    explicit Part(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Part() = default;
    virtual std::string kind() const { return "part"; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Valve : public Part {
public:
    explicit Valve(std::string name) : Part(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string kind() const override { return "valve"; }
};

class Pump : public Part {
public:
    explicit Pump(std::string name) : Part(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string kind() const override { return "pump"; }
};

std::string label(const Part& p) {
    return p.name() + " [" + p.kind() + "]";
}

std::string label_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>>& parts) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& p : parts) {
        if (!out.empty()) out += ", ";
        out += label(*p);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
`label` takes a `Part` by value, so a `Valve` passed in is copied into a plain `Part`. A reference refers to the caller's own object instead.
--- hint
Make the parameter `const Part& p`. In `label_all`, loop with `const auto& p` and call `label(*p)`: the `*` turns each `unique_ptr` into the `Part&` it owns. Add `", "` before every label except the first.
--- check case | A valve stays a valve
label(Valve("V1"))
=> "V1 [valve]"
--- check case | A plain part
label(Part("X"))
=> "X [part]"
--- check case | label_all over a mixed list
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Valve>("V1")); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Pump>("P2")); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Part>("X")); return label_all(v); }()
=> "V1 [valve], P2 [pump], X [part]"
--- check case | No parts
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>> none; return label_all(none); }()
=> ""

+++ practice | A factory that returns the real vehicle
--- task
**Bug report:** "`make_vehicle("rover", "R1").wheels()` is 0, but a rover has 6 wheels."

`make_vehicle` builds a `Rover` or a `Cart`, but returns a `Vehicle` **by value**, so the object is sliced on the way out. Change it to return `std::unique_ptr<Vehicle>`: a `Rover` for `"rover"`, a `Cart` for `"cart"`, built with `std::make_unique`, and `nullptr` for any other kind. Then write `int total_wheels(const std::vector<std::string>& kinds)`, which makes one vehicle of each kind (use any id) and adds up their wheels, skipping unknown kinds. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Vehicle {
public:
    explicit Vehicle(std::string id) : id_(std::move(id)) {}
    virtual ~Vehicle() = default;
    virtual int wheels() const { return 0; }
    const std::string& id() const { return id_; }
private:
    std::string id_;
};

class Rover : public Vehicle {
public:
    explicit Rover(std::string id) : Vehicle(std::move(id)) {}
    int wheels() const override { return 6; }
};

class Cart : public Vehicle {
public:
    explicit Cart(std::string id) : Vehicle(std::move(id)) {}
    int wheels() const override { return 4; }
};

Vehicle make_vehicle(const std::string& kind, const std::string& id) {
    if (kind == "rover") return Rover(id);
    if (kind == "cart") return Cart(id);
    return Vehicle(id);
}

int total_wheels(const std::vector<std::string>& kinds) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Vehicle {
public:
    explicit Vehicle(std::string id) : id_(std::move(id)) {}
    virtual ~Vehicle() = default;
    virtual int wheels() const { return 0; }
    const std::string& id() const { return id_; }
private:
    std::string id_;
};

class Rover : public Vehicle {
public:
    explicit Rover(std::string id) : Vehicle(std::move(id)) {}
    int wheels() const override { return 6; }
};

class Cart : public Vehicle {
public:
    explicit Cart(std::string id) : Vehicle(std::move(id)) {}
    int wheels() const override { return 4; }
};

std::unique_ptr<Vehicle> make_vehicle(const std::string& kind, const std::string& id) {
    if (kind == "rover") return std::make_unique<Rover>(id);
    if (kind == "cart") return std::make_unique<Cart>(id);
    return nullptr;
}

int total_wheels(const std::vector<std::string>& kinds) {
    int total = 0;
    for (const std::string& kind : kinds) {
        std::unique_ptr<Vehicle> v = make_vehicle(kind, "tmp");
        if (v) total += v->wheels();
    }
    return total;
}
--- hint
A return type of `Vehicle` has room for exactly a `Vehicle`, so the `Rover` is cut down to one on the way out. An owning pointer to a `Vehicle` can point at a whole `Rover`.
--- hint
Return `std::make_unique<Rover>(id)` or `std::make_unique<Cart>(id)`; a `unique_ptr<Rover>` turns into a `unique_ptr<Vehicle>` by itself. In `total_wheels`, check the pointer before using it: `if (v) total += v->wheels();`.
--- check test | A rover keeps its six wheels and its id
[] { auto r = make_vehicle("rover", "R1"); return r && r->wheels() == 6 && r->id() == "R1"; }()
--- check test | A cart has four
[] { auto c = make_vehicle("cart", "C9"); return c && c->wheels() == 4; }()
--- check test | Unknown kinds give nullptr
make_vehicle("boat", "B1") == nullptr && make_vehicle("", "x") == nullptr
--- check case | total_wheels skips unknown kinds
total_wheels({"rover", "cart", "boat", "rover"})
=> 16

+++ practice | Make slicing a compile error
--- task
The starter's `Registry` stores sensors in a `std::vector<Sensor>`, so every `Thermo` added is sliced and reads 0. Fix the design so this cannot happen again. No `main`.

1. Delete `Sensor`'s copy constructor and copy assignment operator, so any code that would slice a sensor stops compiling.
2. Make `Registry` own its sensors as `std::unique_ptr<Sensor>`. Its `add` becomes `void add(std::unique_ptr<Sensor> sensor)`.
3. Keep `std::string readings() const`, which returns `name=value` for each sensor in the order added, joined by `"; "`, with each value printed the way a default `std::ostringstream` prints it. With no sensors it returns `""`. Keep `std::size_t size() const`.

After adding a `Thermo("t1", 21.5)` and a plain `Sensor("base")`, `readings()` is `"t1=21.5; base=0"`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Sensor {
public:
    explicit Sensor(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Sensor() = default;
    virtual double read() const { return 0; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Thermo : public Sensor {
public:
    Thermo(std::string name, double celsius) : Sensor(std::move(name)), celsius_(celsius) {}
    double read() const override { return celsius_; }
private:
    double celsius_;
};

class Registry {
public:
    void add(const Sensor& sensor) { sensors_.push_back(sensor); }
    std::size_t size() const { return sensors_.size(); }
    std::string readings() const {
        std::ostringstream out;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < sensors_.size(); ++i) {
            if (i > 0) out << "; ";
            out << sensors_[i].name() << "=" << sensors_[i].read();
        }
        return out.str();
    }
private:
    std::vector<Sensor> sensors_;
};
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Sensor {
public:
    explicit Sensor(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Sensor() = default;
    Sensor(const Sensor&) = delete;
    Sensor& operator=(const Sensor&) = delete;
    virtual double read() const { return 0; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Thermo : public Sensor {
public:
    Thermo(std::string name, double celsius) : Sensor(std::move(name)), celsius_(celsius) {}
    double read() const override { return celsius_; }
private:
    double celsius_;
};

class Registry {
public:
    void add(std::unique_ptr<Sensor> sensor) { sensors_.push_back(std::move(sensor)); }
    std::size_t size() const { return sensors_.size(); }
    std::string readings() const {
        std::ostringstream out;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < sensors_.size(); ++i) {
            if (i > 0) out << "; ";
            out << sensors_[i]->name() << "=" << sensors_[i]->read();
        }
        return out.str();
    }
private:
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Sensor>> sensors_;
};
--- hint
Deleting copying is two lines inside `Sensor`: `Sensor(const Sensor&) = delete;` and `Sensor& operator=(const Sensor&) = delete;`. After that, the starter's `std::vector<Sensor>` no longer compiles, which is the point.
--- hint
Store `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Sensor>> sensors_;`, move each new sensor in with `sensors_.push_back(std::move(sensor));`, and read through the arrow: `sensors_[i]->name()`.
--- check test | Sensor can no longer be copied
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<Sensor> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<Sensor>
--- check case | Readings keep each sensor's real type
[] { Registry r; r.add(std::make_unique<Thermo>("t1", 21.5)); r.add(std::make_unique<Sensor>("base")); return r.readings(); }()
=> "t1=21.5; base=0"
--- check test | An empty registry
[] { Registry r; return r.readings() == "" && r.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Many thermometers, in order
[] { Registry r; for (int i = 1; i <= 3; ++i) r.add(std::make_unique<Thermo>("t" + std::to_string(i), -i)); return r.size() == 3 && r.readings() == "t1=-1; t2=-2; t3=-3"; }()

+++ practice | A zoo with gaps in it
--- task
A zoo is a `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>`, and some of its slots may hold `nullptr` (an empty cage). Write two functions that keep every animal's real type and cope with empty cages. No `main`.

- `std::string describe_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>& zoo)` returns `"<name>: <sound>"` for each animal, joined with `", "`, skipping empty cages. If there are no animals at all (an empty vector, or only empty cages), it returns `"(empty)"`.
- `int count_sound(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>& zoo, const std::string& sound)` counts the animals whose `sound()` equals `sound`, skipping empty cages.

For `Dog("Rex")`, an empty cage and `Cat("Tom")`, `describe_all` is `"Rex: woof, Tom: meow"`. The starter slices every animal and crashes on an empty cage.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Animal {
public:
    explicit Animal(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
    virtual std::string sound() const { return "..."; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Dog(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "woof"; }
};

class Cat : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Cat(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "meow"; }
};

std::string describe_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>& zoo) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& p : zoo) {
        Animal a = *p;
        if (!out.empty()) out += ", ";
        out += a.name() + ": " + a.sound();
    }
    return out;
}

int count_sound(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>& zoo, const std::string& sound) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const auto& p : zoo) {
        Animal a = *p;
        if (a.sound() == sound) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Animal {
public:
    explicit Animal(std::string name) : name_(std::move(name)) {}
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
    virtual std::string sound() const { return "..."; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};

class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Dog(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "woof"; }
};

class Cat : public Animal {
public:
    explicit Cat(std::string name) : Animal(std::move(name)) {}
    std::string sound() const override { return "meow"; }
};

std::string describe_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>& zoo) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& p : zoo) {
        if (!p) continue;
        if (!out.empty()) out += ", ";
        out += p->name() + ": " + p->sound();
    }
    return out.empty() ? "(empty)" : out;
}

int count_sound(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>& zoo, const std::string& sound) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const auto& p : zoo) {
        if (p && p->sound() == sound) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
`Animal a = *p;` does two bad things: it copies only the `Animal` part, and if `p` is `nullptr`, the `*` follows a pointer to nothing. Call through the pointer instead, `p->sound()`, after checking it.
--- hint
Skip an empty cage with `if (!p) continue;`. At the end of `describe_all`, if nothing was added, return `"(empty)"` instead of the empty string.
--- check case | Real sounds, with an empty cage skipped
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> z; z.push_back(std::make_unique<Dog>("Rex")); z.push_back(nullptr); z.push_back(std::make_unique<Cat>("Tom")); return describe_all(z); }()
=> "Rex: woof, Tom: meow"
--- check test | No animals at all
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> none; std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> gaps; gaps.push_back(nullptr); gaps.push_back(nullptr); return describe_all(none) == "(empty)" && describe_all(gaps) == "(empty)" && count_sound(gaps, "woof") == 0; }()
--- check test | count_sound counts real types, plain animals included
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> z; z.push_back(std::make_unique<Dog>("A")); z.push_back(std::make_unique<Animal>("B")); z.push_back(nullptr); z.push_back(std::make_unique<Dog>("C")); return count_sound(z, "woof") == 2 && count_sound(z, "...") == 1 && count_sound(z, "meow") == 0; }()
--- check case | Only a plain animal
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>> z; z.push_back(std::make_unique<Animal>("Blob")); return describe_all(z); }()
=> "Blob: ..."

+++ practice | Debug: the strongest signal that forgets itself
--- task
**Bug report:** "`strongest()` always ends in `(signal)`, never `(radio)` or `(laser)`, and it picks the wrong signal: for strengths 30, 80 and 10 it names the last one."

`strongest` should return the source and kind of the signal with the greatest `strength()`, like `"ship (radio)"`. On a tie the first one wins, and with no signals it returns `""`. The loop's logic is fine; the variable that remembers the best signal so far is not. Fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Signal {
public:
    explicit Signal(std::string source) : source_(std::move(source)) {}
    virtual ~Signal() = default;
    virtual double strength() const { return 0; }
    virtual std::string kind() const { return "signal"; }
    const std::string& source() const { return source_; }
private:
    std::string source_;
};

class Radio : public Signal {
public:
    Radio(std::string source, double db) : Signal(std::move(source)), db_(db) {}
    double strength() const override { return db_; }
    std::string kind() const override { return "radio"; }
private:
    double db_;
};

class Laser : public Signal {
public:
    Laser(std::string source, double db) : Signal(std::move(source)), db_(db) {}
    double strength() const override { return db_; }
    std::string kind() const override { return "laser"; }
private:
    double db_;
};

std::string strongest(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Signal>>& signals) {
    if (signals.empty()) return "";
    Signal best = *signals[0];
    for (const auto& s : signals) {
        if (s->strength() > best.strength()) best = *s;
    }
    return best.source() + " (" + best.kind() + ")";
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Signal {
public:
    explicit Signal(std::string source) : source_(std::move(source)) {}
    virtual ~Signal() = default;
    virtual double strength() const { return 0; }
    virtual std::string kind() const { return "signal"; }
    const std::string& source() const { return source_; }
private:
    std::string source_;
};

class Radio : public Signal {
public:
    Radio(std::string source, double db) : Signal(std::move(source)), db_(db) {}
    double strength() const override { return db_; }
    std::string kind() const override { return "radio"; }
private:
    double db_;
};

class Laser : public Signal {
public:
    Laser(std::string source, double db) : Signal(std::move(source)), db_(db) {}
    double strength() const override { return db_; }
    std::string kind() const override { return "laser"; }
private:
    double db_;
};

std::string strongest(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Signal>>& signals) {
    if (signals.empty()) return "";
    const Signal* best = signals[0].get();
    for (const auto& s : signals) {
        if (s->strength() > best->strength()) best = s.get();
    }
    return best->source() + " (" + best->kind() + ")";
}
--- hint
`Signal best = *signals[0];` makes a new plain `Signal`, copied from the first one. What does a plain `Signal` say its `strength()` and `kind()` are?
--- hint
Keep a pointer instead of a copy: `const Signal* best = signals[0].get();`, then `best = s.get();` inside the loop, and read with `best->strength()`, `best->source()` and `best->kind()`.
--- check case | The strongest of three
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Signal>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Radio>("ship", 30)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Laser>("probe", 80)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Radio>("buoy", 10)); return strongest(v); }()
=> "probe (laser)"
--- check case | A tie goes to the first
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Signal>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Radio>("ship", 50)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Laser>("probe", 50)); return strongest(v); }()
=> "ship (radio)"
--- check test | One signal, and none
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Signal>> one; one.push_back(std::make_unique<Laser>("sat", 5)); std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Signal>> none; return strongest(one) == "sat (laser)" && strongest(none) == ""; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: copying shapes without slicing
--- task
Copying a `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>` is not allowed, and copying each shape as a `Shape` would slice it. The usual fix is a virtual **clone** function: each class knows how to copy itself. No `main`.

- `Shape` is an interface with pure virtual `double area() const`, `std::string name() const`, `void scale(double k)` and `std::unique_ptr<Shape> clone() const`, and a virtual destructor.
- `Circle(double r)`: area π·r·r (use `3.141592653589793`), name `"circle"`, and `scale(k)` multiplies the radius by `k`.
- `Rect(double w, double h)`: area w·h, name `"rect"`, and `scale(k)` multiplies both sides by `k`.
- In each class, `clone()` returns a new copy of itself as a `std::unique_ptr<Shape>`: for a `Circle`, `std::make_unique<Circle>(*this)`, which copies the whole `Circle`.
- `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> copy_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes)` returns a deep copy: the same kinds of shape with the same sizes, in the same order, that change independently of the originals.
--- starter
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Shape {
public:
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
    virtual double area() const = 0;
    virtual std::string name() const = 0;
    virtual void scale(double k) = 0;
};

std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> copy_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Shape {
public:
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
    virtual double area() const = 0;
    virtual std::string name() const = 0;
    virtual void scale(double k) = 0;
    virtual std::unique_ptr<Shape> clone() const = 0;
};

class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    explicit Circle(double r) : r_(r) {}
    double area() const override { return 3.141592653589793 * r_ * r_; }
    std::string name() const override { return "circle"; }
    void scale(double k) override { r_ *= k; }
    std::unique_ptr<Shape> clone() const override { return std::make_unique<Circle>(*this); }
private:
    double r_;
};

class Rect : public Shape {
public:
    Rect(double w, double h) : w_(w), h_(h) {}
    double area() const override { return w_ * h_; }
    std::string name() const override { return "rect"; }
    void scale(double k) override {
        w_ *= k;
        h_ *= k;
    }
    std::unique_ptr<Shape> clone() const override { return std::make_unique<Rect>(*this); }
private:
    double w_;
    double h_;
};

std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> copy_all(const std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>>& shapes) {
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> out;
    for (const auto& s : shapes) out.push_back(s->clone());
    return out;
}
--- hint
Inside `Circle::clone`, `*this` is the whole `Circle`, so `std::make_unique<Circle>(*this)` copies it with `Circle`'s own copy constructor: nothing is sliced. A `unique_ptr<Circle>` turns into the `unique_ptr<Shape>` the function returns.
--- hint
`copy_all` loops over the originals and pushes `s->clone()` for each one. The virtual call picks the right `clone` for every shape.
--- check test | Copies keep their kinds and sizes
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Circle>(1)); v.push_back(std::make_unique<Rect>(2, 3)); auto c = copy_all(v); return c.size() == 2 && c[0]->name() == "circle" && c[1]->name() == "rect" && std::abs(c[0]->area() - 3.141592653589793) < 1e-9 && c[1]->area() == 6; }()
--- check test | Copies are independent of the originals
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> v; v.push_back(std::make_unique<Rect>(2, 3)); auto c = copy_all(v); v[0]->scale(10); c[0]->scale(2); return v[0]->area() == 600 && c[0]->area() == 24; }()
--- check test | clone through a base pointer
[] { std::unique_ptr<Shape> s = std::make_unique<Circle>(2); auto t = s->clone(); s->scale(0); return t->name() == "circle" && std::abs(t->area() - 4 * 3.141592653589793) < 1e-9 && t.get() != s.get(); }()
--- check test | Copying nothing gives nothing
[] { std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> none; return copy_all(none).empty(); }()

=== cpp3-10 | std::function and callbacks
--- teach
Last lesson, a `std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Animal>>` let one vector hold dogs, cats and plain animals, all behind one common type. This lesson does the same trick for pieces of code: one type that can hold any function you like, so you can store functions and call them later.

### Code to run later

Picture ordering telescope parts from a shop. You do not stand at the counter until they arrive. You leave your phone number and say, "Call me when they come in." The shop keeps your number and calls it at the right moment.

A **callback** is the code version of that phone number: code you hand to someone else, for them to [[run later|callbacks-everywhere]]. "When a message arrives, call this." "When the timer runs out, call this."

The receiver has to keep many callbacks, from many callers, in one place. They come in different kinds: lambdas with different captures, plain functions, and **function objects** (objects of a class that has an `operator()`, so you can call them like functions).

### Every lambda is its own type

Here is the problem. Each lambda has [[its own unique type|lambda-types]], with no name you could write down. These two lambdas look alike, but their types are different:

```cpp
auto add = [](int a, int b) { return a + b; };
auto mul = [](int a, int b) { return a * b; };
add = mul;   // error: add and mul are different types
```

So there is no way to write "a vector of lambdas like these" using the lambdas' own types. You need one common type that can hold any of them.

### std::function: one type for "anything callable"

That common type is `std::function<Signature>`, from `<functional>`. Inside the angle brackets goes a [[signature|reading-signatures]]: what the function takes and what it gives back. Read `int(int, int)` as "takes two ints, gives back an int":

```cpp
#include <functional>

std::function<int(int, int)> op = [](int a, int b) { return a + b; };
op(2, 3);                      // 5
op = [](int a, int b) { return a * b; };
op(2, 3);                      // 6
```

The same `op` held two different lambdas, one after the other. It can hold a plain function too, as long as the signature matches:

```cpp
int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; }

op = add;
op(4, 5);                      // 9
```

A `std::function` can also be **empty**: holding nothing at all. Test it like a `bool` before calling it:

```cpp
std::function<void()> empty;   // void(): takes nothing, gives back nothing
if (!empty) { /* nothing stored: calling it would be an error */ }
```

Calling an empty `std::function` is an error. With exceptions switched off, as in this app, it ends the program.

### What it costs

`std::function` is a small example of **type erasure**: hiding many different types behind one. Whatever you put in, from the outside it is [["something callable with this signature"|type-erasure]]. Its real type is erased.

That convenience [[costs a little|the-cost]]: it may need a heap allocation to store a bigger callable, and every call goes through an extra step to find the real function.

So choose by what you need:

- When a function only **passes a callable straight through** and calls it, use a template parameter, as in the function templates lesson. The compiler then knows the exact type and there is no extra cost:

```cpp
template <typename F>
int apply_twice(F f, int x) { return f(f(x)); }

apply_twice([](int x) { return x + 1; }, 3);   // 5
```

- When you need to **store** callables for later, especially many of different kinds, use `std::function`.

### Storing callbacks: an event bus

A typical design is an **event bus**: a central place where parts of a program [[subscribe to events|software-bus]] and publish them. It could map each event name to a list of handlers:

```cpp
std::map<std::string, std::vector<std::function<void(int)>>> handlers;

handlers["tick"].push_back([](int v) { /* react to v */ });
for (const auto& h : handlers["tick"]) h(3);   // call every "tick" handler with 3
```

Callers will also want to **unsubscribe**: to take a handler off the list later. They cannot find their handler again by looking for the lambda, because two lambdas cannot be compared with `==`, and neither can two `std::function`s. So `subscribe` hands back a **handle**: a number, the id, that the caller keeps and gives back later.

To keep an id with each handler, store small records in one vector. The vector also keeps the order they subscribed in:

```cpp
struct Subscription {
    int id;
    std::string event;
    std::function<void(int)> handler;
};
std::vector<Subscription> subs;
subs.push_back({1, "tick", [](int v) { /* … */ }});
```

To remove the record with a given id, walk the vector with an iterator (as in the iterators lesson) and pass the match to `erase`, which takes that element out and closes the gap:

```cpp
for (auto it = subs.begin(); it != subs.end(); ++it) {
    if (it->id == 1) {
        subs.erase(it);
        break;                 // stop: it no longer points at a valid element
    }
}
```

After `erase`, the iterator `it` is no longer valid, so stop the loop at once, with `break` or a `return`.

### Returning a function

Functions can **return** callables, too. `compose(f, g)` builds a new function that runs `g` first, then runs `f` on the result, so `compose(f, g)(x)` is `f(g(x))`:

```cpp
std::function<int(int)> compose(std::function<int(int)> f, std::function<int(int)> g) {
    return [f, g](int x) { return f(g(x)); };
}
```

The lambda captures `f` and `g` **by value**. They are parameters, so they end when `compose` returns, and the lambda needs its own copies to use later.

**Watch out:** a handler that captures a local variable **by reference** must not outlive that variable. If a function subscribes `[&count](int) { ++count; }` and then returns, the bus is left holding a reference to a `count` that no longer exists. When that handler is called, the program has undefined behavior, and nothing warns you. Capture by value, or make sure the handler is unsubscribed first.

::: context callbacks-everywhere You have used callbacks already
The comparator you gave `std::sort` in the lambdas lesson was a callback: `sort` called your code every time it needed to compare two values. A button in an app works the same way: the app hands the button a function, and the button calls it on each click.

The name comes from the telephone: you "call back" someone who left you their number. Callbacks let one piece of code react to events without the code that raises the event knowing anything about it.
:::

::: context lambda-types Why each lambda has its own type
The lambdas lesson showed what the compiler does with a lambda: it writes a small class with no usable name, and the lambda is an object of that class. Every lambda expression gets its own class, even when two are written the same, character for character. `add` and `mul` are objects of two different classes, so assigning one to the other fails, the same way assigning a `std::string` to an `int` would.

That is why `auto` is how you usually store a lambda: it is the only way to name its type.
:::

::: context reading-signatures Reading a function type
`int(int, int)` is a **function type**: the return type first, then the parameter types in brackets, with no function name. A few more:

- `void()` takes nothing and gives back nothing;
- `bool(const std::string&)` takes a string by const reference and gives back true or false;
- `void(int)` takes an int and gives back nothing, like the handlers in this lesson's event bus.

A callable fits a `std::function` if it can be called with those argument types and its result can be turned into the return type.
:::

::: context type-erasure One box, many shapes
Type erasure means the outside of an object shows one type while the inside can be many. A `std::function<int(int, int)>` hides a lambda, a plain function or a function object behind the same face: "call me with two ints, get an int".

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="20" width="110" height="30" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="65" y="40" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a lambda</text>
  <rect x="10" y="70" width="110" height="30" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="65" y="90" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a plain function</text>
  <rect x="10" y="120" width="110" height="30" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="65" y="140" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a function object</text>
  <line x1="124" y1="35" x2="196" y2="80" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="124" y1="85" x2="196" y2="85" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="124" y1="135" x2="196" y2="90" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="200" y="55" width="150" height="60" rx="8" fill="#fff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="275" y="80" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">std::function</text>
  <text x="275" y="98" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;int(int, int)&gt;</text>
</svg>
```

You have met the same idea already: a `std::unique_ptr<Animal>` hides whether it holds a dog or a cat, and a virtual call finds the right function. Inside, `std::function` works in a similar way.
:::

::: context the-cost Where the cost comes from
A `std::function` object has a fixed size, but the callables put into it do not: a lambda that captures ten strings is much bigger than one that captures nothing. Standard libraries keep a small space inside the `std::function` for small callables, and put bigger ones on the heap.

Each call also goes through a pointer to find the real function, so the compiler usually cannot paste the function's body in at the call site, a speed-up it makes all the time for templates.

Flight software watches this closely. NASA JPL's "Power of 10" coding rules forbid dynamic memory allocation after initialization, so a program that stores callbacks registers them while it starts up, not in the middle of a flight.
:::

::: context software-bus A bus on a real spacecraft
NASA's core Flight System (cFS), free flight software that has flown on many NASA missions, is built around a **Software Bus**. Each application subscribes to the kinds of message it cares about, identified by a number called a message ID. Another application publishes a message, and the bus delivers it to every subscriber, without the publisher knowing who they are.

That is this lesson's event bus on a larger scale: the camera software does not need to know which programs want its pictures, and a new program can subscribe later without changing the camera code.
:::
--- task
Write `class EventBus` with these three member functions. No `main`.

- `int subscribe(const std::string& event, std::function<void(int)> handler)` — registers `handler` for `event` and returns a new id: 1 for the first subscription, then 2, 3, … in order.
- `bool unsubscribe(int id)` — removes the handler with that id and returns `true`; returns `false` if there was no such id.
- `int publish(const std::string& event, int value)` — calls every handler for `event`, in the order they subscribed, passing `value`; returns how many handlers were called (0 if none).

Also write `std::function<int(int)> compose(std::function<int(int)> f, std::function<int(int)> g)`, returning a function that computes `f(g(x))`.
--- starter
#include <functional>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class EventBus {
public:
    int subscribe(const std::string& event, std::function<void(int)> handler) { return 0; }
    bool unsubscribe(int id) { return false; }
    int publish(const std::string& event, int value) { return 0; }
};

std::function<int(int)> compose(std::function<int(int)> f, std::function<int(int)> g) {
    return f;
}
--- solution
#include <functional>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class EventBus {
public:
    int subscribe(const std::string& event, std::function<void(int)> handler) {
        int id = next_id_++;
        subs_.push_back({id, event, std::move(handler)});
        return id;
    }

    bool unsubscribe(int id) {
        for (auto it = subs_.begin(); it != subs_.end(); ++it) {
            if (it->id == id) {
                subs_.erase(it);
                return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }

    int publish(const std::string& event, int value) {
        int called = 0;
        for (const auto& s : subs_) {
            if (s.event == event) {
                s.handler(value);
                ++called;
            }
        }
        return called;
    }

private:
    struct Subscription {
        int id;
        std::string event;
        std::function<void(int)> handler;
    };
    std::vector<Subscription> subs_;
    int next_id_ = 1;
};

std::function<int(int)> compose(std::function<int(int)> f, std::function<int(int)> g) {
    return [f, g](int x) { return f(g(x)); };
}
--- hint
Store each subscription as a small struct `{ id, event, handler }` in a private `std::vector` — the vector keeps subscription order for free. A private `int next_id_ = 1;` member gives out the ids.
--- hint
`publish` walks the vector and calls `s.handler(value)` for each record whose `event` matches, counting as it goes. `unsubscribe` walks it with an iterator, and on a matching `it->id` calls `erase(it)` and returns `true` at once.
--- hint
`compose` returns a lambda that captures `f` and `g` **by value**, `[f, g](int x) { return f(g(x)); }`, because they must outlive the call to `compose`.
--- check test | Handlers run in order with the value
[] { EventBus bus; std::vector<int> log; bus.subscribe("tick", [&log](int v) { log.push_back(v); }); bus.subscribe("tick", [&log](int v) { log.push_back(v * 10); }); int n = bus.publish("tick", 3); return n == 2 && log == std::vector<int>{3, 30}; }()
--- check test | Only the named event's handlers run
[] { EventBus bus; int a = 0, b = 0; bus.subscribe("a", [&a](int v) { a += v; }); bus.subscribe("b", [&b](int v) { b += v; }); bus.publish("a", 5); return a == 5 && b == 0 && bus.publish("nobody", 1) == 0; }()
--- check test | Ids count up and unsubscribe removes one handler
[] { EventBus bus; int hits = 0; int id1 = bus.subscribe("x", [&hits](int) { hits += 1; }); int id2 = bus.subscribe("x", [&hits](int) { hits += 100; }); bool gone = bus.unsubscribe(id1); bus.publish("x", 0); return id1 == 1 && id2 == 2 && gone && hits == 100 && !bus.unsubscribe(id1) && !bus.unsubscribe(42); }()
--- check test | compose runs g, then f
[] { auto add1 = [](int x) { return x + 1; }; auto twice = [](int x) { return x * 2; }; auto h = compose(add1, twice); auto k = compose(twice, add1); return h(5) == 11 && k(5) == 12; }()

+++ practice | A table of operations
--- task
A calculator keeps its operations in a table, found by name. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>> make_ops()` returns a map with four entries: `"+"` adds, `"-"` subtracts the second number from the first, `"*"` multiplies, and `"max"` gives the larger of the two.
- `std::optional<int> calc(const std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>>& ops, const std::string& op, int a, int b)` looks `op` up in `ops` and returns the result of calling it with `a` and `b`, or `std::nullopt` if there is no such operation.

With `auto ops = make_ops();`, `calc(ops, "-", 2, 9)` is -7 and `calc(ops, "/", 8, 2)` is `std::nullopt`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>> make_ops() {
    return {};
}

std::optional<int> calc(const std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>>& ops, const std::string& op, int a, int b) {
    return std::nullopt;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>> make_ops() {
    std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>> ops;
    ops["+"] = [](int a, int b) { return a + b; };
    ops["-"] = [](int a, int b) { return a - b; };
    ops["*"] = [](int a, int b) { return a * b; };
    ops["max"] = [](int a, int b) { return std::max(a, b); };
    return ops;
}

std::optional<int> calc(const std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int, int)>>& ops, const std::string& op, int a, int b) {
    auto it = ops.find(op);
    if (it == ops.end()) return std::nullopt;
    return it->second(a, b);
}
--- hint
Each entry is a lambda with the signature `int(int, int)`, stored under its name: `ops["+"] = [](int a, int b) { return a + b; };`.
--- hint
`ops` is a `const` map, so look the name up with `find`. If it is found, `it->second` is the stored function: call it like any function, `it->second(a, b)`.
--- check test | All four operations
[] { auto ops = make_ops(); return calc(ops, "+", 2, 9) == 11 && calc(ops, "-", 2, 9) == -7 && calc(ops, "*", -3, 4) == -12 && calc(ops, "max", -3, -8) == -3 && ops.size() == 4; }()
--- check test | An unknown operation gives nullopt
[] { auto ops = make_ops(); return calc(ops, "/", 8, 2) == std::nullopt && calc(ops, "", 1, 1) == std::nullopt; }()
--- check test | The table can take a new entry
[] { auto ops = make_ops(); ops["pow2"] = [](int a, int b) { return a * a + b * 0; }; return calc(ops, "pow2", 7, 0) == 49; }()

+++ practice | A validator made of rules
--- task
Write `class Validator`, which checks a number against named rules. Each rule is a `std::function<bool(int)>` that returns `true` when the number passes. No `main`.

- `void add_rule(const std::string& name, std::function<bool(int)> rule)` adds a rule.
- `std::vector<std::string> failures(int x) const` returns the names of the rules that `x` fails, in the order the rules were added.
- `bool ok(int x) const` is `true` when `x` fails no rule. With no rules at all, every number is fine.

With a rule `"positive"` (x > 0) and a rule `"even"` (x % 2 == 0), `failures(-3)` is `{"positive", "even"}` and `ok(4)` is `true`.
--- starter
#include <functional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Validator {
public:
    void add_rule(const std::string& name, std::function<bool(int)> rule) {}
    std::vector<std::string> failures(int x) const { return {}; }
    bool ok(int x) const { return false; }
};
--- solution
#include <functional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Validator {
public:
    void add_rule(const std::string& name, std::function<bool(int)> rule) {
        rules_.push_back({name, std::move(rule)});
    }

    std::vector<std::string> failures(int x) const {
        std::vector<std::string> out;
        for (const auto& r : rules_) {
            if (!r.test(x)) out.push_back(r.name);
        }
        return out;
    }

    bool ok(int x) const { return failures(x).empty(); }

private:
    struct Rule {
        std::string name;
        std::function<bool(int)> test;
    };
    std::vector<Rule> rules_;
};
--- hint
Keep each rule as a small struct with a `name` and a `std::function<bool(int)>`, in a private `std::vector`. The vector keeps them in the order they were added.
--- hint
`failures` calls each stored function with `x` and collects the names of those that return `false`. `ok` can simply ask whether `failures(x)` is empty.
--- check case | Two failures, in order
[] { Validator v; v.add_rule("positive", [](int x) { return x > 0; }); v.add_rule("even", [](int x) { return x % 2 == 0; }); return v.failures(-3); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"positive", "even"}
--- check test | A number that passes everything
[] { Validator v; v.add_rule("positive", [](int x) { return x > 0; }); v.add_rule("even", [](int x) { return x % 2 == 0; }); return v.ok(4) && v.failures(4).empty() && !v.ok(3); }()
--- check test | No rules: everything is fine
[] { Validator v; return v.ok(-99) && v.failures(0).empty(); }()
--- check test | A rule that captures a limit
[] { Validator v; int limit = 10; v.add_rule("small", [limit](int x) { return x < limit; }); limit = 100; return v.failures(50) == std::vector<std::string>{"small"} && v.ok(9); }()

+++ practice | Rules built from rules
--- task
Write three functions that each **return** a new rule built from other rules. A rule is a `std::function<bool(int)>`. No `main`.

- `std::function<bool(int)> all_pass(std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> rules)` returns a rule that is `true` when every rule in `rules` is `true`. With no rules, it is always `true`.
- `std::function<bool(int)> any_pass(std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> rules)` returns a rule that is `true` when at least one rule is `true`. With no rules, it is always `false`.
- `std::function<bool(int)> negate(std::function<bool(int)> rule)` returns a rule that is `true` exactly when `rule` is `false`.

With `positive` and `even` as rules, `all_pass({positive, even})(4)` is `true` and `(3)` is `false`, while `any_pass({positive, even})(3)` is `true`.
--- starter
#include <functional>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::function<bool(int)> all_pass(std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> rules) {
    return [](int) { return true; };
}

std::function<bool(int)> any_pass(std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> rules) {
    return [](int) { return true; };
}

std::function<bool(int)> negate(std::function<bool(int)> rule) {
    return rule;
}
--- solution
#include <functional>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::function<bool(int)> all_pass(std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> rules) {
    return [rules](int x) {
        for (const auto& r : rules) {
            if (!r(x)) return false;
        }
        return true;
    };
}

std::function<bool(int)> any_pass(std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> rules) {
    return [rules](int x) {
        for (const auto& r : rules) {
            if (r(x)) return true;
        }
        return false;
    };
}

std::function<bool(int)> negate(std::function<bool(int)> rule) {
    return [rule](int x) { return !rule(x); };
}
--- hint
Each function returns a lambda. The lambda must capture the rules **by value**: they are parameters, gone once the function returns, and the lambda is called later.
--- hint
Inside `all_pass`'s lambda, loop over the captured rules and return `false` at the first one that fails, then `true` after the loop. `any_pass` is the mirror image. `negate` returns `[rule](int x) { return !rule(x); }`.
--- check test | all_pass and any_pass on two rules
[] { std::function<bool(int)> positive = [](int x) { return x > 0; }; std::function<bool(int)> even = [](int x) { return x % 2 == 0; }; auto both = all_pass({positive, even}); auto either = any_pass({positive, even}); return both(4) && !both(3) && !both(-2) && either(3) && either(-2) && !either(-3); }()
--- check test | With no rules
[] { auto a = all_pass({}); auto b = any_pass({}); return a(0) && a(-5) && !b(0) && !b(7); }()
--- check test | negate, and rules nested inside rules
[] { std::function<bool(int)> big = [](int x) { return x > 100; }; auto small = negate(big); auto odd = negate([](int x) { return x % 2 == 0; }); auto small_odd = all_pass({small, odd}); return small(5) && !small(500) && small_odd(7) && !small_odd(8) && !small_odd(101); }()
--- check test | The rules outlive the call that built them
[] { std::function<bool(int)> r; { std::vector<std::function<bool(int)>> list{[](int x) { return x != 0; }}; r = all_pass(list); } return r(3) && !r(0); }()

+++ practice | A button that may have no handler
--- task
Write `class Button`, which calls one stored handler, a `std::function<void()>`, when clicked. The handler may be missing, and calling an empty `std::function` ends the program, so every edge must be handled. No `main`.

- `void on_click(std::function<void()> handler)` stores `handler`, replacing any earlier one. It may be given an empty `std::function`.
- `void clear()` removes the handler.
- `bool click()` calls the handler and returns `true`, or returns `false` if there is no handler (never set, cleared, or set to an empty function).
- `int clicks() const` is how many clicks really called a handler.
--- starter
#include <functional>
#include <utility>

class Button {
public:
    void on_click(std::function<void()> handler) { handler_ = std::move(handler); }
    void clear() {}
    bool click() {
        handler_();
        return true;
    }
    int clicks() const { return 0; }
private:
    std::function<void()> handler_;
};
--- solution
#include <functional>
#include <utility>

class Button {
public:
    void on_click(std::function<void()> handler) { handler_ = std::move(handler); }
    void clear() { handler_ = nullptr; }
    bool click() {
        if (!handler_) return false;
        handler_();
        ++clicks_;
        return true;
    }
    int clicks() const { return clicks_; }
private:
    std::function<void()> handler_;
    int clicks_ = 0;
};
--- hint
A `std::function` can be tested like a `bool`: `if (!handler_)` is true when it holds nothing. Check that before calling it.
--- hint
`clear()` can set `handler_ = nullptr;`, which empties it. Keep an `int clicks_ = 0;` member that goes up only after a real call.
--- check test | Clicking calls the handler
[] { Button b; int n = 0; b.on_click([&n] { ++n; }); bool a = b.click(); bool c = b.click(); return a && c && n == 2 && b.clicks() == 2; }()
--- check test | A new button has no handler
[] { Button b; return !b.click() && b.clicks() == 0; }()
--- check test | Cleared, or given an empty function
[] { Button b; int n = 0; b.on_click([&n] { ++n; }); b.clear(); bool after_clear = b.click(); b.on_click(std::function<void()>()); bool after_empty = b.click(); return !after_clear && !after_empty && n == 0 && b.clicks() == 0; }()
--- check test | A new handler replaces the old one
[] { Button b; int first = 0, second = 0; b.on_click([&first] { ++first; }); b.click(); b.on_click([&second] { ++second; }); b.click(); b.click(); return first == 1 && second == 2 && b.clicks() == 3; }()

+++ practice | Debug: running a command nobody added
--- task
**Bug report:** "`run` with a name nobody added crashes the program. And if I call `has` first with that name I get `false`, but after a `run` with it, `has` would say `true`."

`Commands` maps names to functions. `run(name, x)` should call the function stored under `name` with `x` and return its result, or return `std::nullopt` if the name is unknown **or** its stored function is empty. It must never add a name. Fix `run`, keeping its name, parameters and return type. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <functional>
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

class Commands {
public:
    void add(const std::string& name, std::function<int(int)> fn) { table_[name] = std::move(fn); }
    std::optional<int> run(const std::string& name, int x) { return table_[name](x); }
    bool has(const std::string& name) const { return table_.count(name) > 0; }
private:
    std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int)>> table_;
};
--- solution
#include <functional>
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

class Commands {
public:
    void add(const std::string& name, std::function<int(int)> fn) { table_[name] = std::move(fn); }
    std::optional<int> run(const std::string& name, int x) {
        auto it = table_.find(name);
        if (it == table_.end() || !it->second) return std::nullopt;
        return it->second(x);
    }
    bool has(const std::string& name) const { return table_.count(name) > 0; }
private:
    std::map<std::string, std::function<int(int)>> table_;
};
--- hint
`table_[name]` on a missing name adds it, holding an **empty** `std::function`, and then the code calls that empty function. Two problems from one `[ ]`.
--- hint
Use `table_.find(name)`. Return `std::nullopt` if it is `table_.end()`, or if `it->second` is empty (test it like a `bool`). Otherwise return `it->second(x)`.
--- check test | A known command runs
[] { Commands c; c.add("double", [](int x) { return x * 2; }); return c.run("double", 21) == 42; }()
--- check test | An unknown name gives nullopt and adds nothing
[] { Commands c; auto r = c.run("launch", 1); return r == std::nullopt && !c.has("launch"); }()
--- check test | An empty stored function gives nullopt
[] { Commands c; c.add("noop", std::function<int(int)>()); return c.run("noop", 5) == std::nullopt && c.has("noop"); }()

+++ practice | Stretch: a job scheduler
--- task
Write `class Scheduler`, which runs jobs when a clock reaches their time. A job is a name and a `std::function<void()>`. The clock starts at tick 0. No `main`.

- `void at(int tick, const std::string& name, std::function<void()> job)` schedules `job` to run at `tick`.
- `std::vector<std::string> advance(int ticks)` moves the clock forward by `ticks` (never negative), then runs every job whose tick is at or before the new time: earliest tick first, and jobs with the same tick in the order they were scheduled. Each job runs once and is then removed. It returns the names of the jobs it ran, in the order it ran them.
- `int now() const` is the current tick, and `std::size_t pending() const` is how many jobs are still waiting.

Jobs never schedule other jobs. After `at(5, "b", …)`, `at(2, "a", …)` and `at(5, "c", …)`, `advance(5)` runs and returns `{"a", "b", "c"}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <functional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Scheduler {
public:
    void at(int tick, const std::string& name, std::function<void()> job) {}
    std::vector<std::string> advance(int ticks) { return {}; }
    int now() const { return 0; }
    std::size_t pending() const { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <functional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Scheduler {
public:
    void at(int tick, const std::string& name, std::function<void()> job) {
        jobs_.push_back({tick, name, std::move(job)});
    }

    std::vector<std::string> advance(int ticks) {
        now_ += ticks;
        std::vector<Job> due;
        std::vector<Job> later;
        for (Job& j : jobs_) {
            if (j.tick <= now_) due.push_back(std::move(j));
            else later.push_back(std::move(j));
        }
        jobs_ = std::move(later);
        std::stable_sort(due.begin(), due.end(), [](const Job& a, const Job& b) { return a.tick < b.tick; });
        std::vector<std::string> ran;
        for (const Job& j : due) {
            j.run();
            ran.push_back(j.name);
        }
        return ran;
    }

    int now() const { return now_; }
    std::size_t pending() const { return jobs_.size(); }

private:
    struct Job {
        int tick;
        std::string name;
        std::function<void()> run;
    };
    std::vector<Job> jobs_;
    int now_ = 0;
};
--- hint
Keep the jobs as small structs `{ tick, name, function }` in a private vector, plus an `int now_ = 0;`. In `advance`, first split the jobs into those that are due and those that are not, and keep only the ones not due.
--- hint
Sort the due jobs with `std::stable_sort` by tick: "stable" keeps jobs with the same tick in the order they were scheduled. Then call each one's function and collect its name.
--- check test | Earliest first, ties in the order scheduled
[] { Scheduler s; std::vector<std::string> log; s.at(5, "b", [&log] { log.push_back("B"); }); s.at(2, "a", [&log] { log.push_back("A"); }); s.at(5, "c", [&log] { log.push_back("C"); }); auto ran = s.advance(5); return ran == std::vector<std::string>{"a", "b", "c"} && log == std::vector<std::string>{"A", "B", "C"} && s.pending() == 0 && s.now() == 5; }()
--- check test | Jobs in the future wait
[] { Scheduler s; int hits = 0; s.at(10, "late", [&hits] { ++hits; }); auto a = s.advance(3); auto b = s.advance(6); bool waiting = a.empty() && b.empty() && s.pending() == 1 && hits == 0; auto c = s.advance(1); return waiting && c == std::vector<std::string>{"late"} && hits == 1 && s.now() == 10; }()
--- check test | Each job runs once
[] { Scheduler s; int hits = 0; s.at(1, "x", [&hits] { ++hits; }); s.advance(2); s.advance(2); return hits == 1 && s.pending() == 0; }()
--- check test | A job already due runs on advance(0)
[] { Scheduler s; s.advance(4); int hits = 0; s.at(0, "past", [&hits] { ++hits; }); auto r = s.advance(0); return r == std::vector<std::string>{"past"} && hits == 1; }()

=== cpp3-11 | std::variant and std::visit
--- teach
Last lesson, `std::function` let one type hold any callable. In the intermediate course, `std::optional<T>` held "a T, or nothing". This lesson's type holds one value out of a short list of types, and always knows which one it is holding.

### Exactly one of several

Picture a parking space with a sign next to it. The space fits a car, a bike or a van, but only one at a time, and the sign always says which is parked there right now. You never have to guess.

`std::variant<A, B, C>`, from `<variant>`, is that parking space. It holds **exactly one** of `A`, `B` or `C`. It is a type-safe **[[union|union-history]]**: a value that can be one of several types, and that remembers which one it holds.

```cpp
#include <variant>

std::variant<int, double, std::string> v = 42;   // holds an int
v = "hello";                                     // now holds a std::string
```

Writing that long type again and again is tiring. `using Value = …;` gives a type a shorter name, so from here on `Value` means that whole variant:

```cpp
using Value = std::variant<int, double, std::string>;
Value v = 2.5;   // holds a double
```

### Asking what is inside

Three ways to ask a variant about its contents:

```cpp
Value v = "hello";

v.index();                          // 2: the position of the held type (int 0, double 1, string 2)
std::holds_alternative<int>(v);     // false: it is not holding an int
std::get_if<std::string>(&v);       // pointer to the string, or nullptr
```

`std::get_if` takes the **address** of the variant, `&v`, and gives back a [[pointer|get-if-pointer]]. If `v` holds a string, the pointer points at it. If not, it is `nullptr`, the pointer to nothing. So you can test and use it in one go:

```cpp
if (auto* s = std::get_if<std::string>(&v)) {
    // here s points at the string: s->size() is 5
}
```

`std::get<T>(v)` also exists, and gives you the value directly. But if `v` is not holding a `T`, it signals an error with an exception, and with exceptions switched off, as in this app, a wrong guess ends the program. Prefer `get_if` or `holds_alternative`.

### std::visit: handle every case

The real power is **`std::visit`**: it calls a function with whatever the variant holds. You give it a **visitor**, something callable with each of the variant's types, and the variant itself.

A common way to build a visitor bundles several lambdas, one per type, into one object. It needs this helper, one line long:

```cpp
template <class... Fs> struct overloaded : Fs... { using Fs::operator()...; };
```

For now, treat `overloaded` as a recipe to copy: it bundles several lambdas into one object that has [[all of their call operators|overloaded-recipe]]. How its `class... Fs` works (variadic templates) is covered in the expert course.

With it, a function that describes any `Value`:

```cpp
std::string describe(const Value& v) {
    return std::visit(overloaded{
        [](int i)                { return "int " + std::to_string(i); },
        [](double)               { return std::string("a double"); },
        [](const std::string& s) { return "text " + s; },
    }, v);
}
// describe(Value(42)) is "int 42", describe(Value(2.5)) is "a double"
```

`[](double)` has a parameter with no name: that lambda does not need the value, only the fact that it is a double. Every lambda must return the **same** type, here `std::string`, because `std::visit` has one return type.

Leave out a case and, usually, it does not compile: the compiler checks that the visitor can handle every type in the variant.

**Watch out:** that check is about what can be *called*, not what you meant. Numbers convert to each other, so if you leave out the `double` lambda above, a `double` quietly goes to the `int` lambda: `describe(Value(2.5))` becomes `"int 2"`, with no error and no warning. Write one lambda for every type in the variant, in the same order, so a missing one stands out.

### Closed sets and open sets

A variant is the right tool for a **closed** set of alternatives: a list you know in full, up front. Tokens in a parser (a number, a name, a symbol), the kinds of value in a spreadsheet cell. Adding a new type means changing the variant, and then every `std::visit` that misses it is flagged.

Virtual functions suit the [[opposite: an open set|closed-and-open]] of types that other people will add later, each in its own class, without touching your code.

### Visiting two variants at once

`std::visit` can also take **two** variants. The visitor then gets both values, one from each, which is handy for "combine two values of mixed kinds":

```cpp
std::visit(visitor, a, b);   // calls visitor(value in a, value in b)
```

With three types in each variant, that is 3 × 3 = 9 combinations, and the visitor must handle all nine. Writing nine lambdas would be a lot. The next step shows a shorter way.

### One lambda for every type

Instead of one lambda per type, you can write a single **generic lambda**, one with `auto` as its parameter type:

```cpp
auto show = [](const auto& x) { /* … */ };
```

The compiler stamps out a fresh copy of it for each type it is called with, the same way it does for a function template. Inside, you can ask what `x` really is, in three small steps.

**Its plain type.** `decltype(x)`, read "declared type of x", gives the type `x` was declared with: for a string that is `const std::string&`. `std::decay_t<…>` strips the `const` and `&`, leaving `std::string`:

```cpp
using X = std::decay_t<decltype(x)>;   // X is int, double or std::string
```

**Comparing types.** `std::is_same_v<A, B>`, from `<type_traits>`, is `true` when two types are the same type:

```cpp
constexpr bool is_text = std::is_same_v<X, std::string>;
```

**Choosing a branch.** **`if constexpr (…)`** is an `if` decided while compiling. It keeps only the branch that applies to this type; the [[other branches are not even compiled|why-if-constexpr]] for it:

```cpp
if constexpr (std::is_same_v<X, std::string>) {
    // only compiled when x is a string: x.size() is fine here
} else {
    // only compiled when x is an int or a double
}
```

### Saying the return type

A lambda normally works out its return type from its `return` statements. When its branches would return different types, say the return type yourself by writing `->` and the type after the parameters:

```cpp
auto half = [](const auto& x) -> std::optional<Value> {
    using X = std::decay_t<decltype(x)>;
    if constexpr (std::is_same_v<X, std::string>) {
        return std::nullopt;            // no half of a string
    } else {
        return Value(x / 2.0);          // a double
    }
};
std::visit(half, Value(3));             // an optional holding the double 1.5
```

Without `-> std::optional<Value>`, one branch returns `std::nullopt` and the other a `Value`, and the compiler would stop, because a lambda cannot return two different types.

::: context union-history Where "union" comes from
C, the language C++ grew out of, has a `union`: several members that share one piece of memory, big enough for the largest. It does not remember which member you stored last. Read the wrong one and you get undefined behavior. The programmer has to keep track, usually with a separate number called a **tag**.

A `std::variant` keeps the tag for you, which is why it is called a tagged union:

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="240" height="44" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="140" y="56" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">room for the largest: int, double or string</text>
  <rect x="266" y="30" width="74" height="44" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="303" y="56" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">index: 2</text>
  <text x="140" y="96" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">the value</text>
  <text x="303" y="96" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">which one</text>
  <text x="180" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">one std::variant&lt;int, double, std::string&gt;</text>
</svg>
```

On a typical 64-bit build with GCC or Clang, this `Value` takes 40 bytes: 32 for a `std::string`, the largest of the three, and the rest for the index and padding.
:::

::: context get-if-pointer Why get_if works with pointers
A pointer can say "nothing" in a way a reference cannot: it can be `nullptr`. That makes it a natural answer for a question that might have no answer, "give me the string, if there is one". The same pattern comes back in the next lesson, on errors without exceptions, where `std::get_if<ParseError>(&result)` checks whether a function failed.

`auto* s` means "a pointer, of whatever type the right side gives": here `std::string*`. Inside the `if`, `s` is known not to be `nullptr`, so following it with `->` is safe.
:::

::: context overloaded-recipe What the overloaded recipe does
Each lambda is an object of its own unnamed class, with a call operator, `operator()`. `overloaded` is a struct that inherits from every one of those classes at once, so it has all their call operators. The `using Fs::operator()...;` line brings them together into one overload set, so the compiler can pick the right one for each argument, exactly as it picks between overloaded functions, a choice you met in the overloading lesson.

`overloaded{ lambda1, lambda2, lambda3 }` then builds one object from the three lambdas. The `...` is what lets one line handle any number of lambdas; that is the variadic template part the expert course explains.
:::

::: context closed-and-open Two ways to handle "many kinds"
With virtual functions, adding a new *type* is easy: write one new class, and no old code changes. Adding a new *operation* is hard: every class needs a new function.

With a variant it is the other way round. Adding a new operation is easy: write one new `std::visit`. Adding a new type is hard: every visit must handle it, and the compiler points them out.

So ask which will grow. A set of shapes that users of your library will extend is open: use an interface. The kinds of value in a spreadsheet cell, fixed by you, are closed: use a variant.
:::

::: context why-if-constexpr Why a normal if is not enough
In a normal `if`, both branches are compiled, even the one that will never run. Inside a generic lambda stamped out for `int`, a branch that calls `x.size()` would fail to compile, because an `int` has no `.size()`, even though that branch could never run for an int.

`if constexpr` needs a condition the compiler can work out while compiling, such as `std::is_same_v<X, std::string>`. It then throws away the branch that does not apply before checking it, so each stamped-out copy contains only code that makes sense for its type. The `constexpr` in its name is the next lesson's subject: work done by the compiler.
:::
--- task
Start from `using Value = std::variant<int, double, std::string>;` (it is in the starter). Write these three functions, no `main`:

- `std::string type_name(const Value& v)` — returns `"int"`, `"double"` or `"string"`, for the type `v` holds.
- `std::string to_text(const Value& v)` — ints as their digits (`42` gives `"42"`); doubles the way a default `std::ostringstream` prints them (`2.5` gives `"2.5"`); strings inside double quotes (`"hi"` gives `"\"hi\""`, the text `"hi"` with its quote marks).
- `std::optional<Value> add(const Value& a, const Value& b)`:
  - int + int gives an int (2 + 3 is the int 5);
  - any other mix of int and double gives a double (1 + 0.5 is 1.5; 0.25 + 2 is 2.25);
  - string + string joins them (`"ab"` + `"cd"` is `"abcd"`);
  - a string with a number gives `std::nullopt`.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <variant>

using Value = std::variant<int, double, std::string>;

std::string type_name(const Value& v) {
    return "";
}

std::string to_text(const Value& v) {
    return "";
}

std::optional<Value> add(const Value& a, const Value& b) {
    return std::nullopt;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <variant>

using Value = std::variant<int, double, std::string>;

template <class... Fs> struct overloaded : Fs... { using Fs::operator()...; };

std::string type_name(const Value& v) {
    return std::visit(overloaded{
        [](int) { return std::string("int"); },
        [](double) { return std::string("double"); },
        [](const std::string&) { return std::string("string"); },
    }, v);
}

std::string to_text(const Value& v) {
    return std::visit(overloaded{
        [](int i) { return std::to_string(i); },
        [](double d) {
            std::ostringstream out;
            out << d;
            return out.str();
        },
        [](const std::string& s) { return "\"" + s + "\""; },
    }, v);
}

std::optional<Value> add(const Value& a, const Value& b) {
    return std::visit([](const auto& x, const auto& y) -> std::optional<Value> {
        using X = std::decay_t<decltype(x)>;
        using Y = std::decay_t<decltype(y)>;
        constexpr bool xs = std::is_same_v<X, std::string>;
        constexpr bool ys = std::is_same_v<Y, std::string>;
        if constexpr (xs && ys) {
            return Value(x + y);
        } else if constexpr (xs || ys) {
            return std::nullopt;
        } else if constexpr (std::is_same_v<X, int> && std::is_same_v<Y, int>) {
            return Value(x + y);
        } else {
            return Value(static_cast<double>(x) + static_cast<double>(y));
        }
    }, a, b);
}
--- hint
`type_name` and `to_text` are one `std::visit` each with three lambdas, one per type. Copy the `overloaded` helper line from the lesson above them, and make every lambda return a `std::string`.
--- hint
For `add`, `std::visit` can take both values, with one generic lambda that states its return type: `std::visit([](const auto& x, const auto& y) -> std::optional<Value> { … }, a, b)`. Add `#include <type_traits>` for `std::is_same_v`.
--- hint
Inside that lambda, `std::is_same_v<std::decay_t<decltype(x)>, std::string>` tells you whether `x` is a string. With `if constexpr`, check "both strings" first (return `Value(x + y)`), then "one string" (`std::nullopt`), then "both ints" (`Value(x + y)`), and otherwise return `Value(static_cast<double>(x) + static_cast<double>(y))`.
--- check test | type_name
type_name(Value(3)) == "int" && type_name(Value(2.5)) == "double" && type_name(Value(std::string("a"))) == "string"
--- check test | to_text formats each kind
to_text(Value(42)) == "42" && to_text(Value(2.5)) == "2.5" && to_text(Value(std::string("hi"))) == "\"hi\""
--- check test | int + int stays an int
[] { auto r = add(Value(2), Value(3)); return r && std::holds_alternative<int>(*r) && *std::get_if<int>(&*r) == 5; }()
--- check test | int + double becomes a double
[] { auto r = add(Value(1), Value(0.5)); auto s = add(Value(0.25), Value(2)); return r && std::holds_alternative<double>(*r) && *std::get_if<double>(&*r) == 1.5 && s && *std::get_if<double>(&*s) == 2.25; }()
--- check test | Strings join
[] { auto r = add(Value(std::string("ab")), Value(std::string("cd"))); return r && std::holds_alternative<std::string>(*r) && *std::get_if<std::string>(&*r) == "abcd"; }()
--- check test | A string and a number do not add
!add(Value(std::string("a")), Value(1)).has_value() && !add(Value(2.0), Value(std::string("b"))).has_value()

+++ practice | Tokens: numbers, symbols and words
--- task
A parser splits text into **tokens**, each a number, a one-character symbol or a word. The starter declares `using Token = std::variant<int, char, std::string>;`. Write three functions. No `main`.

- `std::string kind(const Token& t)` returns `"number"`, `"symbol"` or `"word"`. Use `std::visit` with one lambda per type (the `overloaded` helper from the lesson).
- `int count_numbers(const std::vector<Token>& tokens)` counts the tokens holding an `int`.
- `std::string join_words(const std::vector<Token>& tokens)` joins the words, in order, with single spaces between them, skipping every other token. With no words it returns `""`.

For the tokens `"x"`, `'='`, `3`, `'+'`, `"y"`: `count_numbers` is 1 and `join_words` is `"x y"`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <variant>
#include <vector>

using Token = std::variant<int, char, std::string>;

std::string kind(const Token& t) {
    return "";
}

int count_numbers(const std::vector<Token>& tokens) {
    return 0;
}

std::string join_words(const std::vector<Token>& tokens) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <variant>
#include <vector>

using Token = std::variant<int, char, std::string>;

template <class... Fs> struct overloaded : Fs... { using Fs::operator()...; };

std::string kind(const Token& t) {
    return std::visit(overloaded{
        [](int) { return std::string("number"); },
        [](char) { return std::string("symbol"); },
        [](const std::string&) { return std::string("word"); },
    }, t);
}

int count_numbers(const std::vector<Token>& tokens) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const Token& t : tokens) {
        if (std::holds_alternative<int>(t)) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}

std::string join_words(const std::vector<Token>& tokens) {
    std::string out;
    for (const Token& t : tokens) {
