+++ problem | A string type of your own
--- task
`Text` keeps its characters in a `char` array on the heap. The starter has its constructor, destructor and a few readers, but its copies are shallow and it has no move functions and no operators. Finish it. No `main`.

- A deep copy constructor and a deep copy assignment operator. Each adds 1 to the static counter `Text::copies`. Self-assignment changes nothing.
- A move constructor and a move assignment operator, both `noexcept`, that copy no characters and count nothing. The moved-from `Text` is left empty: `size()` 0 and `str()` `""`. Move assignment frees the target's own array first, and moving a `Text` onto itself changes nothing.
- Non-member `operator+`, joining two texts into a new one, so that `Text("ab") + Text("cd")` is `"abcd"` and `std::string("x") + Text("y")` works too (the constructor is not `explicit`).
- Non-member `==`, true when the characters are the same, and `operator<<`, which prints the characters and returns the stream.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <ostream>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Text {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;

    Text(const std::string& s = "") : size_(s.size()), data_(new char[s.size()]) {
        std::copy(s.begin(), s.end(), data_);
    }
    ~Text() { delete[] data_; }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    char at(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, char c) { data_[i] = c; }
    std::string str() const { return size_ == 0 ? std::string() : std::string(data_, data_ + size_); }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    char* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <ostream>
#include <string>
#include <type_traits>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Text {
public:
    static inline int copies = 0;

    Text(const std::string& s = "") : size_(s.size()), data_(new char[s.size()]) {
        std::copy(s.begin(), s.end(), data_);
    }
    ~Text() { delete[] data_; }

    Text(const Text& other) : size_(other.size_), data_(new char[other.size_]) {
        std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + size_, data_);
        ++copies;
    }

    Text& operator=(const Text& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            char* fresh = new char[other.size_];
            std::copy(other.data_, other.data_ + other.size_, fresh);
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = fresh;
            size_ = other.size_;
            ++copies;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    Text(Text&& other) noexcept : size_(other.size_), data_(other.data_) {
        other.size_ = 0;
        other.data_ = nullptr;
    }

    Text& operator=(Text&& other) noexcept {
        if (this != &other) {
            delete[] data_;
            data_ = other.data_;
            size_ = other.size_;
            other.data_ = nullptr;
            other.size_ = 0;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    char at(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, char c) { data_[i] = c; }
    std::string str() const { return size_ == 0 ? std::string() : std::string(data_, data_ + size_); }

private:
    std::size_t size_;
    char* data_;
};

Text operator+(const Text& a, const Text& b) { return Text(a.str() + b.str()); }

bool operator==(const Text& a, const Text& b) { return a.str() == b.str(); }

std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& out, const Text& t) { return out << t.str(); }
--- check test | A copy is deep and counted
[] { Text::copies = 0; Text a("orbit"); Text b = a; b.set(0, 'O'); return a.str() == "orbit" && b.str() == "Orbit" && Text::copies == 1; }()
--- check test | Copy assignment between sizes, and self-assignment
[] { Text::copies = 0; Text a("io"); Text b("callisto"); b = a; a.set(0, 'X'); Text& same = b; b = same; return b.str() == "io" && b.size() == 2 && Text::copies == 1; }()
--- check test | Moves copy nothing and leave the source empty
[] { Text::copies = 0; Text a("burn"); Text b = std::move(a); Text c("x"); c = std::move(b); Text& same = c; c = std::move(same); return c.str() == "burn" && a.size() == 0 && a.str() == "" && b.size() == 0 && Text::copies == 0 && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Text> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Text>; }()
--- check test | A growing vector of texts never copies
[] { Text::copies = 0; std::vector<Text> v; for (int i = 0; i < 100; ++i) v.push_back(Text("t" + std::to_string(i))); return Text::copies == 0 && v[57].str() == "t57"; }()
--- check test | Joining and comparing
(Text("ab") + Text("cd")) == Text("abcd") && std::string("x") + Text("y") == Text("xy") && !(Text("a") == Text("b")) && Text("") + Text("") == Text()
--- check case | Printing
[] { std::ostringstream o; o << Text("go") << "|" << Text() << "|" << Text("ab") + Text("c"); return o.str(); }()
=> "go||abc"

+++ problem | Directory sizes
--- task
A directory tree: each `Dir` has a name, the subdirectories it owns through `unique_ptr`, and its files as `{name, size}` pairs. Write four functions. No `main`.

- `Dir& mkdir(Dir& parent, const std::string& name)` returns the subdirectory of `parent` with that name, making it (at the end of `dirs`) if it does not exist yet.
- `long long total_size(const Dir& d)` is the size of every file in `d` and in every directory below it.
- `const Dir* find(const Dir& root, const std::string& path)` follows a path of names separated by `/`, like `"src/app"`, starting from `root`, and returns a pointer to that directory, or `nullptr` if any part is missing. The empty path `""` is `root` itself.
- `std::vector<std::string> small_dirs(const Dir& root, long long limit)` returns the path of every directory **below** `root` (not `root` itself) whose total size is at most `limit`. A path is written like `"src/app"`. They are ordered smallest total first, and equal totals by path A→Z.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <tuple>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Dir {
    std::string name;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Dir>> dirs;
    std::vector<std::pair<std::string, long long>> files;
};

Dir& mkdir(Dir& parent, const std::string& name) {
    return parent;
}

long long total_size(const Dir& d) {
    return 0;
}

const Dir* find(const Dir& root, const std::string& path) {
    return nullptr;
}

std::vector<std::string> small_dirs(const Dir& root, long long limit) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <memory>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <tuple>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Dir {
    std::string name;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Dir>> dirs;
    std::vector<std::pair<std::string, long long>> files;
};

Dir& mkdir(Dir& parent, const std::string& name) {
    for (const auto& d : parent.dirs) {
        if (d->name == name) return *d;
    }
    parent.dirs.push_back(std::make_unique<Dir>());
    parent.dirs.back()->name = name;
    return *parent.dirs.back();
}

long long total_size(const Dir& d) {
    long long total = 0;
    for (const auto& f : d.files) total += f.second;
    for (const auto& sub : d.dirs) total += total_size(*sub);
    return total;
}

const Dir* find(const Dir& root, const std::string& path) {
    const Dir* at = &root;
    if (path.empty()) return at;
    std::istringstream in(path);
    std::string part;
    while (std::getline(in, part, '/')) {
        const Dir* next = nullptr;
        for (const auto& d : at->dirs) {
            if (d->name == part) next = d.get();
        }
        if (next == nullptr) return nullptr;
        at = next;
    }
    return at;
}

void collect(const Dir& d, const std::string& path, std::vector<std::pair<long long, std::string>>& out) {
    for (const auto& sub : d.dirs) {
        std::string p = path.empty() ? sub->name : path + "/" + sub->name;
        out.push_back({total_size(*sub), p});
        collect(*sub, p, out);
    }
}

std::vector<std::string> small_dirs(const Dir& root, long long limit) {
    std::vector<std::pair<long long, std::string>> all;
    collect(root, "", all);
    std::sort(all.begin(), all.end(), [](const std::pair<long long, std::string>& a, const std::pair<long long, std::string>& b) {
        return std::tie(a.first, a.second) < std::tie(b.first, b.second);
    });
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    for (const auto& [size, path] : all) {
        if (size <= limit) out.push_back(path);
    }
    return out;
}
--- check test | mkdir makes a directory once, and reuses it
[] { Dir root{"", {}, {}}; Dir& a = mkdir(root, "src"); Dir& b = mkdir(root, "src"); mkdir(root, "docs"); return &a == &b && root.dirs.size() == 2 && root.dirs[1]->name == "docs"; }()
--- check test | total_size counts every level
[] { Dir root{"", {}, {}}; root.files.push_back({"readme", 5}); Dir& src = mkdir(root, "src"); src.files.push_back({"main", 100}); mkdir(src, "app").files.push_back({"ui", 40}); return total_size(root) == 145 && total_size(src) == 140; }()
--- check test | find follows paths
[] { Dir root{"", {}, {}}; Dir& src = mkdir(root, "src"); Dir& app = mkdir(src, "app"); return find(root, "src/app") == &app && find(root, "src") == &src && find(root, "") == &root && find(root, "src/lib") == nullptr && find(root, "app") == nullptr && find(src, "app") == &app; }()
--- check case | small_dirs: smallest first, ties by path
[] { Dir root{"", {}, {}}; Dir& src = mkdir(root, "src"); src.files.push_back({"a", 10}); Dir& app = mkdir(src, "app"); app.files.push_back({"b", 30}); Dir& docs = mkdir(root, "docs"); docs.files.push_back({"c", 30}); mkdir(root, "tmp"); return small_dirs(root, 35); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"tmp", "docs", "src/app"}
--- check test | Nothing small enough, and an empty tree
[] { Dir root{"", {}, {}}; bool none = small_dirs(root, 100).empty(); Dir& big = mkdir(root, "big"); big.files.push_back({"x", 1000}); return none && small_dirs(root, 999).empty() && small_dirs(root, 1000) == std::vector<std::string>{"big"}; }()

+++ problem | Fibonacci by matrix powers, at compile time
--- task
Fast exponentiation computes xⁿ in about log₂ n multiplications: for each step, if `n` is odd multiply the result by `x`, then halve `n` and square `x`. It works for any type with a `*`. Write these, all usable at compile time. No `main`.

- `struct Mat2 { long long a, b, c, d; };` is the 2×2 matrix with top row `a b` and bottom row `c d`.
- `constexpr` non-member `operator*` (the usual matrix product: top-left `x.a*y.a + x.b*y.c`, top-right `x.a*y.b + x.b*y.d`, bottom-left `x.c*y.a + x.d*y.c`, bottom-right `x.c*y.b + x.d*y.d`) and `operator==` for `Mat2`.
- `template <typename T> constexpr T power(T base, unsigned long long n, T one)`: `base` to the power `n` by fast exponentiation, where `one` is the value to start from (1 for numbers, the identity matrix `{1, 0, 0, 1}` for `Mat2`). It must never multiply more than it needs: at compile time, an overflow in a step you did not need is still a compile error.
- `constexpr long long fib(int n)` for `n` from 0 to 92, with `fib(0) = 0` and `fib(1) = 1`. The matrix `{1, 1, 1, 0}` to the power `k` is `{F(k+1), F(k), F(k), F(k-1)}`, so use `power` on it; note that F(93) does not fit in a `long long`, so `fib(92)` must not compute the 92nd power.

`power(3LL, 13, 1LL)` is 1594323, and `fib(92)` is 7540113804746346429. `power` is also called with `n` as large as 10¹⁵.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

struct Mat2 {
    long long a, b, c, d;
};

template <typename T>
T power(T base, unsigned long long n, T one) {
    T result = one;
    for (unsigned long long i = 0; i < n; ++i) result = result * base;
    return result;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>

struct Mat2 {
    long long a, b, c, d;
};

constexpr Mat2 operator*(const Mat2& x, const Mat2& y) {
    return {x.a * y.a + x.b * y.c, x.a * y.b + x.b * y.d, x.c * y.a + x.d * y.c, x.c * y.b + x.d * y.d};
}

constexpr bool operator==(const Mat2& x, const Mat2& y) {
    return x.a == y.a && x.b == y.b && x.c == y.c && x.d == y.d;
}

template <typename T>
constexpr T power(T base, unsigned long long n, T one) {
    T result = one;
    while (n > 0) {
        if (n % 2 == 1) result = result * base;
        n /= 2;
        if (n > 0) base = base * base;
    }
    return result;
}

constexpr long long fib(int n) {
    if (n == 0) return 0;
    return power(Mat2{1, 1, 1, 0}, static_cast<unsigned long long>(n - 1), Mat2{1, 0, 0, 1}).a;
}
--- check test | Matrix product and equality at compile time
[] { constexpr Mat2 m = Mat2{1, 2, 3, 4} * Mat2{5, 6, 7, 8}; static_assert(m == Mat2{19, 22, 43, 50}); static_assert(!(Mat2{1, 0, 0, 1} == Mat2{1, 0, 0, 2})); return true; }()
--- check test | power on numbers
[] { static_assert(power(3LL, 13, 1LL) == 1594323LL); static_assert(power(2LL, 62, 1LL) == 4611686018427387904LL); static_assert(power(5LL, 0, 1LL) == 1); return power(-2LL, 5, 1LL) == -32; }()
--- check test | fib at compile time, up to 92
[] { static_assert(fib(0) == 0 && fib(1) == 1 && fib(2) == 1 && fib(10) == 55); static_assert(fib(90) == 2880067194370816120LL); static_assert(fib(92) == 7540113804746346429LL); return true; }()
--- check test | A huge exponent, so it must be fast
power(3ULL, 1000000000000000ULL, 1ULL) == 10183499812279812097ULL

+++ problem | An editor with undo and redo
--- task
Build a tiny text editor where every change is a command object that can undo itself. No `main`.

- `Command` is an interface: pure virtual `void apply(std::string& doc)` and `void undo(std::string& doc)`, and a virtual destructor.
- `Append(std::string text)`: `apply` adds `text` at the end of `doc`; `undo` removes it again.
- `Erase(std::size_t n)`: `apply` removes the last `n` characters of `doc` (all of them, if there are fewer than `n`), remembering what it removed; `undo` puts those characters back.
- `class Editor`, holding the document (starting empty) and two histories of `std::unique_ptr<Command>`:
  - `void run(std::unique_ptr<Command> cmd)` applies `cmd` to the document, keeps it so it can be undone, and forgets everything that could have been redone;
  - `bool undo()` undoes the most recent command still applied and returns `true`, or returns `false` if there is none;
  - `bool redo()` applies again the most recently undone command and returns `true`, or returns `false` if there is none;
  - `const std::string& text() const`.

Running `Append("hello")`, `Append(" world")` and `Erase(3)` gives `"hello wo"`; `undo()` gives `"hello world"`, and `undo()` again gives `"hello"`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Command {
public:
    virtual ~Command() = default;
    virtual void apply(std::string& doc) = 0;
    virtual void undo(std::string& doc) = 0;
};

class Editor {
public:
    void run(std::unique_ptr<Command> cmd) { cmd->apply(doc_); }
    bool undo() { return false; }
    bool redo() { return false; }
    const std::string& text() const { return doc_; }
private:
    std::string doc_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Command {
public:
    virtual ~Command() = default;
    virtual void apply(std::string& doc) = 0;
    virtual void undo(std::string& doc) = 0;
};

class Append : public Command {
public:
    explicit Append(std::string text) : text_(std::move(text)) {}
    void apply(std::string& doc) override { doc += text_; }
    void undo(std::string& doc) override { doc = doc.substr(0, doc.size() - text_.size()); }
private:
    std::string text_;
};

class Erase : public Command {
public:
    explicit Erase(std::size_t n) : n_(n) {}
    void apply(std::string& doc) override {
        std::size_t k = n_ < doc.size() ? n_ : doc.size();
        removed_ = doc.substr(doc.size() - k);
        doc = doc.substr(0, doc.size() - k);
    }
    void undo(std::string& doc) override { doc += removed_; }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    std::string removed_;
};

class Editor {
public:
    void run(std::unique_ptr<Command> cmd) {
        cmd->apply(doc_);
        done_.push_back(std::move(cmd));
        undone_.clear();
    }

    bool undo() {
        if (done_.empty()) return false;
        std::unique_ptr<Command> cmd = std::move(done_.back());
        done_.pop_back();
        cmd->undo(doc_);
        undone_.push_back(std::move(cmd));
        return true;
    }

    bool redo() {
        if (undone_.empty()) return false;
        std::unique_ptr<Command> cmd = std::move(undone_.back());
        undone_.pop_back();
        cmd->apply(doc_);
        done_.push_back(std::move(cmd));
        return true;
    }

    const std::string& text() const { return doc_; }

private:
    std::string doc_;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Command>> done_;
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Command>> undone_;
};
--- check test | The example from the task
[] { Editor e; e.run(std::make_unique<Append>("hello")); e.run(std::make_unique<Append>(" world")); e.run(std::make_unique<Erase>(3)); bool a = e.text() == "hello wo"; bool u1 = e.undo() && e.text() == "hello world"; bool u2 = e.undo() && e.text() == "hello"; return a && u1 && u2; }()
--- check test | Redo, and a new command forgets the redo history
[] { Editor e; e.run(std::make_unique<Append>("ab")); e.run(std::make_unique<Append>("cd")); e.undo(); bool r = e.redo() && e.text() == "abcd"; e.undo(); e.run(std::make_unique<Append>("X")); return r && e.text() == "abX" && !e.redo(); }()
--- check test | Erasing more than there is, and undoing it
[] { Editor e; e.run(std::make_unique<Append>("hi")); e.run(std::make_unique<Erase>(10)); bool empty = e.text() == ""; e.undo(); return empty && e.text() == "hi"; }()
--- check test | Nothing to undo or redo
[] { Editor e; bool a = !e.undo() && !e.redo(); e.run(std::make_unique<Append>("x")); e.undo(); return a && !e.undo() && e.text() == "" && e.redo() && e.text() == "x" && !e.redo(); }()

+++ problem | A small stack machine
--- task
A tiny machine runs a program of instructions on a stack of `long long` values. The starter declares the instructions as structs and `using Instr = std::variant<Push, Add, Mul, Dup, Swap, Pop>;`, plus `struct VmError { std::size_t at; std::string message; };`. Write `std::variant<long long, VmError> run(const std::vector<Instr>& program)`. No `main`.

- `Push{v}` pushes `v`. `Add` and `Mul` pop the top value `b`, then the next `a`, and push `a + b` or `a * b`. `Dup` pushes a copy of the top value. `Swap` swaps the top two values. `Pop` throws the top value away.
- If an instruction needs more values than the stack holds, stop and return `VmError{i, "stack underflow"}`, where `i` is that instruction's index, counting from 0.
- When the program ends, the stack must hold exactly one value, which is returned. Otherwise return `VmError{program.size(), "expected 1 value, found N"}`, with N the number of values left.

`{Push{2}, Push{3}, Add{}, Push{4}, Mul{}}` returns 20.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <variant>
#include <vector>

struct Push { long long value; };
struct Add {};
struct Mul {};
struct Dup {};
struct Swap {};
struct Pop {};

using Instr = std::variant<Push, Add, Mul, Dup, Swap, Pop>;

struct VmError {
    std::size_t at;
    std::string message;
};

std::variant<long long, VmError> run(const std::vector<Instr>& program) {
    return VmError{0, "not written yet"};
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <string>
#include <variant>
#include <vector>

struct Push { long long value; };
struct Add {};
struct Mul {};
struct Dup {};
struct Swap {};
struct Pop {};

using Instr = std::variant<Push, Add, Mul, Dup, Swap, Pop>;

struct VmError {
    std::size_t at;
    std::string message;
};

template <class... Fs> struct overloaded : Fs... { using Fs::operator()...; };

std::variant<long long, VmError> run(const std::vector<Instr>& program) {
    std::vector<long long> stack;
    auto take = [&stack] {
        long long top = stack.back();
        stack.pop_back();
        return top;
    };
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < program.size(); ++i) {
        bool ok = std::visit(overloaded{
            [&](const Push& p) { stack.push_back(p.value); return true; },
            [&](const Add&) {
                if (stack.size() < 2) return false;
                long long b = take(), a = take();
                stack.push_back(a + b);
                return true;
            },
            [&](const Mul&) {
                if (stack.size() < 2) return false;
                long long b = take(), a = take();
                stack.push_back(a * b);
                return true;
            },
            [&](const Dup&) {
                if (stack.empty()) return false;
                stack.push_back(stack.back());
                return true;
            },
            [&](const Swap&) {
                if (stack.size() < 2) return false;
                long long b = take(), a = take();
                stack.push_back(b);
                stack.push_back(a);
                return true;
            },
            [&](const Pop&) {
                if (stack.empty()) return false;
                stack.pop_back();
                return true;
            },
        }, program[i]);
        if (!ok) return VmError{i, "stack underflow"};
    }
    if (stack.size() != 1) return VmError{program.size(), "expected 1 value, found " + std::to_string(stack.size())};
    return stack.back();
}
--- check test | Arithmetic
[] { auto r = run({Push{2}, Push{3}, Add{}, Push{4}, Mul{}}); auto* v = std::get_if<long long>(&r); return v && *v == 20; }()
--- check test | Dup, Swap and Pop
[] { auto r = run({Push{5}, Dup{}, Mul{}, Push{1}, Push{100}, Swap{}, Pop{}, Add{}}); auto* v = std::get_if<long long>(&r); return v && *v == 125; }()
--- check test | Underflow names the instruction
[] { auto a = run({Push{1}, Add{}}); auto b = run({Pop{}}); auto c = run({Push{1}, Push{2}, Swap{}, Pop{}, Pop{}, Dup{}}); auto* ea = std::get_if<VmError>(&a); auto* eb = std::get_if<VmError>(&b); auto* ec = std::get_if<VmError>(&c); return ea && ea->at == 1 && ea->message == "stack underflow" && eb && eb->at == 0 && ec && ec->at == 5; }()
--- check test | Too many or too few values at the end

+++ problem | A message bus with priorities
--- task
Write `class Bus`, which delivers messages to handlers. A handler is a `std::function<void(const std::string&)>`, called with the message. No `main`.

- `int subscribe(const std::string& topic, int priority, std::function<void(const std::string&)> handler)` registers `handler` and returns its id: 1 for the first subscription, then 2, 3, …. The topic `"*"` means every topic.
- `bool unsubscribe(int id)` removes that subscription and returns `true`, or returns `false` if there is none.
- `int publish(const std::string& topic, const std::string& message)` calls every handler subscribed to `topic` **or** to `"*"`, highest priority first; handlers with equal priority are called in the order they subscribed. It returns how many were called. `topic` is never `"*"` itself.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Bus {
public:
    int subscribe(const std::string& topic, int priority, std::function<void(const std::string&)> handler) { return 0; }
    bool unsubscribe(int id) { return false; }
    int publish(const std::string& topic, const std::string& message) { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Bus {
public:
    int subscribe(const std::string& topic, int priority, std::function<void(const std::string&)> handler) {
        int id = next_id_++;
        subs_.push_back({id, topic, priority, std::move(handler)});
        return id;
    }

    bool unsubscribe(int id) {
        for (auto it = subs_.begin(); it != subs_.end(); ++it) {
            if (it->id == id) {
                subs_.erase(it);
                return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }

    int publish(const std::string& topic, const std::string& message) {
        std::vector<const Sub*> hits;
        for (const Sub& s : subs_) {
            if (s.topic == topic || s.topic == "*") hits.push_back(&s);
        }
        std::stable_sort(hits.begin(), hits.end(), [](const Sub* a, const Sub* b) { return a->priority > b->priority; });
        for (const Sub* s : hits) s->handler(message);
        return static_cast<int>(hits.size());
    }

private:
    struct Sub {
        int id;
        std::string topic;
        int priority;
        std::function<void(const std::string&)> handler;
    };
    std::vector<Sub> subs_;
    int next_id_ = 1;
};
--- check test | Highest priority first, ties in subscription order
[] { Bus bus; std::vector<std::string> log; bus.subscribe("alarm", 1, [&log](const std::string& m) { log.push_back("low:" + m); }); bus.subscribe("alarm", 5, [&log](const std::string& m) { log.push_back("high:" + m); }); bus.subscribe("alarm", 1, [&log](const std::string& m) { log.push_back("low2:" + m); }); int n = bus.publish("alarm", "fire"); return n == 3 && log == std::vector<std::string>{"high:fire", "low:fire", "low2:fire"}; }()
--- check test | "*" hears every topic, in priority order with the rest
[] { Bus bus; std::vector<std::string> log; bus.subscribe("*", 3, [&log](const std::string& m) { log.push_back("all:" + m); }); bus.subscribe("temp", 9, [&log](const std::string& m) { log.push_back("temp:" + m); }); bus.subscribe("temp", 0, [&log](const std::string& m) { log.push_back("t0:" + m); }); int a = bus.publish("temp", "21"); int b = bus.publish("door", "open"); return a == 3 && b == 1 && log == std::vector<std::string>{"temp:21", "all:21", "t0:21", "all:open"}; }()
--- check test | Ids and unsubscribing
[] { Bus bus; int hits = 0; int a = bus.subscribe("x", 0, [&hits](const std::string&) { hits += 1; }); int b = bus.subscribe("*", 0, [&hits](const std::string&) { hits += 10; }); bool gone = bus.unsubscribe(b); int n = bus.publish("x", ""); return a == 1 && b == 2 && gone && n == 1 && hits == 1 && !bus.unsubscribe(b) && !bus.unsubscribe(7); }()
--- check test | Nobody listening
[] { Bus bus; bus.subscribe("a", 1, [](const std::string&) {}); return bus.publish("b", "m") == 0; }()

+++ problem | The smallest battery for a route
--- task
A rover drives a route of legs, in order. It starts with a full battery and may recharge at most `stops` times, only between legs; a battery of capacity C can drive at most C kilometres between charges. Write `std::variant<long long, std::string> min_battery(const std::string& route, int stops)`, which returns the smallest capacity that completes the route, or an error message. No `main`.

`route` lists the leg lengths in kilometres, separated by single commas, like `"4,2,7,1,5"`. Check in this order:

- `stops` below 0 gives `"negative stops"`.
- An empty `route` gives `"empty route"`.
- A leg that is not 1 to 9 digits, or is 0, gives `"bad leg N"`, where N counts legs from 1: `"4,,2"` gives `"bad leg 2"`.

`min_battery("4,2,7,1,5", 1)` is 13, with 0 stops it is 19, and with 10 stops it is 7. Routes can have 200,000 legs, and the answer can be far larger than an `int`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cctype>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <variant>
#include <vector>

std::variant<long long, std::string> min_battery(const std::string& route, int stops) {
    return 0LL;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cctype>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <variant>
#include <vector>

std::variant<std::vector<long long>, std::string> read_legs(const std::string& route) {
    std::vector<long long> legs;
    std::istringstream in(route);
    std::string part;
    int n = 0;
    while (std::getline(in, part, ',')) {
        ++n;
        bool ok = !part.empty() && part.size() <= 9;
        long long v = 0;
        for (char c : part) {
            if (!std::isdigit(static_cast<unsigned char>(c))) ok = false;
            else v = v * 10 + (c - '0');
        }
        if (!ok || v == 0) return "bad leg " + std::to_string(n);
        legs.push_back(v);
    }
    if (!route.empty() && route.back() == ',') return "bad leg " + std::to_string(n + 1);
    return legs;
}

bool enough(const std::vector<long long>& legs, int stops, long long cap) {
    int used = 0;
    long long left = cap;
    for (long long leg : legs) {
        if (leg > left) {
            ++used;
            left = cap;
        }
        left -= leg;
    }
    return used <= stops;
}

std::variant<long long, std::string> min_battery(const std::string& route, int stops) {
    if (stops < 0) return std::string("negative stops");
    if (route.empty()) return std::string("empty route");
    auto read = read_legs(route);
    if (auto* err = std::get_if<std::string>(&read)) return *err;
    const std::vector<long long>& legs = *std::get_if<std::vector<long long>>(&read);
    long long lo = *std::max_element(legs.begin(), legs.end());
    long long hi = 0;
    for (long long leg : legs) hi += leg;
    while (lo < hi) {
        long long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (enough(legs, stops, mid)) hi = mid;
        else lo = mid + 1;
    }
    return lo;
}
--- check test | The example with 1, 0 and 10 stops
[] { auto a = min_battery("4,2,7,1,5", 1); auto b = min_battery("4,2,7,1,5", 0); auto c = min_battery("4,2,7,1,5", 10); auto* x = std::get_if<long long>(&a); auto* y = std::get_if<long long>(&b); auto* z = std::get_if<long long>(&c); return x && *x == 13 && y && *y == 19 && z && *z == 7; }()
--- check test | Errors, in order
[] { auto a = min_battery("", -1); auto b = min_battery("", 2); auto c = min_battery("4,,2", 1); auto d = min_battery("0,3", 1); auto e = min_battery("3,1234567890", 1); auto f = min_battery("5,x", 0); auto g = min_battery("5,", 0); return std::get<std::string>(a) == "negative stops" && std::get<std::string>(b) == "empty route" && std::get<std::string>(c) == "bad leg 2" && std::get<std::string>(d) == "bad leg 1" && std::get<std::string>(e) == "bad leg 2" && std::get<std::string>(f) == "bad leg 2" && std::get<std::string>(g) == "bad leg 2"; }()
--- check test | One leg
[] { auto r = min_battery("999999999", 3); auto* v = std::get_if<long long>(&r); return v && *v == 999999999; }()
--- check test | 200,000 long legs
[] { std::string route; for (int i = 0; i < 200000; ++i) { if (i) route += ","; route += "999999999"; } auto a = min_battery(route, 1); auto b = min_battery(route, 199999); auto c = min_battery(route, 3); return std::get<long long>(a) == 99999999900000LL && std::get<long long>(b) == 999999999LL && std::get<long long>(c) == 49999999950000LL; }()

+++ problem | A ring buffer the compiler can run
--- task
Write the class template `Ring<T, N>`, which keeps the last `N` items pushed, in a fixed `std::array<T, N>` with no heap memory. Every member function must be `constexpr`, so a `Ring` can be used inside compile-time code. No `main`.

- `static_assert` that `N` is greater than 0.
- `void push(const T& x)` adds `x` as the newest item. When the ring is full, the oldest item is overwritten.
- `std::optional<T> pop()` removes and returns the **oldest** item, or `std::nullopt` when empty.
- `const T* peek() const` points at the oldest item, or is `nullptr` when empty.
- `std::size_t size() const` and `bool full() const`.

After pushing 1 to 5 into a `Ring<int, 3>`, it holds 3, 4 and 5; `pop()` gives 3.
--- starter
#include <array>
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class Ring {
public:
    void push(const T& x) {}
    std::optional<T> pop() { return std::nullopt; }
    const T* peek() const { return nullptr; }
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    bool full() const { return false; }
};
--- solution
#include <array>
#include <cstddef>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

template <typename T, std::size_t N>
class Ring {
    static_assert(N > 0, "Ring needs room for at least one item");
public:
    constexpr void push(const T& x) {
        data_[(head_ + size_) % N] = x;
        if (size_ < N) ++size_;
        else head_ = (head_ + 1) % N;
    }

    constexpr std::optional<T> pop() {
        if (size_ == 0) return std::nullopt;
        T oldest = data_[head_];
        head_ = (head_ + 1) % N;
        --size_;
        return oldest;
    }

    constexpr const T* peek() const { return size_ == 0 ? nullptr : &data_[head_]; }
    constexpr std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    constexpr bool full() const { return size_ == N; }

private:
    std::array<T, N> data_{};
    std::size_t head_ = 0;
    std::size_t size_ = 0;
};
--- check test | Overwriting the oldest, at compile time
[] { constexpr int code = [] { Ring<int, 3> r; for (int i = 1; i <= 5; ++i) r.push(i); int first = *r.pop(); return first * 100 + static_cast<int>(r.size()) * 10 + *r.peek(); }(); static_assert(code == 324); return true; }()
--- check test | Oldest first, then empty
[] { Ring<std::string, 2> r; r.push("a"); r.push("b"); bool full = r.full(); auto x = r.pop(); auto y = r.pop(); return full && x == "a" && y == "b" && r.pop() == std::nullopt && r.peek() == nullptr && r.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | A ring of one
[] { Ring<int, 1> r; r.push(7); r.push(8); bool one = r.size() == 1 && *r.peek() == 8; r.pop(); r.push(9); return one && *r.peek() == 9 && r.full(); }()
--- check test | Wrapping round many times
[] { Ring<int, 4> r; long long total = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 1000; ++i) { r.push(i); if (i % 3 == 0) total += *r.pop(); } std::size_t left = r.size(); while (auto x = r.pop()) total += *x; return left == 3 && total == 168829; }()

+++ problem | A leaderboard of best scores
--- task
Write `class Leaderboard`. Each player's **best** score counts. Players with equal best scores are ranked by who reached that score first. No `main`.

- `void submit(const std::string& name, int score)` records a score. If it beats the player's best (or the player is new), it becomes their best, reached now. A score equal to or below their best changes nothing.
- `std::optional<int> best(const std::string& name) const` is the player's best score, or `std::nullopt` for an unknown player.
- `std::vector<std::string> top(std::size_t k) const` returns up to `k` names: highest best score first, and for equal best scores, whoever reached theirs earlier first.
- `int rank(const std::string& name) const` is the player's position in that full order, counting from 1, or 0 for an unknown player.

After `submit("ada", 50)`, `submit("bo", 70)`, `submit("cy", 50)`, `submit("ada", 50)`: `top(3)` is `{"bo", "ada", "cy"}`: `ada` reached 50 before `cy`, and her second 50 changes nothing.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <tuple>
#include <vector>

class Leaderboard {
public:
    void submit(const std::string& name, int score) {}
    std::optional<int> best(const std::string& name) const { return std::nullopt; }
    std::vector<std::string> top(std::size_t k) const { return {}; }
    int rank(const std::string& name) const { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <tuple>
#include <vector>

class Leaderboard {
public:
    void submit(const std::string& name, int score) {
        ++clock_;
        auto it = players_.find(name);
        if (it == players_.end() || score > it->second.score) players_[name] = Entry{name, score, clock_};
    }

    std::optional<int> best(const std::string& name) const {
        auto it = players_.find(name);
        if (it == players_.end()) return std::nullopt;
        return it->second.score;
    }

    std::vector<std::string> top(std::size_t k) const {
        std::vector<std::string> out;
        for (const Entry& e : ranked()) {
            if (out.size() == k) break;
            out.push_back(e.name);
        }
        return out;
    }

    int rank(const std::string& name) const {
        std::vector<Entry> all = ranked();
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < all.size(); ++i) {
            if (all[i].name == name) return static_cast<int>(i) + 1;
        }
        return 0;
    }

private:
    struct Entry {
        std::string name;
        int score;
        long long when;
    };

    std::vector<Entry> ranked() const {
        std::vector<Entry> all;
        for (const auto& [name, e] : players_) all.push_back(e);
        std::sort(all.begin(), all.end(), [](const Entry& a, const Entry& b) {
            return std::tie(b.score, a.when) < std::tie(a.score, b.when);
        });
        return all;
    }

    std::map<std::string, Entry> players_;
    long long clock_ = 0;
};
--- check case | The example
[] { Leaderboard b; b.submit("ada", 50); b.submit("bo", 70); b.submit("cy", 50); b.submit("ada", 50); return b.top(3); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"bo", "ada", "cy"}
--- check test | A new best moves a player, and resets their time
[] { Leaderboard b; b.submit("ada", 50); b.submit("cy", 60); b.submit("ada", 40); bool low_ignored = b.best("ada") == 50; b.submit("ada", 60); return low_ignored && b.top(2) == std::vector<std::string>{"cy", "ada"} && b.rank("ada") == 2 && b.rank("cy") == 1; }()
--- check test | Unknown players, and k past the end
[] { Leaderboard b; b.submit("x", -5); return b.best("nobody") == std::nullopt && b.rank("nobody") == 0 && b.top(10) == std::vector<std::string>{"x"} && b.best("x") == -5 && b.top(0).empty(); }()
--- check test | Many players
[] { Leaderboard b; for (int i = 0; i < 1000; ++i) b.submit("p" + std::to_string(i), i % 10); auto t = b.top(3); return t == std::vector<std::string>{"p9", "p19", "p29"} && b.rank("p0") == 901 && b.rank("p999") == 100; }()

+++ problem | Sprites that share their textures
--- task
A game scene has named sprites, and each sprite shows a texture loaded from a file. Sprites showing the same file share **one** texture. Write `class Scene`, using `std::shared_ptr<Texture>` for the starter's `struct Texture { std::string file; };`. The scene keeps a cache of loaded textures by file name, and which texture each sprite shows. No `main`.

- `void add_sprite(const std::string& sprite, const std::string& file)` makes the sprite show `file`'s texture, loading it only if it is not in the cache yet. If the sprite already exists, it switches to the new file.
- `bool remove_sprite(const std::string& sprite)` removes the sprite and returns `true`, or returns `false` if there is no such sprite.
- `std::shared_ptr<const Texture> texture_of(const std::string& sprite) const` is the sprite's texture, or `nullptr` for an unknown sprite.
- `long users(const std::string& file) const` is how many sprites show that file now (0 if it is not in the cache).
- `int loaded() const` is how many textures are in the cache. Textures stay cached when no sprite uses them, until:
- `int purge()` removes from the cache every texture that no sprite uses, and returns how many it removed.
- `std::vector<std::string> report() const` has one line per cached texture, `"<file>: <users>"`, most users first, and equal users by file A→Z.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <tuple>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Texture {
    std::string file;
};

class Scene {
public:
    void add_sprite(const std::string& sprite, const std::string& file) {}
    bool remove_sprite(const std::string& sprite) { return false; }
    std::shared_ptr<const Texture> texture_of(const std::string& sprite) const { return nullptr; }
    long users(const std::string& file) const { return 0; }
    int loaded() const { return 0; }
    int purge() { return 0; }
    std::vector<std::string> report() const { return {}; }
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <tuple>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Texture {
    std::string file;
};

class Scene {
public:
    void add_sprite(const std::string& sprite, const std::string& file) {
        if (cache_.count(file) == 0) cache_[file] = std::make_shared<Texture>(Texture{file});
        sprites_[sprite] = cache_[file];
    }

    bool remove_sprite(const std::string& sprite) { return sprites_.erase(sprite) > 0; }

    std::shared_ptr<const Texture> texture_of(const std::string& sprite) const {
        auto it = sprites_.find(sprite);
        if (it == sprites_.end()) return nullptr;
        return it->second;
    }

    long users(const std::string& file) const {
        auto it = cache_.find(file);
        if (it == cache_.end()) return 0;
        long n = 0;
        for (const auto& [name, tex] : sprites_) {
            if (tex == it->second) ++n;
        }
        return n;
    }

    int loaded() const { return static_cast<int>(cache_.size()); }

    int purge() {
        std::vector<std::string> unused;
        for (const auto& [file, tex] : cache_) {
            if (users(file) == 0) unused.push_back(file);
        }
        for (const std::string& file : unused) cache_.erase(file);
        return static_cast<int>(unused.size());
    }

    std::vector<std::string> report() const {
        std::vector<std::pair<long, std::string>> rows;
        for (const auto& [file, tex] : cache_) rows.push_back({users(file), file});
        std::sort(rows.begin(), rows.end(), [](const std::pair<long, std::string>& a, const std::pair<long, std::string>& b) {
            return std::tie(b.first, a.second) < std::tie(a.first, b.second);
        });
        std::vector<std::string> lines;
        for (const auto& [n, file] : rows) lines.push_back(file + ": " + std::to_string(n));
        return lines;
    }

private:
    std::map<std::string, std::shared_ptr<Texture>> cache_;
    std::map<std::string, std::shared_ptr<Texture>> sprites_;
};
--- check test | Sprites showing one file share one texture
[] { Scene s; s.add_sprite("tree1", "grass.png"); s.add_sprite("tree2", "grass.png"); s.add_sprite("hero", "hero.png"); return s.texture_of("tree1") == s.texture_of("tree2") && s.texture_of("tree1")->file == "grass.png" && s.loaded() == 2 && s.users("grass.png") == 2 && s.users("hero.png") == 1; }()
--- check test | Switching and removing sprites
[] { Scene s; s.add_sprite("a", "x.png"); s.add_sprite("b", "x.png"); s.add_sprite("a", "y.png"); bool gone = s.remove_sprite("b"); return gone && !s.remove_sprite("b") && s.users("x.png") == 0 && s.users("y.png") == 1 && s.loaded() == 2 && s.texture_of("b") == nullptr && s.users("z.png") == 0; }()
--- check test | purge drops only unused textures
[] { Scene s; s.add_sprite("a", "x.png"); s.add_sprite("b", "y.png"); s.add_sprite("c", "z.png"); s.remove_sprite("a"); s.remove_sprite("c"); int n = s.purge(); int again = s.purge(); return n == 2 && again == 0 && s.loaded() == 1 && s.users("y.png") == 1; }()
--- check test | A purged texture is loaded fresh next time
[] { Scene s; s.add_sprite("a", "x.png"); std::shared_ptr<const Texture> old = s.texture_of("a"); s.remove_sprite("a"); s.purge(); s.add_sprite("b", "x.png"); return s.texture_of("b") != old && s.users("x.png") == 1 && old->file == "x.png"; }()
--- check case | The report
[] { Scene s; s.add_sprite("t1", "grass.png"); s.add_sprite("t2", "grass.png"); s.add_sprite("h", "hero.png"); s.add_sprite("r", "rock.png"); s.add_sprite("r", "hero.png"); return s.report(); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"grass.png: 2", "hero.png: 2", "rock.png: 0"}

+++ question | The order of destructors
--- ask
What does this program print? Type it on one line, exactly as printed.

```cpp
#include <iostream>

struct Tag {
    char c;
    explicit Tag(char ch) : c(ch) { std::cout << "+" << c << " "; }
    ~Tag() { std::cout << "-" << c << " "; }
};

int main() {
    Tag a('a');
    {
        Tag b('b');
        Tag c('c');
    }
    Tag d('d');
}
```
--- answer
+a +b +c -c -b +d -d -a
--- why
Destructors run at the closing brace of the block that made the object, in reverse order of construction. `b` and `c` die at the inner `}`, `c` first because it was made last. `d` is made after that block, and at the end of `main` the remaining objects die newest first: `d`, then `a`.

+++ question | The copy nobody wrote
--- ask
`Samples` has a raw pointer member `int* data_`, a constructor that allocates with `new int[n]`, and a destructor that calls `delete[] data_`. It declares no copy constructor. What goes wrong with `Samples b = a;`?
--- choice correct
Both objects hold the same pointer, so a change through one shows in the other, and both destructors delete the same block: a double free.
--- choice
The copy gets a new block of its own, but its numbers are left uninitialised.
--- choice
It does not compile: a class with a destructor must write its own copy constructor before it can be copied.
--- choice
The copy leaks its block, but nothing else goes wrong.
--- why
The compiler writes a copy constructor that copies each member, and copying a pointer copies only the address: a shallow copy. That is the rule of three: a class that needs its own destructor almost always needs its own copy constructor and copy assignment too, or better, a member such as `std::vector` that already copies correctly.

+++ question | A move that is not noexcept
--- ask
`Frame` has a correct move constructor, but it is not marked `noexcept`. What happens when a `std::vector<Frame>` has to grow?
--- choice correct
It compiles and gives the right result, but every time the vector grows it copies every element instead of moving it.
--- choice
It does not compile: `std::vector` requires a `noexcept` move constructor.
--- choice
The vector moves the elements anyway; `noexcept` only changes the compiler's warnings.
--- choice
The program stops with an error the first time the vector grows.
--- why
To keep its elements safe if something fails halfway through growing, `std::vector` only moves elements whose move constructor promises never to fail. Without `noexcept` it falls back to copying: no error, no warning, just slower code.

+++ question | What std::move really does
--- ask
In `Buffer b = std::move(a);`, which part actually takes `a`'s memory?
--- choice correct
`Buffer`'s move constructor. `std::move` only casts `a` to an rvalue reference, which lets the compiler choose that constructor.
--- choice
`std::move` itself: it copies `a`'s pointer into `b` and sets `a`'s to `nullptr`.
--- choice
The compiler: whenever `std::move` appears, the memory moves, even if the class has only a copy constructor.
--- choice
`std::move` frees `a`'s memory so that `b` can allocate a fresh block.
--- why
`std::move` is a cast that says "you may treat this as expendable". The stealing is done by whatever move constructor or move assignment the class has; a class with only copy functions is simply copied.

+++ question | The loop over owners
--- ask
Which line is wrong, and why?

```cpp
std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Node>> kids = make_kids();   // line 1
int total = 0;                                            // line 2
for (auto k : kids) {                                     // line 3
    total += k->value;                                    // line 4
}
```
--- choice correct
Line 3: `auto k` copies each `unique_ptr`, and copying a `unique_ptr` is deleted, so it does not compile. Write `const auto& k`.
--- choice
Line 4: `->` does not work on a `unique_ptr`; write `(*k).value`.
--- choice
Line 1: a vector cannot hold `unique_ptr`s, because they cannot be copied.
--- choice
Line 3: the loop moves every pointer out of `kids`, leaving them all null afterwards.
--- why
A `unique_ptr` has exactly one owner, so its copy constructor is deleted. A range-for without `&` copies each element into the loop variable, which is not allowed here. A reference only looks at each pointer. A vector of `unique_ptr` is fine: it moves them when it grows.

+++ question | Counting shared owners
--- ask
What does this print? Type it exactly, on one line.

```cpp
auto a = std::make_shared<int>(7);
auto b = a;
{
    auto c = b;
    std::cout << a.use_count() << " ";
}
b = nullptr;
std::cout << a.use_count();
```
--- answer
3 1
--- why
`a`, `b` and `c` all share the one `int`, so inside the block the count is 3. `c` dies at the closing brace (2), and setting `b` to `nullptr` lets go of another (1). The `int` is deleted only when the count reaches 0.

+++ question | Two answers for one blank
--- ask
What happens here?

```cpp
template <typename T>
T biggest(const T& a, const T& b) { return a < b ? b : a; }

auto x = biggest(3, 4.5);
```
--- choice correct
It does not compile: `T` is deduced as `int` from `3` and as `double` from `4.5`, and deduction never converts arguments to make them agree.
--- choice
`x` is 4.5: the compiler turns 3 into 3.0 and picks `T = double`.
--- choice
`x` is 4: `T` comes from the first argument, and 4.5 is cut down to 4.
--- choice
It compiles, but calling it is undefined behavior.
--- why
Every parameter that uses `T` must deduce the same type exactly. The fixes are to name the type (`biggest<double>(3, 4.5)`), to give the parameters two template parameters, or to stop deduction on one of them with `std::type_identity_t<T>`.

+++ question | Deleting through the base
--- ask
`Shape` has virtual functions but a destructor that is **not** virtual. A `std::unique_ptr<Shape>` owns a `Circle`, and `Circle` has a `std::vector` member. What is wrong when the `unique_ptr` deletes it?
--- choice correct
It deletes through a `Shape*`, so without a virtual destructor the behavior is undefined; in practice `Circle`'s destructor, and its vector's, usually never run.
--- choice
Nothing: a `unique_ptr` always remembers the real type it was made with and calls the right destructor.
--- choice
It does not compile: a `unique_ptr<Shape>` can only own a `Circle` if the destructor is virtual.
--- choice
Only `Shape`'s own members leak; the vector frees itself.
--- why
Deleting a derived object through a base pointer only finds the derived destructor if the base destructor is virtual. That is why an interface meant to be used through pointers gets `virtual ~Shape() = default;`.

+++ question | Three ways to pass a dog
--- ask
What does this program print? Type it on one line.

```cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

struct Animal {
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
    virtual std::string sound() const { return "..."; }
};
struct Dog : Animal {
    std::string sound() const override { return "woof"; }
};

void speak(Animal a) { std::cout << a.sound() << " "; }
void shout(const Animal& a) { std::cout << a.sound() << " "; }

int main() {
    Dog d;
    speak(d);
    shout(d);
    Animal copy = d;
    std::cout << copy.sound();
}
```
--- answer
... woof ...
--- why
`speak` takes an `Animal` by value and `copy` is an `Animal` variable, so both hold a sliced copy: only the `Animal` part of the dog, which answers `"..."`. `shout` takes a reference to the real `Dog`, so the virtual call reaches `Dog::sound`.

+++ question | constexpr and consteval with a run-time value
--- ask
What happens with these two lines?

```cpp
constexpr int sq(int x) { return x * x; }
consteval int cube(int x) { return x * x * x; }

int main() {
    int n;
    std::cin >> n;
    int a = sq(n);     // line A
    int b = cube(n);   // line B
}
```
--- choice correct
Line A is fine and runs at run time; line B is a compile error, because a `consteval` function must run at compile time and `n` is only known at run time.
--- choice
Both lines are compile errors: neither function can take a value read at run time.
--- choice
Both lines compile, and both functions simply run at run time because `n` is not a constant.
--- choice
Line A is a compile error and line B is fine.
--- why
`constexpr` means "may run at compile time": with a run-time argument it runs like any function. `consteval` means "must run at compile time", so every call needs arguments the compiler already knows.

+++ question | Highest priority first, the wrong way
--- ask
Why is this comparator wrong?

```cpp
std::sort(tasks.begin(), tasks.end(), [](const Task& a, const Task& b) {
    return a.priority >= b.priority;   // highest priority first
});
```
--- choice correct
`comp(x, x)` is true, so it is not a strict weak ordering. With equal priorities `std::sort` has undefined behavior: a wrong order, or even reading outside the vector. Use `>`.
--- choice
It sorts lowest priority first: `>=` reverses the order.
--- choice
It is correct, only slower, because equal elements get swapped back and forth.
--- choice
A lambda given to `std::sort` must take its parameters by value.
--- why
A comparator must behave like `<`: "does `a` come strictly before `b`?". With `>=`, two equal tasks each claim to come before the other, which breaks the rules `std::sort` relies on. Break ties with another key instead.

+++ question | The cost of searching for the answer
--- ask
The shipping problem checks one capacity in a single pass over the `n` packages, and binary-searches capacities from the heaviest package up to the total weight `W`. What does the whole search cost?
--- choice
O(n)
--- choice correct
O(n log W)
--- choice
O(n · W)
--- choice
O(log n)
--- why
Binary search halves the range of capacities each time, so it makes about log₂ W checks, and each check walks all `n` packages. Trying every capacity in turn would be O(n · W), which is what makes brute force hopeless for big weights.
